  - _sources:_ https://opensource.contentauthenticity.org/docs/c2pa-python/, https://github.com/contentauth/c2pa-python, https://sightengine.com/python-c2pa-tutorial-verifying-images-detecting-tampering
- **ICAO 9303 MRZ check-digit validation + MRZ↔VIZ↔2D-Doc cross-zone consistency + specimen blocklist** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Glaring gap: the candidate list validates IBAN/SIREN/VAT/NIR checksums but never the MRZ check digits on ID documents, despite identity.py being an audited module. Implement the ICAO 9303 weighted 7-3-1 mod-10 scheme over document number, DOB, expiry, and the composite digit, for TD3 (passport), TD1/TD2 (CNI formats including the pre-2021 French 2x36 layout), and visas — the 'mrz' PyPI package or ~40 lines of code. Then cross-compare the three independent representations on the same document: MRZ fields vs OCR'd visual zone (name/DOB printed on the card) vs decoded 2D-Doc payload, and blocklist known specimen data (issuer UTO/'UTOPIA', ANTS specimen strings).
  - _signal:_ The classic change-one-digit DOB/expiry fraud fails check digits deterministically unless the fraudster recomputes them (most don't); MRZ-vs-VIZ or MRZ-vs-2D-Doc disagreement is a near-certain forgery indicator since a genuine document is internally consistent by construction. Very strong, near-zero FP once gated on OCR confidence (OCR misreads are the only FP source — require the check digit to fail under multiple OCR hypotheses before flagging).
  - _local:_ Pure Python: pip 'mrz' or PassportEye (which bundles MRZ detection + checksum scoring via tesseract). No new system deps beyond tesseract already in the stack. Milliseconds of compute.
  - _sources:_ https://pypi.org/project/mrz/, https://passporteye.readthedocs.io/en/latest/mrz_algorithm.html, https://idcheck.dev/icao-9303-check-digits/, ICAO Doc 9303 Part 3 (Specifications Common to all MRTDs)
- **Capture-channel authentication: real scan vs fake-scan filter vs print-and-rescan laundering** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ Two symmetric attacks are unaddressed: (a) 'fake scan' tools (Lookscanned-style filters, OnlyFake's 'photographed on a tabletop' mode) make a digitally forged document look like a physical capture; (b) print-and-rescan launders a tampered file, destroying all PDF/JPEG history the candidate list depends on. Detection: FFT analysis of the page for halftone dot peaks and moiré (genuine print reproduction), scanner noise spectrum and paper-texture statistics, illumination gradient shape, and coherence between claimed capture (EXIF scanner/camera model, DPI) and observed physics. Synthetic scan filters betray themselves with spatially UNIFORM added noise, perfectly constant skew angle, and noise that doesn't modulate with ink coverage.
  - _signal:_ For (a): document presents as scan but lacks physical capture traces → the 'scanned' look is cosmetic, high suspicion. For (b): confirming a genuine print-scan pass tells the aggregator that ALL digital-history modules are blind, so their clean scores must not count as positive evidence — a scoring-critical signal, not just a flag. Medium strength; FP risk on high-quality flatbed scans of laser-printed originals, so use as confidence modifier more than penalty.
  - _local:_ opencv-python + numpy FFT, Pillow for EXIF. No models required for the heuristic tier; optional tiny sklearn classifier trained on scan/fake-scan/digital corpora. Fast, low RAM.
  - _sources:_ https://arxiv.org/pdf/2102.06792 (VIPPrint: printed/scanned dataset for synthetic image detection and source linking), https://www.researchgate.net/publication/220962324_Document_Frauds_Identification_and_Linking_Fake_Document_to_Scanners_and_Printers, https://resistant.ai/blog/onlyfake-generative-ai-fraud
- **Face-region forensics for ID documents (portrait-swap and morphing-attack detection)** (effort: large)
  - _how:_ No listed technique looks at the portrait — the highest-value zone of an ID. Detect the face region locally (OpenCV DNN or dlib), then (1) run the existing splice detectors (ELA, noise-variance, resampling p-map, JPEG-grid alignment) specifically on the portrait rectangle and its border seam, where a pasted photo leaves discontinuities; (2) check geometric plausibility (portrait position/aspect vs the document template); (3) optionally run a single-image morphing-attack-detection (S-MAD) model exported to ONNX for passport-style photos.
  - _signal:_ Portrait replacement is the canonical physical/digital ID forgery; border-seam and localized-forensics signals are medium-strength with manageable FP if scoped to the portrait rect. S-MAD must be sold honestly: single-image morph detection generalizes poorly across morphing tools (documented in the MAD literature), so treat any morph score as weak corroborating evidence, never standalone.
  - _local:_ Face detection: opencv-python's DNN module with bundled Caffe/ONNX face detector (no GPU, ~10MB). Region-scoped forensics reuse Module H code. S-MAD ONNX model ~20-100MB on onnxruntime CPU. All offline; fine for a small box if S-MAD stays optional.
  - _sources:_ https://arxiv.org/pdf/2011.02045 (Face Morphing Attack Generation & Detection: A Comprehensive Survey), https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.09817 (Generalized Single-Image-Based MAD Using ViT), https://www.christoph-busch.de/projects-mad.html, https://decrypt.co/216188/ai-generated-fake-id-bypass-kyc-aml-banks-crypto-onlyfakes
- **Page-level provenance consistency (inserted/substituted page detection)** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ A common multi-page fraud is splicing one forged page into a genuine document (or assembling a PDF from pages of different origins) — every listed check operates file-wide or object-wide, none compares pages against each other. Fingerprint each page: font set and subset tags, content-stream operator style (the 'coding-style' features already planned, computed per page), embedded-image compression parameters/DPI/quantization tables, MediaBox/CropBox/rotation, and annotation/resource dictionary dialect. Flag pages that are statistical outliers versus their siblings.
  - _signal:_ A page produced by a different generator than the rest of the document (LibreOffice page inside an SAP-generated invoice; a rescanned page 2 inside a digital original) is strong evidence of substitution. FP risk: legitimately merged documents (cover letter + invoice), so severity medium and message framed as 'page N has different origin than the rest'.
  - _local:_ Pure PyMuPDF/pikepdf metadata walking plus numpy for outlier scoring — no new dependencies, negligible compute. Reuses the per-producer structural fingerprint features already on the roadmap, just bucketed per page.
  - _sources:_ Builds on producer coding-style fingerprinting literature already cited in the candidate list (PDF producer identification via structural dialect), https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1007/978-3-031-41682-8_28 (ICDAR 2023, document forgery via composition)
- **Known-BAD template fingerprint database (fake receipt/invoice generator detection)** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ The list has a known-GOOD producer/template DB but no negative counterpart. Curate outputs of public fake-receipt/fake-invoice generator sites and template kits (expensify-style receipt makers, 'free invoice generator' sites abused for fraud, known OnlyFake template layouts): store perceptual hashes (pHash/dHash) of their layouts, their characteristic Producer strings (wkhtmltopdf builds, specific headless-Chrome versions with default margins), default fonts, and fixed layout geometry (line positions, logo boxes). Match uploads against this DB offline.
  - _signal:_ Exact or near template match to a known fraud-generator output is high-severity, very low FP (legitimate businesses don't invoice from receipt-maker templates). Coverage is inherently reactive — it only catches known generators — but these tools produce enormous volumes of low-effort fraud, so hit rate per curation hour is high.
  - _local:_ pip imagehash + Pillow; DB is a small JSON/SQLite shipped with the app (no document leaves the box). The cost is curation, not compute.
  - _sources:_ https://www.aiaaic.org/aiaaic-repository/ai-algorithmic-and-automation-incidents/chatgpt-4o-generation-of-realistic-fake-receipts-raises-scam-concerns, https://www.techspot.com/news/111504-us-arrests-onlyfake-operator-accused-selling-over-10000.html, https://resistant.ai/blog/onlyfake-generative-ai-fraud (documents template-reuse as the actual OnlyFake mechanism)
- **Illumination/shadow-gradient consistency for photographed documents** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ The listed white-point/background-flatness detector assumes a flat digital background; on phone photos of documents (the dominant capture mode) the paper has a smooth illumination gradient and perspective shading. Estimate the low-frequency luminance field (large-kernel morphological closing or polynomial surface fit on background pixels), then flag rectangular regions whose local background deviates from the fitted global gradient — a patch pasted from a differently-lit source, or a digitally flat (255,255,255) box composited onto a photographed page.
  - _signal:_ Catches patch-paste on photos where white-point and noise checks fail (paper isn't uniformly white on a photo). Medium strength; FPs from genuine shadows/folds, so require rectangularity + alignment with text regions before flagging, and cap at medium severity.
  - _local:_ Pure opencv-python + numpy (morphology, polyfit). Milliseconds per page, no deps, no RAM concerns.
  - _sources:_ Classic illumination-inconsistency splicing literature (Kee & Farid, 'Exposing digital forgeries from 3-D lighting environments'); background-surface-fit techniques standard in document binarization (Sauvola preprocessing)
- **Container-anomaly signals: repaired xref, encryption, and file-type masquerade as first-class flags** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ The extraction layer silently discards three cheap high-value signals the audit confirmed are unread: fitz's doc.is_repaired (MuPDF auto-fixed a broken xref — frequent after crude binary edits), doc.is_encrypted/needs_pass (analysis-evasion via encryption), and doc.is_pdf (an image/XPS renamed to .pdf — the screenshot-fraud disguise). Surface each as an explicit flag with its own severity, plus a qpdf --check pass whose warnings/errors feed the same channel, and magic-byte sniffing to name the true file type.
  - _signal:_ is_repaired after a binary edit is a strong tamper indicator otherwise invisible to every listed structural check (the repair erases the evidence those checks need). Encryption and masquerade are evasion signals: the correct output is 'could not analyze — treat as unverified', which the aggregation layer must score as suspicion, not neutral confidence-0.1 silence. Near-zero FP for masquerade; low FP for is_repaired (some legitimate but broken generators exist — medium severity).
  - _local:_ Trivial: attributes already returned by PyMuPDF; qpdf is a small apt package (already compatible with packages.txt deployment); python-magic for sniffing. Zero measurable cost.
  - _sources:_ https://pymupdf.readthedocs.io/en/latest/document.html (is_repaired, is_encrypted, is_pdf), https://qpdf.readthedocs.io/en/stable/cli.html#option-check
- **Annotation-overlay forensics (FreeText/Stamp edits, hidden-flag annotations, flattening residue)** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ One-click Acrobat/Preview edits add content as ANNOTATIONS, not content-stream text — invisible to the listed z-order, font, and mid-line checks (which read the page stream) and to get_text defaults. Enumerate annotations via PyMuPDF: flag FreeText/Square/Stamp/Redact annotations whose rects overlap existing text or regex-matched amount/date/IBAN regions; decode the /F bit flags to catch Hidden/NoView annotations (the standard way to conceal a white square over the real amount); inspect appearance streams for text; and render each page twice (with and without annotations) and pixel-diff to show exactly what the annotations change.
  - _signal:_ A FreeText annotation sitting on an amount region is close to a smoking gun for the laziest and most common consumer edit path. Hidden-flag annotations on an invoice have essentially no legitimate use — high severity, very low FP. The render-diff doubles as user-facing evidence ('this box was added on top').
  - _local:_ PyMuPDF only (page.annots(), annot.flags, get_pixmap with/without annots). A few lines per check, milliseconds per page.
  - _sources:_ PDF 2.0 spec ISO 32000-2 §12.5.3 (annotation flags: Hidden, NoView), https://pymupdf.readthedocs.io/en/latest/annot.html
- **French typographic and lexical plausibility check (fraudster-error detector)** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Fabricated French documents routinely violate conventions that real French billing software never gets wrong: decimal comma vs point, thin/non-breaking space before € and %, correct mandatory phrases ('TVA non applicable, art. 293 B du CGI', the exact late-payment penalty clause, 'indemnité forfaitaire de 40 €'), legal-form/capital coherence ('SARL au capital de…'), and plain spelling errors in boilerplate. Run hunspell-fr over extracted/OCR'd text, plus a rule table for typography and required-mention phrasing, and flag deviations concentrated in boilerplate zones.
  - _signal:_ Weak individually but cheap and orthogonal to everything listed; GPT-4o receipt fakes were specifically observed making comma/decimal and arithmetic errors, and human fraudsters mangle legal boilerplate they retype. Low severity only, best consumed by the log-odds aggregator as several tiny LR contributions; FP risk from OCR noise means it must only fire on text-layer (not OCR) input or high-confidence OCR.
  - _local:_ pip hunspell/pyspellchecker + French dictionary files shipped locally; rule table is a YAML of regexes. Milliseconds, no system deps beyond dictionary files.
  - _sources:_ https://techcrunch.com/2025/03/31/chatgpts-new-image-generator-is-really-good-at-faking-receipts/ (documents comma/decimal and arithmetic errors in AI fakes), Code général des impôts art. 242 nonies A (mandatory French invoice mentions)

---

### RESEARCH — Aggregating many weak forensic signals into one trust score while minimizing false positives (scoring/combination architecture for TrustyFile's scoring.py)

**Key insights:**
- The single most important change: replace the weighted AVERAGE with an ADDITIVE LOG-ODDS (log-likelihood-ratio) sum. Averaging is mathematically the wrong operation for evidence: one critical finding (score 5) averaged with nine clean modules (score 95) yields ~86 and the fraud vanishes. In log-odds space each check ADDS its weight of evidence, so a strong signal (large log-LR) is preserved regardless of how many clean checks surround it. This is the shared backbone of SpamAssassin, credit scorecards, biometric score fusion, and naive-Bayes spam filters.
- Separate HARD evidence from SOFT evidence architecturally. Cryptographic facts (a valid PDF signature verified offline with pyHanko, a checksum-valid IBAN/VAT) and a single critical tamper finding should VETO or CLAMP the score, never be averaged into it. Fraud engines universally combine a small set of hard-fail/veto rules with a cumulative soft-score, then map the soft score to green/yellow/red decision bands (APPROVE / REVIEW / SUSPICIOUS).
- The dominant false-positive driver in a naive-Bayes / product-of-LR scheme is CORRELATED evidence double-counting: metadata-producer, font-diversity, and structure modules often all fire off the same underlying fact ('re-saved in Photoshop'). Multiplying their LRs treats them as independent and produces overconfident false accusations. Mitigate by grouping correlated checks and capping each group's total contribution, or by learning weights on real labeled data (perceptron-style) rather than hand-guessing.
- Minimizing false positives is a CALIBRATION + ASYMMETRIC-COST problem, not just a threshold problem. SpamAssassin explicitly trains its rule weights to favor false negatives over false positives ~100:1 and targets ~1 FP per 2500 legit messages. TrustyFile should weight 'wrongly accusing a genuine document' far more heavily than 'missing a subtle forgery', tune the decision threshold on a labeled set, and validate with Brier score / ECE / reliability diagrams that a '70' really means ~70% genuine.
- Confidence and coverage must propagate into the final output. When modules conflict or many are low-confidence, the honest answer is 'uncertain' (mid-score wide band), NOT a confident verdict. Gary Robinson's chi-square spam method deliberately returns ~0.5 on conflicting evidence; TrustyFile should surface a score RANGE + verbal scale (ENFSI-style) + the per-check evidence ledger that drove the number, rather than a single false-precision integer.
- Track legitimacy as a first-class POSITIVE-evidence ledger, not merely 'absence of red flags'. Hard positive signals (valid signature chain, exact template match, single consistent producer, coherent date chain) produce LR<1 (evidence FOR genuine) and should actively raise trust. A two-sided log-odds ledger (evidence-for-tamper minus evidence-for-genuine) is the clean way to fuse detection and confirmation in one number.
- All of this is pure-Python and cheap: numpy + scipy.stats + scikit-learn (CalibratedClassifierCV, isotonic/Platt) + pyHanko for offline signature verification. No document ever leaves the box; combination/calibration math is microseconds; the only cost is building a small labeled corpus of genuine vs tampered documents to fit weights and calibration curves.

**Techniques:**
- **Additive log-odds / log-likelihood-ratio aggregation (replace the weighted average)** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ For each check, define a weight of evidence w_i = log( P(finding | tampered) / P(finding | genuine) ). Start from a prior log-odds (log of base-rate of tampering), then SUM the weights of every fired check: logit_post = logit_prior + sum(w_i). Convert once at the end with the logistic sigmoid p_tampered = 1/(1+exp(-logit_post)); trust_score = round(100*(1-p_tampered)). Positive w_i pushes toward tamper, negative (LR<1) toward genuine. This is exactly the credit-scorecard 'points to double the odds' scaling (Score = Offset - Factor*ln(odds)) and the naive-Bayes spam log-sum.
  - _signal:_ Catches the core failure mode: a single strong finding contributes a large fixed number of evidence-points that adding cannot dilute, unlike averaging where 9 clean modules drown 1 critical flag. FALSE-POSITIVE RISK: real, if weights are guessed too high or checks are correlated (double counting -> overconfident accusations). Controlled by fitting w_i from data and by grouping/capping correlated checks.
  - _local:_ Pure Python: math/numpy only, sub-millisecond. Weights can be hand-set initially (e.g. critical=+3.0 nats, high=+1.5, medium=+0.7, low=+0.2) then refined from a labeled corpus. pip: numpy. Negligible CPU/RAM.
  - _sources:_ https://wiki.datrics.ai/scorecard, https://www.linuxjournal.com/article/6467, https://arxiv.org/pdf/1704.08275, https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/jres/122/jres.122.027.pdf
- **Noisy-OR aggregation for the tamper side** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Model overall tamper probability as the probability that AT LEAST ONE independent cause fired: P(tampered) = 1 - (1-leak) * PRODUCT_over_fired_checks(1 - p_i), where p_i is the per-check probability that finding i alone indicates tampering, and 'leak' is a small baseline. Each clean/absent check contributes factor 1 (no dilution). One confident detector (p_i=0.9) drives the product's complement high regardless of how many modules stayed clean.
  - _signal:_ Excellent for 'any one strong tamper signal should dominate' semantics; naturally non-diluting and bounded in [0,1]. Proves nothing about legitimacy (one-sided). FALSE-POSITIVE RISK: moderate/high if many weakly-suspicious checks each have inflated p_i — their complements multiply down and stack into a high P even with no single strong signal; keep p_i honest and small for weak heuristics. Assumes independence between causes, so correlated checks inflate it.
  - _local:_ Pure Python one-liner over a list of p_i (numpy.prod). Microseconds, no deps beyond numpy. Ideal as the 'detection' half of a two-track score.
  - _sources:_ https://arxiv.org/pdf/1303.1479, https://arxiv.org/pdf/1711.08324, https://arxiv.org/pdf/1203.3470
- **Maximum / damped-max / top-k combination (anti-dilution, from outlier ensembles)** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Instead of averaging normalized per-module suspicion scores, take the MAXIMUM (Aggarwal/LOF ensemble convention: max avoids dilution from irrelevant models). Softer variants: 'damped averaging' applies sqrt or log to scores before averaging so a few components can't dominate but strong ones aren't washed out; 'pruned averaging / aggregate' discards low scores and sums the top-k, implicitly counting how many modules independently found something. Combine max-of-tamper-scores with the log-odds sum as a sanity gate.
  - _signal:_ Directly answers 'why does averaging hide the one bad module': max keeps the worst finding front-and-center. Aggarwal argues max is the right default when each component should give a comparable score. FALSE-POSITIVE RISK: pure max is noisy/over-sensitive (any single flaky module dominates) — hence damped or top-k aggregate as the robust middle ground; still needs per-module normalization first or the highest-scaled module always wins.
  - _local:_ Pure Python: max()/sorted()/numpy over normalized scores. Trivial CPU/RAM. pip: numpy.
  - _sources:_ https://www.charuaggarwal.net/ensembles.pdf, https://dl.acm.org/doi/pdf/10.1145/2481244.2481252
- **Hard-evidence vs soft-evidence separation with veto rules and decision bands** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ Split checks into (a) HARD facts — cryptographically or arithmetically certain: valid PDF signature chain, checksum-valid IBAN/VAT/SIREN, exact template/logo match; and (b) SOFT heuristics — everything probabilistic. Hard NEGATIVE facts veto/clamp the score to a floor (e.g. confirmed tampered content stream after signing -> cap at 15); hard POSITIVE facts raise a floor. Only soft evidence goes through the log-odds/noisy-OR combiner. Map the final number to explicit bands: green APPROVE, yellow REVIEW, red SUSPICIOUS.
  - _signal:_ Prevents the two worst outcomes: a mathematically-proven forgery being 'averaged back to safe', and a heuristic pile-up overriding cryptographic proof of authenticity. Mirrors how fraud engines pair hard-fail rules with cumulative soft scores + review bands. FALSE-POSITIVE RISK: very low for the hard track (facts), and the REVIEW band absorbs ambiguous cases instead of forcing a wrong verdict.
  - _local:_ Architectural, not compute: routing logic in scoring.py. Hard signature check uses pyHanko fully offline (pip: pyHanko, pyhanko-certvalidator; Python 3.10+, ~tens of MB RAM, <1s). IBAN/VAT/SIREN checksums are pure Python. No document leaves the box.
  - _sources:_ https://seon.io/resources/fraud-scores-how-to-calculate-them/, https://docs.pyhanko.eu/en/latest/lib-guide/validation.html, https://github.com/MatthiasValvekens/pyHanko
- **Fisher / Stouffer weighted p-value combination (Gary Robinson chi-square model)** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ Treat each check as a hypothesis test under the null 'document is genuine', producing a p-value p_i (probability of seeing this finding on a genuine doc). Combine independent p_i with Fisher's statistic -2*sum(ln p_i) ~ chi2(2k), or Stouffer's weighted-Z sum(w_i * Phi^-1(1-p_i)) / sqrt(sum w_i^2) which allows per-check reliability weights. Robinson's spam refinement computes TWO combined values — H (support for genuine) and S (support for tampered) — and reports I = S/(S+H).
  - _signal:_ Robinson's H/S/I gives a crucial FP-reducing property: when strong evidence points BOTH ways (conflicting checks), both H and S go small and I -> ~0.5 = 'uncertain / manual review', instead of a confident wrong verdict. Stouffer's weighting lets you down-weight low-precision checks. FALSE-POSITIVE RISK: low with the two-sided formulation; but the independence assumption inflates significance for correlated checks.
  - _local:_ scipy.stats.combine_pvalues implements Fisher/Stouffer directly (pip: scipy). Microseconds. Main work is defining a defensible p_i per check from your labeled genuine corpus.
  - _sources:_ https://www.linuxjournal.com/article/6467, https://docs.scipy.org/doc/scipy/reference/generated/scipy.stats.combine_pvalues.html, https://www.nature.com/articles/s41598-021-86465-y
- **Per-check weight learning with an asymmetric FP cost (SpamAssassin perceptron)** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ Rather than hand-guessing severity weights, learn each check's contribution from a labeled corpus of genuine + tampered documents. SpamAssassin trains a single-layer perceptron / logistic model via stochastic gradient descent (logsig activation) and maps learned weights to score space. Critically it injects an asymmetric cost ('ham preference'): legitimate examples are oversampled so the model favors false negatives over false positives (~100:1), targeting ~1 FP in 2500. Equivalent modern route: fit sklearn LogisticRegression on the per-module flag vector with class_weight favoring the 'genuine' class.
  - _signal:_ Turns 'why is this rule worth 15 points?' into a data-driven, precision-weighted answer, and bakes FP-minimization into the objective. Learned log-odds coefficients ARE the weights-of-evidence for technique #1. FALSE-POSITIVE RISK: this is the primary lever to REDUCE it; risk shifts to overfitting a small corpus (use regularization + cross-val).
  - _local:_ sklearn LogisticRegression / SGDClassifier, pure CPU, trains in <1s on thousands of docs (pip: scikit-learn, numpy). Needs a labeled dataset — the real cost. Inference is a dot product. Dataset stays local (org rule: never leak it).
  - _sources:_ https://svn.apache.org/repos/asf/spamassassin/trunk/masses/README.perceptron, https://cwiki.apache.org/confluence/display/SPAMASSASSIN/HowScoresAreAssigned
- **Score normalization before combining (rank / min-max / probability conversion)** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Heterogeneous module outputs (a font-diversity count, an ELA energy, a metadata boolean) live on incomparable scales; averaging or maxing them raw lets whichever module has the largest natural range dominate. Normalize first: rank-based, min-max to [0,1], z-score, or — best per Aggarwal — convert each module's raw score to a PROBABILITY via a fitted sigmoid or a Gaussian/exponential mixture (EM) so the numbers are genuinely comparable before combination.
  - _signal:_ Prevents silent domination of the aggregate by one high-variance module — a subtle, common cause of both false positives and false negatives. Rank-normalization is robust but loses magnitude info (Aggarwal's {1.71,1.71,1.70} vs {1.72,1.03,1.01} example); probability conversion preserves it. FALSE-POSITIVE RISK: itself low; it's a prerequisite that makes every other combiner behave.
  - _local:_ numpy for rank/min-max/z-score; sklearn for sigmoid/mixture fits. Microseconds. pip: numpy, scikit-learn.
  - _sources:_ https://www.charuaggarwal.net/ensembles.pdf, https://arxiv.org/pdf/1911.10418
- **Probability calibration (Platt scaling / isotonic regression) of the final score** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Raw combined scores are usually not true probabilities (over/under-confident). Fit a monotone map from raw score to empirical genuine-rate on a held-out calibration set: Platt scaling fits a logistic sigmoid (good for S-shaped distortion, works with little data); isotonic regression fits any monotone step function via pool-adjacent-violators (more flexible, ~22% better ECE, needs more data). scikit-learn's CalibratedClassifierCV wraps both (method='sigmoid'|'isotonic'). After calibration a displayed '70' means ~70% of docs scoring 70 are genuine.
  - _signal:_ Makes the trust score HONEST and thresholds meaningful — the foundation for choosing an FP-minimizing cutoff. Directly supports 'present uncertainty honestly'. FALSE-POSITIVE RISK: reduces it by exposing/correcting overconfidence; isotonic can overfit on tiny calibration sets (prefer Platt when data is scarce).
  - _local:_ sklearn CalibratedClassifierCV / IsotonicRegression / _sigmoid_calibration, pure CPU, trivial cost (pip: scikit-learn). Needs a labeled calibration split, kept local.
  - _sources:_ https://machinelearningmastery.com/calibrated-classification-model-in-scikit-learn/, https://fastml.com/classifier-calibration-with-platts-scaling-and-isotonic-regression/, https://arxiv.org/pdf/2601.19944
- **Correlated-evidence grouping / double-counting guard** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ Naive-Bayes/product-of-LR and noisy-OR both assume conditional independence; TrustyFile's modules violate it (producer string, font substitution, and structure-revision checks all reflect the same edit). Group checks that share a root cause into clusters; within a cluster, take the strongest signal or cap the cluster's total log-odds contribution instead of summing all members. Optionally learn pairwise correlations from the corpus and discount redundant features (feature-selection / tree-augmented naive Bayes).
  - _signal:_ Attacks the biggest FALSE-POSITIVE source in additive/multiplicative schemes: several correlated weak flags stacking into a confident-but-wrong accusation of a genuine doc that merely went through one converter. Independence violation makes the raw sum overconfident. FALSE-POSITIVE RISK: this technique specifically lowers it; cost is some lost sensitivity if grouping is too aggressive.
  - _local:_ Config-level grouping in scoring.py + optional correlation estimate via numpy/pandas on the labeled corpus. Negligible compute. pip: numpy, pandas.
  - _sources:_ https://nlp.stanford.edu/IR-book/html/htmledition/properties-of-naive-bayes-1.html, https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0305054821002070, https://medium.com/@vishalnayak/why-naive-bayes-algorithm-makes-assumptions-of-features-independence-cc98292b9e28
- **Two-sided legitimacy-confirmation ledger (positive evidence with LR<1)** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ Model trust as a running log-odds ledger with BOTH directions. Tamper evidence adds positive weight; legitimacy evidence adds negative weight (LR<1, i.e. more likely on genuine docs): a cryptographically valid signature chain, an exact known-template/logo SSIM match, a single self-consistent Producer across the whole file, a coherent CreationDate<=ModDate<=today chain, embedded ICC/XMP consistent with the claimed generator. Same additive machinery as #1, so detection and confirmation fuse into one number.
  - _signal:_ Directly serves goal (2) 'better confirm legitimate files' and combats false positives: strong positive proof can offset weak heuristic suspicion, so a clean signed invoice isn't dinged for using a common converter. Signature validity and exact template match are near-cryptographic-strength positive signals. FALSE-POSITIVE RISK: low, but avoid letting a forged/self-signed certificate count as positive — verify the trust chain, don't just check 'a signature exists'.
  - _local:_ pyHanko for offline signature+chain validation (pip: pyHanko, pyhanko-certvalidator); OpenCV/skimage SSIM for template/logo match (pip: opencv-python, scikit-image); rest is PyMuPDF metadata parsing. All local, <1-2s. pyHanko needs a local trust root store, no network.
  - _sources:_ https://docs.pyhanko.eu/en/latest/lib-guide/validation.html, https://wp.unil.ch/forensicdecision/files/2015/01/ChampodBiedermannVuilleWillisDeKinder_2016PDF.pdf, https://researchportal.hw.ac.uk/en/publications/likelihood-ratio-based-biometric-score-fusion
- **Honest uncertainty presentation: score bands, verbal scale, and evidence ledger in the UI** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ Instead of a single integer, show (a) a point score PLUS a confidence band whose width grows when modules are low-confidence, conflicting, or when coverage is thin (e.g. scanned image with no text layer); (b) an ENFSI-style verbal qualifier ('weak / moderate / strong support' mapped from log-LR magnitude); (c) an explainable per-check ledger listing which findings pushed the score up or down and by how much (SpamAssassin-style rule contribution list). Prefer frequency framing ('~5 in 100 documents like this are genuine') over bare probabilities for lay users.
  - _signal:_ Serves goal (3) minimize false-positive HARM: a wide band + REVIEW label communicates 'we're not sure' rather than a false-precise accusation, and the ledger lets a human sanity-check. Research shows range+point estimates improve decision quality and trust. FALSE-POSITIVE RISK: reduces perceived/actioned FPs by routing ambiguous docs to human review rather than auto-verdict.
  - _local:_ Pure Streamlit UI + the confidence numbers already produced by modules (ModuleResult.confidence). No extra compute. Band width can be derived from module confidences (e.g. variance-weighted) or from conformal-prediction intervals (pip: mapie) if a labeled set exists.
  - _sources:_ https://wp.unil.ch/forensicdecision/files/2015/01/ChampodBiedermannVuilleWillisDeKinder_2016PDF.pdf, https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/full/10.1080/13669877.2020.1801807, https://analystsuncertaintytoolkit.github.io/UncertaintyWeb/chapter_6.html, https://mapie.readthedocs.io/en/latest/index.html
- **Calibration monitoring: Brier score, ECE, reliability diagrams (Murphy decomposition)** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ On a held-out labeled set, bin documents by predicted trust and plot observed genuine-rate vs predicted (reliability diagram); points below the diagonal = overconfident. Summarize with Expected Calibration Error (mean |predicted-observed| across bins) and the Brier score (mean squared error of the probability), decomposable via Murphy into reliability + resolution + uncertainty. Good models: Brier 0.1-0.2, excellent <0.1. Re-run whenever weights change to catch regressions.
  - _signal:_ This is the measurement layer that PROVES the FP rate is under control and that weight/threshold changes actually help rather than just feel better. Resolution term rewards a score that actually separates genuine from tampered (not just a safe mid-score). FALSE-POSITIVE RISK: it's a diagnostic — its value is exposing over/under-confidence before it reaches users.
  - _local:_ sklearn.metrics.brier_score_loss + calibration_curve, or hand-rolled binning in numpy; matplotlib for the diagram (pip: scikit-learn, numpy, matplotlib). Runs offline in seconds on a labeled corpus.
  - _sources:_ https://www.statstest.com/calibration-checks-brier-score-reliability-diagrams, https://www.convexly.app/answers/how-to-measure-forecasting-calibration, https://arxiv.org/pdf/2605.03816
- **Dempster-Shafer fusion — considered and mostly rejected (Zadeh paradox)** (effort: large)
  - _how:_ D-S theory assigns belief mass to SETS of hypotheses including an explicit 'unknown' mass, then fuses sources with Dempster's rule of combination (orthogonal sum, normalized by conflict). Attractive because it can represent ignorance directly (a module that abstains contributes mass to 'unknown' rather than 50/50). However Zadeh's paradox shows that when two sources strongly conflict, the normalization produces counterintuitive certainty (e.g. backing a hypothesis both sources deemed nearly impossible).
  - _signal:_ Its explicit 'unknown' mass is philosophically nice for low-confidence forensic modules. But the classic combination rule is a documented FALSE-POSITIVE hazard under conflicting evidence — exactly TrustyFile's situation when detection and confirmation modules disagree. Recommendation: use log-odds/noisy-OR as primary; only consider D-S with a conflict-robust variant if explicit ignorance modeling proves necessary.
  - _local:_ Pure Python (e.g. pip: pyds / py_dempster_shafer) but the paradox handling requires modified combination rules that add complexity. Compute is cheap; conceptual risk is the cost.
  - _sources:_ https://arxiv.org/pdf/1304.2718, https://ojs.aaai.org/aimagazine/index.php/aimagazine/article/view/542, https://arxiv.org/pdf/1107.0018

---

### RESEARCH — Local algorithmic validation of business document content (checksums, cross-field consistency, arithmetic, dates, barcodes) for invoice/French-document fraud detection

**Key insights:**
- One dependency covers most identifier math: python-stdnum (pure Python, LGPL, offline) validates IBAN mod-97, SIREN/SIRET Luhn (incl. the La Poste mod-5 exception), all EU VAT checksums, French NIF/SPI (mod 511), NIR (mod 97), ISO 11649 RF references, and SEPA creditor IDs — adopt it as the backbone of a 'Module J: identifiers' and get a dozen checks nearly for free.
- Cross-FIELD consistency beats single-field validity: the French VAT key is a deterministic function of the SIREN ((12+3*(SIREN mod 97)) mod 97), the RIB key double-checks the IBAN, the 2D-Doc payload and EPC QR duplicate the printed text, and Factur-X XML duplicates the visible layer. A fraudster editing one surface almost never fixes its mathematically or cryptographically linked twin — these mismatch checks are your highest-signal, lowest-false-positive tamper detectors.
- Interpret checksums asymmetrically in scoring: a checksum PASS proves only well-formedness (weak legitimacy, fraudsters can paste a fully valid IBAN they control), but a checksum FAIL on a text-layer-extracted identifier detects ~99%+ of single-character edits — score FAIL high-severity, PASS as a small trust bonus, and downgrade severity when the value came from OCR (misreads like O/0 are the dominant false-positive source).
- Benford's law is statistically inapplicable to a single invoice (needs hundreds of values across orders of magnitude; <500 values are non-conforming by nature) — replace it with Nigrini's small-sample digit tests (round-number, threshold-proximity, duplication) capped at low severity, and reserve Benford/last-two-digits for future batch-upload mode.
- Arithmetic checks need engineered tolerance, not equality: EU law permits both per-line and per-total VAT rounding (ECJ C-302/07; divergence up to (n-1)*€0.01), so validate the EN 16931 BR-CO identities (line sum, rate×base, subtotal+VAT=total) with tolerance ~max(0.01×n_lines, 0.02) using decimal.Decimal — within tolerance is itself a positive trust signal, and a discrepancy equal to one digit's place value is a strong edited-digit signature.
- The two strongest local LEGITIMACY proofs for French documents are cryptographic/structural, not statistical: (1) ANTS 2D-Doc ECDSA signature verification (betagouv/2ddoc-parser, offline via bundled TSL; needs pylibdmtx + apt libdmtx0b) on tax notices/payslips/attestations, and (2) Factur-X embedded XML that passes EN 16931 schematron AND matches the visible text (akretion/factur-x 5.1+). Both also flip into near-zero-FP critical tamper flags when the signed/embedded data contradicts the printed layer.
- Cheap date logic catches human forgery mistakes: weekday-name vs calendar-date mismatch ('lundi 3 juin 2025' when it was a Tuesday) is deterministic and near-zero-FP; combine with date ordering (invoice ≤ due date, ≤60-day French statutory terms per L441-10, low severity since real-world violations are common) and born-digital-only cross-checks of content dates vs PDF CreationDate.
- Everything above fits the deployment envelope: all checksum/arithmetic/date checks are pure-Python microsecond operations; the only real costs are barcode scanning (~0.2-0.8 s/page for DataMatrix/QR at 300 dpi) and schematron validation (~0.1-0.5 s), keeping total added latency within a couple of seconds per document on a small cloud box with no document ever leaving the machine.

**Techniques:**
- **IBAN ISO 7064 mod-97 checksum + per-country BBAN format validation** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Move the first 4 chars (country code + 2 check digits) to the end, convert letters to numbers (A=10..Z=35), and the resulting big integer mod 97 must equal 1. Then validate the country-specific BBAN structure (length, digit/letter positions) against the ISO 13616 registry. Both python-stdnum (stdnum.iban) and schwifty implement this in pure Python; schwifty also validates country-specific bank/branch/account sub-formats.
  - _signal:_ Catches any single-digit edit or transposition in an IBAN (~99% detection for one changed character) — the classic 'invoice IBAN swap' fraud where a fraudster hand-edits digits usually breaks mod-97. As legitimacy: a valid IBAN is only a weak positive (fraudsters can paste their own fully valid IBAN), so a PASS should add little trust while a FAIL is a high-severity flag. False-positive risk is near zero if OCR is reliable; the main FP source is OCR misreads (O/0, I/1) on scanned documents — mitigate by only firing high severity when the IBAN came from the text layer, lower severity from OCR.
  - _local:_ Pure Python: pip install python-stdnum (LGPL, no deps) or schwifty. No system deps, microseconds per check, negligible RAM.
  - _sources:_ https://github.com/arthurdejong/python-stdnum/blob/master/stdnum/iban.py, https://arthurdejong.org/python-stdnum/doc/1.20/stdnum.iban, https://github.com/mdomke/schwifty
- **French RIB key — second, independent national checksum inside FR IBANs** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ A French BBAN is bank code (5) + branch code (5) + account number (11, letters converted via the official table A,J=1; B,K,S=2; ...) + 2-digit RIB key. Recompute: key = 97 − ((concatenated 21-digit number) mod 97), computed digit-by-digit as remainder = (remainder*10 + digit) mod 97 to avoid big ints. Validate that the recomputed key matches digits 21-22 of the BBAN.
  - _signal:_ Gives a second checksum independent of the IBAN's own mod-97, so a tampered French IBAN must satisfy two constraints simultaneously — an attacker who fixes the IBAN check digits but not the RIB key (or vice versa) is caught. Also lets you validate bare RIBs printed without the IBAN form (common on older French invoices). FP risk essentially zero on clean text-layer extraction; note python-stdnum's iban module only checks national checksums for a few countries, so implement this ~20-line function yourself.
  - _local:_ Pure Python, ~20 lines, no dependencies, microseconds. Trivially testable.
  - _sources:_ https://fr.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cl%C3%A9_RIB, https://www.dcode.fr/bban-check
- **IBAN-to-bank identification and BIC/IBAN consistency via schwifty's offline registry** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ schwifty ships a manually curated, offline bank registry (France included) mapping domestic bank codes to bank names and BICs. From a French IBAN, extract the 5-digit bank code and look up the bank name locally; if the invoice also prints a BIC, verify the BIC's bank code and country agree with the IBAN. Compare the resolved bank name against any bank name printed on the invoice/RIB block.
  - _signal:_ Catches the common fraud where the IBAN is replaced but the printed bank name/BIC is left unchanged (e.g., invoice says 'Crédit Agricole' but the IBAN resolves to a fintech or foreign bank), and IBAN country ≠ supplier country is a known red flag. As legitimacy: IBAN resolving to a real registered French bank consistent with the printed bank name is a decent positive signal. Moderate FP risk: registry can lag bank mergers, neobanks/payment institutions are legitimate but look 'unusual' — flag as medium, never critical, and phrase as 'verify with sender'.
  - _local:_ pip install schwifty (pure Python, registry data bundled, updated via CalVer releases). No network, no system deps, sub-millisecond lookups.
  - _sources:_ https://github.com/mdomke/schwifty, https://schwifty.readthedocs.io/en/latest/examples.html
- **SIREN/SIRET Luhn validation (with La Poste exception)** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ SIREN (9 digits) and SIRET (14 digits = SIREN + 5-digit NIC) must both pass the Luhn mod-10 checksum. Exception: SIRETs of La Poste (SIREN 356000000) validate by sum-of-digits mod 5 == 0 instead. Also cross-check internal consistency: the first 9 digits of any SIRET on the document must equal the SIREN printed elsewhere (header, footer, legal mentions). python-stdnum implements all of this including the La Poste special case (stdnum.fr.siren / stdnum.fr.siret).
  - _signal:_ A single edited digit in a SIRET breaks Luhn with 100% certainty for single-digit substitutions — strong tamper signal on fabricated or hand-edited legal identifiers, which appear on virtually every French invoice, payslip and attestation. Legitimacy side: valid format+checksum proves only well-formedness, not existence (existence needs the INSEE API — your optional external module, which only sends the number, never the file). FP risk near zero on text-layer extraction; OCR confusions are the only realistic FP source.
  - _local:_ pip install python-stdnum, pure Python, microseconds. Extraction: regex for \b\d{3}\s?\d{3}\s?\d{3}(\s?\d{5})?\b near keywords SIREN/SIRET/RCS.
  - _sources:_ https://github.com/arthurdejong/python-stdnum, https://lookuptax.com/docs/how-to-verify/siren-verification-france
- **French VAT-number key ↔ SIREN cross-consistency check** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ A French intra-community VAT number is FR + 2-digit key + SIREN, where key = (12 + 3 × (SIREN mod 97)) mod 97. So on any French invoice you can (a) verify the VAT number's key is arithmetically correct for its embedded SIREN, and (b) verify the SIREN embedded in the VAT number equals the SIREN/SIRET printed elsewhere on the document. Example: SIREN 775670417 → key 61 → FR61775670417.
  - _signal:_ This is one of the strongest pure-arithmetic tamper detectors for French invoices: a fraudster who edits the SIRET but not the VAT number (or vice versa) breaks the mathematical link between two fields that must agree. Both fields individually valid but mutually inconsistent = high-severity flag with near-zero false-positive rate (the relation is deterministic). Matching fields are also a genuine legitimacy-confirmation signal (internal coherence).
  - _local:_ Pure Python, ~10 lines (or stdnum.fr.tva for the key check). No deps, microseconds.
  - _sources:_ https://www.randomsiret.fr/en/tva-calculator, https://www.vatdb.com/guides/validate-fr-vat-number/, https://arthurdejong.org/python-stdnum/doc/
- **EU-wide VAT number format + per-country checksum validation (offline)** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Every EU member state's VAT number has a country-specific structure and most have a checksum: DE mod-11, IT Luhn, BE mod-97, ES letter-check, NL mod-11 (old style) etc. stdnum.eu.vat dispatches on the 2-letter prefix to the right country module and validates entirely offline (it separately offers a VIES online check — do NOT use that path given your locality constraint, or treat it like your INSEE module: identifier-only, optional).
  - _signal:_ Catches fabricated or digit-edited VAT numbers on any EU supplier invoice, not just French ones — useful because your users will upload Amazon/PayPal/foreign-supplier invoices. Signal strength like other checksums: FAIL is strong (medium-high severity), PASS is weak-positive. FP risk: very low; a few countries have weak or no checksums (format-only), so report confidence per country.
  - _local:_ pip install python-stdnum; the offline validate() path makes no network calls. Microseconds per check.
  - _sources:_ https://pypi.org/project/python-stdnum/, https://github.com/arthurdejong/python-stdnum
- **French personal fiscal number (SPI/NIF) mod-511 check + NIR mod-97 (tax notices, payslips)** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ The 13-digit numéro fiscal (SPI) on French tax notices: C1 must be 0-3; the last 3 digits (C11-C13) equal (first 10 digits) mod 511, zero-padded (official example: 3023217600 mod 511 = 53 → number ends 053). The 15-digit NIR (social security number, on payslips/attestations): last 2 digits = 97 − (first 13 digits mod 97), with Corsica letter handling (2A→19, 2B→18 substitutions). Both implemented in python-stdnum (stdnum.fr.nif, stdnum.fr.nir); the SPI rule is published by the DGFiP itself.
  - _signal:_ Directly targets your French-document scope: an edited or invented numéro fiscal on a tampered avis d'impôt, or a fabricated NIR on a fake payslip, fails these checksums. High-severity flag on failure, near-zero FP (deterministic algorithms from official sources). Passing is a mild legitimacy signal (fabricators frequently don't know the mod-511 rule).
  - _local:_ pip install python-stdnum, pure Python, microseconds. Extraction: 13-digit runs near 'numéro fiscal'/'N° fiscal', 15-digit near 'sécurité sociale'.
  - _sources:_ https://www.impots.gouv.fr/sites/default/files/media/1_metier/3_partenaire/tiers_declarants/spi/algorithme_spi.pdf, https://arthurdejong.org/python-stdnum/doc/1.20/stdnum.fr.nif
- **Invoice arithmetic consistency using EN 16931 BR-CO calculation rules with rounding tolerance** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ Adopt the European e-invoicing standard's calculation rules as your ground truth: sum of line net amounts = declared lines total (BR-CO-10); per VAT rate group, VAT amount = round(taxable base × rate, 2) (BR-CO-17); total with VAT = total without VAT + VAT total (BR-CO-15); grand total = total with VAT − prepaid (BR-CO-16). Because legitimate software rounds either per-line or per-invoice (both legal in the EU; the choice is national law per ECJ C-302/07), accept a tolerance: |Σ(rounded lines) − declared| ≤ max(0.01 × n_lines, 0.02) for line sums, and ±0.01-0.05 per VAT rate group for VAT amounts; anything beyond is a flag.
  - _signal:_ Catches the classic 'change one number in Paint/PDF editor' fraud: editing a line amount, the VAT, or the total breaks at least one identity (an attacker must consistently re-derive 3+ dependent figures). Deviation of exactly one 'round' step (e.g. total off by 100.00 or by the edited digit's place value) is an especially strong tamper signature. FP risk is the main design issue: per-line vs per-total rounding legitimately diverges by up to (n−1)×0.01, discounts/éco-participation/shipping lines get missed by extraction, and OCR of amounts is error-prone — so gate severity: within tolerance = pass (positive trust signal, +legitimacy since all arithmetic coheres), 0.05-1.00 off = medium, exact-digit-place discrepancies = high.
  - _local:_ Pure Python + your existing pdfplumber extraction; the hard part is robustly parsing amounts (French formats: '1 234,56 €', non-breaking spaces, thin spaces). Use decimal.Decimal, never float. No new deps; milliseconds. Amount-extraction regexes + rate detection (20%, 10%, 5.5%, 2.1% French rates) is where the effort goes.
  - _sources:_ https://github.com/ConnectingEurope/eInvoicing-EN16931, https://www.invoicenavigator.eu/blog/en16931-validation-rules-complete-guide, https://eur-lex.europa.eu/legal-content/EN/TXT/?uri=CELEX:62007CJ0302, https://vat-calculator.net/art/vat-rounding-eu-uk-europe
- **Factur-X embedded XML extraction, schematron validation, and XML-vs-visible-text cross-check** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ French/German invoices increasingly are Factur-X: a PDF/A-3 with an embedded EN 16931-compliant CII XML file (mandatory in France's e-invoicing reform). Extract the XML with the akretion/factur-x lib (get_xml_from_pdf), validate it locally against the official XSD and — since factur-x 5.1 (June 2026) — the official schematron rules (xml_check_schematron()). Then cross-compare the XML's seller name, SIRET, IBAN, totals, and date against what the visible PDF layer shows.
  - _signal:_ Extremely strong tamper detector for the growing share of Factur-X invoices: fraudsters who edit the visible text layer virtually never regenerate the embedded XML, so any XML↔text mismatch on amount/IBAN/date is near-proof of tampering (critical severity, ~zero FP). Conversely, a present, XSD+schematron-valid XML whose values match the visible text is one of the strongest local legitimacy confirmations available for invoices. Absence of XML is neutral (most PDFs today still lack it).
  - _local:_ pip install factur-x (BSD; depends on pypdf + lxml). Schematron validation runs locally via lxml XSLT; ~100-500 ms. The official CEN schematron artefacts are on GitHub (ConnectingEurope/eInvoicing-EN16931) if you want rule-level control. No system deps.
  - _sources:_ https://github.com/akretion/factur-x, https://pypi.org/project/factur-x/, https://github.com/ConnectingEurope/eInvoicing-EN16931
- **Targeted digit tests instead of Benford's law (round-number, threshold-proximity, duplication, last-two-digits)** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Benford's law needs hundreds of numbers spanning orders of magnitude — a single invoice with 5-30 amounts cannot conform or violate it meaningfully (datasets under ~500 values are non-conforming by nature), so do NOT score Benford per document. Instead run Nigrini's small-sample surrogates on the extracted line amounts: (1) round-number test — fraudulent amounts are disproportionately round (x000.00, x500.00); (2) threshold-proximity — amounts clustered just under approval thresholds (…4 900-4 999 under a 5 000 limit); (3) number-duplication — identical non-trivial amounts repeated across lines; (4) last-two-digits distribution only if the user batch-uploads many documents from one vendor.
  - _signal:_ Weak-to-moderate signals individually; per Journal of Accountancy, round numbers are 'a fingerprint of fraud', but plenty of legitimate invoices have round amounts (fixed fees, subscriptions, rents) — so cap these at low severity and require co-occurrence with other flags before they move the score. Their real value is as corroborating evidence and for batch mode. Explicitly excluding Benford avoids a whole class of false positives.
  - _local:_ Pure Python over already-extracted amounts; trivial CPU. No deps.
  - _sources:_ https://www.oreilly.com/library/view/forensic-analytics-methods/9781118087633/chapter08.html, https://www.journalofaccountancy.com/issues/2018/may/fraud-round-numbers/, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benford's_law, https://www.acfe.com/fraud-magazine/all-issues/issue/article?s=2024-novdec-revisiting-benfords-law
- **Weekday-name vs calendar-date consistency check** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ French documents often spell dates with the weekday: 'Fait à Paris, le lundi 3 juin 2025'. Parse the date, compute datetime(2025,6,3).weekday(), and compare against the written weekday name (French names: lundi..dimanche; handle abbreviations lun./mar. and English for foreign invoices). June 3, 2025 is a Tuesday, so 'lundi 3 juin 2025' is a fabrication artifact. Apply the same to any '(day-name) DD month YYYY' pattern found by regex over the extracted text.
  - _signal:_ Surprisingly effective tamper catch: when someone edits only the date digits of a template (attestation, contract, invoice), the weekday name silently becomes wrong — a mistake humans rarely notice but that is deterministic to verify. A mismatch is a high-severity flag with essentially zero false positives (only risk: OCR misreading the day name or digits; require exact token match from the text layer for high severity). Match = small legitimacy bonus. Same family: reject impossible dates (Feb 30) and 'future' document dates vs today.
  - _local:_ Pure Python stdlib (datetime + a 7-entry French/English name table); combine with your existing datefinder pipeline. Microseconds.
  - _sources:_ https://docs.python.org/3/library/datetime.html, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Determination_of_the_day_of_the_week
- **Invoice/due-date/service-period logic incl. French statutory payment-term bounds (Code de commerce L441-10)** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Enforce the partial order: service/delivery period start ≤ end ≤ invoice date (usually), invoice date ≤ due date, and due date − invoice date within legal French B2B maxima: 30 days default, 60 days net or '45 jours fin de mois' when contractually agreed, 45 days for periodic invoices (Article L441-10). Also cross-check the document's textual dates against PDF metadata: text invoice date far AFTER the file's CreationDate is impossible for a genuinely generated invoice; ModDate years after CreationDate corroborates editing (ties into your Module A).
  - _signal:_ Catches anachronisms typical of forged or altered invoices: due date before issue date, service period after invoice date, payment terms wildly exceeding legal limits (e.g., 180 days on a French B2B invoice), or a '2024' invoice inside a PDF created in 2026. Individually medium signals; the metadata-vs-content date cross is strong. FP risk: moderate — deposits/prepayment invoices legitimately precede service, credit notes invert logic, and scanned old invoices have recent CreationDates (only apply the metadata cross to born-digital PDFs, detectable via your structure module). Legal-limit violations are common in reality (late-payment culture) so keep them low severity.
  - _local:_ Pure Python over datefinder/regex output + fitz metadata you already extract. Microseconds.
  - _sources:_ https://www.legifrance.gouv.fr/codes/article_lc/LEGIARTI000038414392, https://entreprendre.service-public.gouv.fr/vosdroits/F23211?lang=en
- **Reference-number consistency: internal repetition, French sequencing rules, and ISO 11649 'RF' creditor-reference mod-97 checksum** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Three layers: (1) Internal consistency — the invoice number typically appears 2-4 times (header, footer, payment-reference line, filename); extract all occurrences of the INV/FA/F-YYYY-NNNN pattern and flag any disagreement, and check the year token inside the reference matches the invoice date year. (2) Plausibility per French law — Article 242 nonies A CGI requires numbering 'basée sur une séquence chronologique et continue', so a reference whose embedded year/month contradicts the date, or mixed formats within one document, is suspect. (3) Structured payment references — an RF creditor reference (ISO 11649, e.g. 'RF18 5390 0754 7034') carries an IBAN-style mod-97 checksum, validatable with stdnum.iso11649; Belgian '+++xxx/xxxx/xxxxx+++' OGM references carry a mod-97 check too. SEPA Creditor Identifiers (FR ICS on direct-debit mandates) have mod-97 check digits validated by stdnum.eu.at_02.
  - _signal:_ Mismatched copies of the same invoice number within one document is a high-severity, near-zero-FP tamper signal (an editor changed one instance and missed another — exactly how amount/reference edits fail). A checksum-valid RF reference or ICS is a mild legitimacy signal; an invalid one is medium severity. Year-in-reference vs date-year mismatch is medium (FP risk: fiscal-year numbering, migrated numbering schemes — keep below high).
  - _local:_ Pure Python + regex; stdnum.iso11649 and stdnum.eu.at_02 from python-stdnum for the checksums. Microseconds.
  - _sources:_ https://www.legifrance.gouv.fr/codes/article_lc/LEGIARTI000050811276, https://bofip.impots.gouv.fr/bofip/140-PGP.html/identifiant=BOI-TVA-DECLA-30-20-20-10-20131018, https://github.com/arthurdejong/python-stdnum
- **French 2D-Doc DataMatrix decoding + offline ECDSA signature verification (the strongest legitimacy proof available)** (effort: large)
  - _how:_ 2D-Doc is the ANTS-standardized signed DataMatrix on French tax notices, payslips, proofs of address, etc. Pipeline: render page via fitz get_pixmap → locate/decode DataMatrix with pylibdmtx → parse the C40-encoded payload (header: version, CA ID, cert ID, emission date, doc type 00/04/06/28…; then 2-char-tagged data fields like name, reference, income, amount) → verify the ECDSA signature (P-256/384/521) against the issuing certificate resolved from the ANTS Trusted Service List (TSL). betagouv/2ddoc-parser does the parse+verify fully offline with a bundled tsl_signed.xml (note: it targets Python 3.13 and installs from git, so on your 3.11 stack you may vendor/adapt its ~small codebase; it supports DC04 versions 2-4). Finally cross-check the signed payload fields (name, amounts, reference) against the document's visible text.
  - _signal:_ Cryptographic, government-grade: a valid ANTS signature over payload fields that match the visible text PROVES the document content is authentic — the single strongest positive-trust signal you can compute locally, worth a large trust bonus. Signature-valid but payload ≠ visible text = critical tamper flag (someone edited the human-readable layer of a genuine document) with ~zero FP. Failed signature or undecodable barcode on a document type that always carries one (avis d'impôt) = high suspicion. FP risks: low-resolution scans break DataMatrix decoding (render at 300 dpi, try multiple binarizations before concluding 'unreadable'), and the bundled TSL must be refreshed periodically as ANTS rotates certificates (ship it with app releases — no runtime network needed).
  - _local:_ pip install pylibdmtx + cryptography; apt libdmtx0b (or libdmtx0a on older Debian) — matches the libdmtx note already in your CLAUDE.md. Parsing/verification logic from betagouv/2ddoc-parser (vendorable). Cost: page render + DataMatrix scan ~200-800 ms/page, ECDSA verify <5 ms; fine for a small box.
  - _sources:_ https://github.com/betagouv/2ddoc-parser, https://ants.gouv.fr/Les-solutions/2D-Doc, https://pypi.org/project/pylibdmtx/
- **EPC QR code (EPC069-12 'SCT QR' / Girocode) payload validation and QR↔printed-text cross-check** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ European payment QR codes on invoices follow EPC069-12: an ≤331-byte payload of up to 11 lines — 'BCD', version 001/002, charset code, 'SCT', BIC (optional in v002), beneficiary name (≤70 chars), IBAN, 'EUR' amount (0.01-999999999.99), purpose, remittance reference (≤140, may be ISO 11649 RF), display text. Decode the QR from the rendered page (cv2.QRCodeDetector — no extra system dep — or pyzbar), validate every field's format, run the IBAN mod-97 and RF mod-97 checks on the embedded values, then cross-compare QR IBAN/amount/beneficiary against the printed invoice text.
  - _signal:_ The EPC spec itself notes these QRs are meant for paper invoices where the same payment data is also printed in plain text — that redundancy is your weapon: a fraudster who edits the printed IBAN or amount but not the QR (or overlays a new QR but not the text) produces a mismatch that is a critical, near-zero-FP tamper flag (this 'QR swap' is an actively reported invoice-fraud vector). A well-formed EPC payload whose data matches the text is a solid legitimacy signal. FP risk: QR decode failures on poor scans (treat unreadable as neutral); some invoices carry non-EPC QRs (URLs) — only apply after the 'BCD' service tag is confirmed.
  - _local:_ opencv-python (already in stack; QRCodeDetector needs no system dep) or pyzbar + apt libzbar0 (already planned for your Module C). Payload parsing is ~50 lines of pure Python straight from the EPC069-12 table; reuse IBAN/RF validators. ~50-200 ms/page for detection.
  - _sources:_ https://www.europeanpaymentscouncil.eu/sites/default/files/kb/file/2024-03/EPC069-12%20v3.1%20Quick%20Response%20Code%20-%20Guidelines%20to%20Enable%20the%20Data%20Capture%20for%20the%20Initiation%20of%20an%20SCT.pdf, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EPC_QR_code, https://segno.readthedocs.io/en/1.5.2/epc-qrcodes.html

---

### RESEARCH — Positive trust signals for document legitimacy, fully locally verifiable (PDF signatures, embedded structured invoice data, French government barcodes, payment QR codes, producer fingerprints, structural provenance)

**Key insights:**
- The single most powerful pattern is dual-representation cross-checking: one file often contains the same fact twice (visible text vs Factur-X XML, text vs EPC QR payload, text vs 2D-Doc signed fields, bytes vs signature ByteRange). Agreement is a strong, low-false-positive trust bonus; disagreement on an amount or IBAN is near-proof of tampering, because visual editors never update the machine-readable copy.
- Everything requested is achievable fully locally: pyHanko validates PAdES signatures and even EU qualified status offline via a filesystem-cached trusted-list (only the trust LIST refresh needs network — the document never leaves the box); 2ddoc-parser ships the ANTS TSL certificates inside the package; Factur-X XSD/Schematron artifacts are bundled in pip packages.
- Regulatory timing matters: France mandates structured e-invoices (Factur-X/UBL/CII) for reception by ALL companies from 1 Sept 2026 and emission by large/mid-size firms the same date — so 'valid embedded XML that matches the rendered text' is about to become the default legitimacy signal for French invoices, and its absence will gradually become informative.
- zxing-cpp (pip-only wheels, no libzbar0/libdmtx apt deps) decodes both QR codes AND DataMatrix — one dependency unblocks the project's currently-disabled pyzbar QR feature and enables 2D-Doc, with pylibdmtx (apt libdmtx0b) as a fallback for hard-to-detect DataMatrix.
- Producer metadata fingerprints are useful but spoofable; the arXiv 'coding style' work (2103.02702) shows structural invariants (trailer keys, xref style, object ordering) can verify or contradict the claimed Producer — turning a weak metadata signal into a robust spoofing detector.
- Positive signals must be asymmetric in scoring: presence-and-valid gives a bonus, but absence must stay neutral (most legitimate documents today carry no signature, XML, or 2D-Doc) — this is essential for the project's minimize-false-positives goal. Also remember structural pristineness only proves 'not modified after generation', never 'authentic origin': a fake invoice fabricated in Word is structurally clean.
- Cost profile fits a small cloud box: everything except veraPDF (Java, ~300-500MB RAM, 1-3s) and saxonche (~100MB native wheel) is pure-Python and sub-second; run signature/XML/QR/2D-Doc checks inline and make PDF/A + Schematron optional or async.
- Suggested scoring integration: cryptographic proofs (valid qualified signature, valid 2D-Doc matching text) should be able to lift the trust score decisively (+15-20 each), consistency proofs (XML/QR/text agreement, checksums) +5-15, while their mismatches map to critical flags — these map cleanly onto the existing Flag/ModuleResult dataclasses as new modules or extensions of Modules C and G.

**Techniques:**
- **PDF digital signature validation with pyHanko (ByteRange integrity + certificate chain)** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Open the PDF with pyhanko.pdf_utils.reader.PdfFileReader, iterate r.embedded_signatures, and call validate_pdf_signature(sig, ValidationContext(trust_roots=[...])). pyHanko checks the CMS signature over the /ByteRange, builds the certificate chain to the trust roots, and returns a PdfSignatureStatus with bottom_line (bool), coverage (whether the signature covers the whole file), modification_level and docmdp_ok (whether post-signature incremental updates violate the signer's DocMDP policy), plus timestamp validity. Even without trusting the chain, a cryptographically intact signature whose coverage is ENTIRE_FILE proves byte-level integrity since signing; use skip_diff=True if you only want the cheap coverage check.
  - _signal:_ Legitimacy confirmation: intact signature covering the whole file is near-cryptographic proof of no modification since signing (very strong, trust bonus +15-20). Tamper detection: an invalid digest, or invasive incremental updates after signing (modification_level high / docmdp_ok False), is near-proof of tampering (critical flag). False-positive risk is very low for the integrity part; chain-trust failures can be benign (self-signed corporate seals), so score 'intact but untrusted chain' as mild positive, not negative. Absence of a signature must stay neutral — most invoices are unsigned.
  - _local:_ pip install pyhanko (pulls pyhanko-certvalidator, asn1crypto, cryptography). Pure Python, no system deps, <1s per document, negligible RAM. Fully offline if revocation checking is set to soft-fail/skip.
  - _sources:_ https://docs.pyhanko.eu/en/latest/lib-guide/validation.html, https://github.com/MatthiasValvekens/pyHanko
- **EU Trusted Lists (eIDAS EUTL) qualified signature/eSeal verification with local cache** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ Install pyhanko[etsi]; pyHanko bundles bootstrap keys for the EU List-of-the-Lists (LOTL) and can download + verify member-state trusted lists (ETSI TS 119 612), caching them on disk via FileSystemTLCache with configurable expiry (e.g. 14 days). Validation then reports whether the signer certificate is a qualified eSign/eSeal cert per eIDAS, via the AdES-aligned validation path (ades_lta_validation / the CLI's adesverify --eutl). Cold bootstrap of all lists takes minutes, so warm the cache at deploy time or via a cron job; per-document validation afterwards is fast and fully offline.
  - _signal:_ Very strong legitimacy confirmation: French invoices and attestations increasingly carry qualified eSeals ('cachet serveur' from providers like Docaposte, Universign, ChamberSign), and a signature chaining to the EU trusted list is about the strongest positive signal available. False-positive risk near zero for the positive direction. Only caveat: cache refresh needs network (list download only — the document itself never leaves the machine, so it respects the hard constraint), and a stale cache can wrongly downgrade a valid qualified cert — treat 'qualified check unavailable' as neutral.
  - _local:_ pip install 'pyhanko[etsi]'. Pure Python. Disk cache of trusted lists is a few MB. Per-document check <1s once cache is warm; bootstrap of all EU lists takes several minutes and ~modest bandwidth — run at container start or weekly cron, works on a small cloud box.
  - _sources:_ https://docs.pyhanko.eu/en/latest/cli-guide/validation.html, https://docs.pyhanko.eu/en/latest/lib-guide/validation.html
- **Factur-X / ZUGFeRD embedded XML extraction + XSD validation** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Factur-X invoices are PDF/A-3 files with an embedded UN/CEFACT CII XML (filename factur-x.xml, or zugferd-invoice.xml for ZUGFeRD 1.0). Use the factur-x lib: get_xml_from_pdf() to extract, xml_check_xsd(xml, flavor=...) to validate against the official XSDs for the Minimum/Basic WL/EN 16931/Extended profiles (UBL 2.1 also supported). Alternatively pycheval parses the XML into typed profile objects for easy field access. Presence + schema-valid XML proves the file came out of a real invoicing pipeline.
  - _signal:_ Legitimacy confirmation, and rapidly getting stronger: France's e-invoicing mandate makes structured formats (Factur-X/UBL/CII) obligatory for emission by large/mid-size companies from 1 Sept 2026 and SMEs from Sept 2027, so a valid embedded XML is a mainstream trust signal for French invoices (+10-15). Fraudsters who visually edit a PDF almost never regenerate the attachment. Absence is neutral today. FP risk: low; malformed XML in an otherwise fine invoice is rare but possible from buggy generators — flag medium, not critical.
  - _local:_ pip install factur-x (deps: pypdf>=5.3, lxml, python-stdnum; BSD license) or pip install pycheval. Pure Python, no system deps, milliseconds per document. XSDs ship with the package — fully offline.
  - _sources:_ https://pypi.org/project/factur-x/, https://github.com/akretion/factur-x, https://github.com/zfutura/pycheval, https://www.economie.gouv.fr/tout-savoir-sur-la-facturation-electronique-pour-les-entreprises
- **Cross-check Factur-X XML fields against rendered PDF text (dual-representation consistency)** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ After extracting the embedded XML, parse the machine-readable facts — grand total, tax total, currency, invoice number, issue date, seller name/SIREN/VAT, payee IBAN — and compare each against the values extracted from the visible text layer (pdfplumber/PyMuPDF, with tolerant normalization: 1 234,56 € vs 1234.56). The XML and the rendered page are two independent encodings of the same invoice inside one file; a tamperer editing the visible amount with Paint/Acrobat/an online editor leaves the XML untouched.
  - _signal:_ This is the highest-value check in this whole domain. Match on totals+IBAN+invoice number = strong legitimacy confirmation (the document is internally consistent AND machine-generated). Mismatch on an amount or IBAN = near-proof of tampering (critical flag, exactly the classic invoice-redirection fraud). FP risk: low but nonzero — OCR/extraction errors and rounding displays can cause spurious mismatches, so compare normalized numbers and require exact failure on at least one high-stakes field (total, IBAN) before flagging critical.
  - _local:_ Pure Python on top of factur-x/pycheval + lxml + the existing pdfplumber extraction. No new system deps, milliseconds. The whole check is string/number normalization logic.
  - _sources:_ https://github.com/akretion/factur-x, https://validatefin.com/en/blog/facturx-zugferd-explained
- **EN 16931 Schematron business-rule validation of the extracted invoice XML** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ Beyond XSD shape-checking, the EN 16931 standard defines Schematron business rules (e.g. BR-CO-15: total = sum of line nets + tax; VAT category math; date coherence). factur-x exposes xml_check_schematron() (with check_option='fr-ctc' for French CTC rules), which uses saxonche (Saxon-HE Python bindings) to run the official Schematron-compiled XSLT from the ConnectingEurope eInvoicing-EN16931 artifacts. A fraudster who edits the XML to match a doctored visible amount will almost always break the internal arithmetic (line sums, VAT computation) that these rules enforce.
  - _signal:_ Tamper detection AND legitimacy confirmation: passing ~200 business rules means the invoice's numbers are arithmetically self-consistent the way a real ERP emits them (moderate-strong trust bonus); arithmetic-rule failures on an invoice that claims a standard profile are a strong tamper/fabrication indicator. FP risk: moderate — some real-world generators emit rule warnings on edge cases (rounding, exotic VAT categories), so weight 'fatal' assertions high and 'warning' assertions low.
  - _local:_ pip install factur-x saxonche. saxonche is a native wheel (~100MB installed) but works on plain Linux x86_64 — fine for a small cloud box; note lxml alone cannot run these (XSLT 2.0 required). ~1-2s per validation. Fully offline; schematron artifacts bundled/vendored.
  - _sources:_ https://github.com/ConnectingEurope/eInvoicing-EN16931, https://github.com/akretion/factur-x, https://www.invoicexml.com/blog/ubl-peppol-python
- **PDF/A conformance validation with veraPDF (claim-vs-reality check)** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ Two layers: (1) cheap claim check — pikepdf's metadata interface exposes pdfa_status (the pdfaid:part/conformance XMP claim, e.g. '3B' which Factur-X requires); (2) real validation — run the veraPDF CLI as a subprocess (verapdf --format json --flavour 3b file.pdf) and parse the JSON verdict. veraPDF is the industry-reference open-source validator (GPLv3+/MPLv2+) covering all PDF/A parts. Key trick: a file that CLAIMS PDF/A but FAILS validation is suspicious, because editing a PDF/A file with generic tools (online editors, Paint-style workflows, many PDF libraries) typically breaks conformance while leaving the XMP claim in place.
  - _signal:_ Legitimacy confirmation: valid PDF/A-3 + embedded Factur-X XML = professionally generated archival invoice (moderate bonus). Tamper detection: pdfaid claim present but veraPDF fails = medium-high flag (post-generation processing). FP risk: moderate — plenty of legitimate producers emit slightly non-conformant 'PDF/A' files, so never flag critical on conformance failure alone; combine with other signals. Absence of any PDF/A claim is neutral.
  - _local:_ pikepdf: pip install pikepdf (bundles qpdf, no apt). veraPDF: Java required — apt install default-jre-headless (~200MB) plus the veraPDF-greenfield CLI zip; JVM startup ~1-3s and ~300-500MB RAM per run — acceptable but make it an optional/async module on a small box. Fully offline.
  - _sources:_ https://verapdf.org/, https://docs.verapdf.org/cli/validation/, https://pikepdf.readthedocs.io/en/latest/topics/metadata.html
- **French 2D-Doc barcode decode + ECDSA signature verification with cached ANTS certificates** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ 2D-Doc is a DataMatrix standardized by France Titres/ANTS that carries key document fields (tax income, name, address, IBAN, salary...) plus an ECDSA signature (P-256/384/521, base32) from a government-accredited issuer, on tax notices (avis d'imposition), payslips, proof-of-address, RIBs, attestations. Pipeline: render PDF page to image at ~300dpi (fitz page.get_pixmap), locate/decode the DataMatrix, then feed the raw string to betagouv/2ddoc-parser: it parses the DC04 header (CA ID + Cert ID), resolves the signing certificate from the ANTS Trusted Service List that is embedded in the package (crypto/keys/tsl_signed.xml), and verifies the signature — result.is_valid plus typed fields (AvisImposition, BulletinSalaire, JustificatifDomicile, CarteIdentite...). Then cross-check the signed fields against the rendered text: any divergence means the visible text was altered after issuance.
  - _signal:_ Strongest possible signal for French administrative documents: a valid government-signed barcode whose fields match the visible text is cryptographic legitimacy proof (+20); a valid barcode whose fields DIFFER from the page text is near-proof of tampering (critical). FP risk: decode failures on low-resolution scans are common — treat 'barcode present but undecodable' as neutral/low, never as fraud. Absence is neutral (not all doc types carry one).
  - _local:_ pip install from github betagouv/2ddoc-parser (verification fully offline, TSL embedded — vendor/pin it). DataMatrix decoding: pylibdmtx (needs apt libdmtx0b, robust) or zxing-cpp (pip-only wheel, decodes DataMatrix too, occasional detection misses per upstream issue #695 — try zxing-cpp first, fall back to pylibdmtx). Rendering + decode ~1s/page.
  - _sources:_ https://github.com/betagouv/2ddoc-parser, https://ants.gouv.fr/home/our-missions/digital-solutions/2d-doc, https://github.com/zxing-cpp/zxing-cpp/issues/695
- **SEPA EPC QR (EPC069-12) payload parsing + cross-check against invoice text** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ European payment QR codes ('girocode') have a strict 11-line text payload: 'BCD' / version 001|002 / charset / 'SCT' / BIC (optional in v002) / beneficiary name / IBAN / EUR amount / purpose / remittance reference / text. Decode any QR on the page (zxing-cpp on the rendered pixmap), detect the BCD header, parse the lines, validate the IBAN checksum (python-stdnum or schwifty), then cross-check beneficiary IBAN, name and amount against the values extracted from the invoice text layer.
  - _signal:_ Match between QR IBAN/amount and rendered text = strong internal-consistency trust signal (machine-generated payment data agrees with the visible invoice). Mismatch of IBAN = the canonical invoice-redirection fraud pattern — critical flag; mismatched amount = high. FP risk: low for IBAN comparison (exact string after de-spacing); amount can legitimately differ (deposits/partial payments), so weight amount mismatch lower than IBAN mismatch.
  - _local:_ pip install zxing-cpp python-stdnum (or schwifty). No system deps (zxing-cpp ships wheels — this also replaces pyzbar+libzbar0, unblocking the project's disabled QR feature). Sub-second per page. Fully offline.
  - _sources:_ https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EPC_QR_code, https://www.europeanpaymentscouncil.eu/sites/default/files/kb/file/2024-03/EPC069-12%20v3.1%20Quick%20Response%20Code%20-%20Guidelines%20to%20Enable%20the%20Data%20Capture%20for%20the%20Initiation%20of%20an%20SCT.pdf, https://pypi.org/project/zxing-cpp/, https://blog.jonasneubert.com/2022/09/30/the-best-python-packages-for-reading-barcodes/
