- **QR/hyperlink URL domain consistency vs claimed sender** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Collect all URLs from decoded QR payloads, link annotations (page.get_links() in PyMuPDF) and text-layer URLs; reduce each to eTLD+1 with tldextract (offline mode using its bundled public-suffix snapshot). Compare against the claimed issuer domain derived from the document (sender email domain, domains in a template DB like edf.fr, or the majority domain among the document's own links). No fetching — pure string comparison, so no document data leaves the box.
  - _signal:_ Legitimacy confirmation when QR/link domains all match the claimed issuer (small-moderate bonus, works well with the template DB in Module I). Tamper/fabrication detection when a QR points to an unrelated or look-alike domain (payment phishing via replaced QR — high flag). FP risk: moderate — legit invoices link to payment processors, URL shorteners and CDNs; maintain an allowlist of common payment/tracking domains and only flag when the QR is payment- or portal-like and off-domain.
  - _local:_ pip install zxing-cpp tldextract (use tldextract with suffix_list_urls=() for strictly offline PSL snapshot). Trivial CPU cost.
  - _sources:_ https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EPC_QR_code, https://pypi.org/project/zxing-cpp/
- **Known-good producer/creator fingerprint database** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Maintain a curated dict of Creator+Producer combinations with a trust class: institutional generators (SAP NetWeaver, Oracle BI Publisher, PeopleSoft, Temenos T24 for bank docs; iText/OpenPDF/Apache FOP/BIRT/Jasper Reports common behind telecom-utility invoices; Sage/Cegid for French accounting; wkhtmltopdf+'Qt 4.8.7' and Chromium+'Skia/PDF mNNN' for fintech/SaaS web-to-PDF; Microsoft Word pairs for office docs), and post-processing tools (iLovePDF, Smallpdf, PDF24, Sejda, PDFescape). Score: known institutional pair = trust bonus; document that claims to be from a bank/utility but has a consumer/creative tool (Canva, Photoshop, PowerPoint, LibreOffice Draw) = high flag; institutional Creator + editor Producer (e.g. 'LibreOffice' + 'Adobe Acrobat') = re-save evidence. Seed the DB empirically from your legitimate sample corpus (EDF, Orange, Free, Amazon, Chorus Pro portal outputs) since public lists are incomplete.
  - _signal:_ Moderate legitimacy confirmation (metadata is spoofable, so bonus should be modest, +5-10) and good fabrication detection ('bank statement with Creator: Canva is not ambiguous'). FP risk of the negative direction is real — legit senders sometimes pass documents through converters — so per your CLAUDE.md, flag but don't over-penalize; the positive direction (known pair) is low-FP.
  - _local:_ Pure Python dict lookup on existing PyMuPDF metadata extraction; zero new deps, zero cost. Effort is in curating the DB.
  - _sources:_ https://htpbe.tech/blog/pdf-metadata-fields-complete-reference, https://medium.com/@iurii_rogulia/how-pdf-tampering-detection-actually-works-and-where-it-fails-ad398c311798, https://image-ppubs.uspto.gov/dirsearch-public/print/downloadPdf/11651093
- **Coding-style verification of the claimed producer (metadata-spoofing detection)** (effort: large)
  - _how:_ From 'Robust PDF Files Forensics Using Coding Style' (arXiv:2103.02702): each PDF generator leaves structural idiosyncrasies independent of the Producer string — header comment bytes after %PDF-x.y, trailer dictionary key set and ordering, classic xref table vs cross-reference streams, object numbering/ordering patterns, stream filter choices, string/name formatting. The authors derived 192 rules identifying 11 producers (100% accuracy on some, 74% overall on 508k files) and used them to spot inconsistencies in online PDF services. Implementation: for each producer family in your known-good DB, record 3-5 cheap structural invariants (e.g. Skia/PDF always uses xref streams; wkhtmltopdf emits a specific header comment; iText numbers objects sequentially) from your legitimate corpus, then verify the claimed Producer's invariants hold. pikepdf/raw byte parsing suffices.
  - _signal:_ Tamper/fabrication detection with a legitimacy-confirmation flip side: metadata Producer that matches its structural fingerprint upgrades the producer-DB bonus to a solid signal; a mismatch (claims 'SAP' but file is structured like LibreOffice) is a high flag for spoofed metadata — something naive metadata checks miss entirely. FP risk: moderate — producers change style across versions, so verify against version-tagged corpora and treat single-invariant misses as low severity.
  - _local:_ pip install pikepdf plus raw file parsing (you already depend on PyMuPDF). Milliseconds per file. The cost is corpus collection and rule curation per producer family.
  - _sources:_ https://arxiv.org/abs/2103.02702
- **Single-revision deterministic structure as a positive trust signal** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Machine-generated invoices are written once, deterministically. Compute a 'pristine structure' score from cheap checks: exactly one %%EOF/startxref (no incremental updates); trailer /ID array where ID[0]==ID[1] (unmodified since creation) or a producer-consistent pair; CreationDate == ModDate; no /Prev key in trailer; one content stream per page; consistent font-subset prefixes (ABCDEF+Font) with a small uniform font set; modern generators additionally use xref+object streams. Count revisions by scanning for b'%%EOF' / parsing xref chain with pikepdf. Note: exactly 2 revisions is normal for a signed document (signature appended incrementally) — pair this check with the pyHanko coverage result.
  - _signal:_ Legitimacy confirmation: all-pristine structure = document never touched after generation (moderate bonus, +5-10, low FP). The inverse (3+ revisions, ModDate >> CreationDate, mixed font subsets) is your existing tamper heuristic — this technique makes the ABSENCE of those anomalies an explicit positive score rather than mere silence. Key limitation (from the Medium analysis): a fraudulent document FABRICATED from scratch in Word is also structurally pristine — so this signal proves 'not modified', never 'authentic origin'; combine with producer fingerprint + content checks.
  - _local:_ pikepdf (pip, bundles qpdf) + PyMuPDF you already have. Milliseconds, no system deps.
  - _sources:_ https://medium.com/@iurii_rogulia/how-pdf-tampering-detection-actually-works-and-where-it-fails-ad398c311798, https://arxiv.org/abs/2103.02702, https://htpbe.tech/blog/pdf-metadata-fields-complete-reference
- **XMP media-management provenance analysis (DocumentID / InstanceID / History)** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Parse the XMP packet (pikepdf pdf.open_metadata(), or lxml on the raw metadata stream) for the xmpMM namespace: xmpMM:DocumentID (GUID assigned at creation), xmpMM:InstanceID (new GUID on every save), and xmpMM:History (ordered ResourceEvent list recording each save's action, softwareAgent and timestamp). Positive signal: no history or a single creation event, InstanceID consistent with DocumentID, agents matching the Producer. Negative: history showing 'saved' events by Adobe Acrobat/Photoshop AFTER creation by an institutional generator — an explicit edit trail with tool names and timestamps; also cross-check XMP dates against Info-dictionary dates (mismatch = partial metadata scrubbing).
  - _signal:_ Both directions: clean single-event provenance is a small-moderate legitimacy bonus; a multi-save history with editing tools is high-confidence modification evidence (it literally names the editor and when). FP risk: low for the negative direction when history exists (it's written by the editing software itself), but many producers write no xmpMM at all — absence must be neutral. Sophisticated fraudsters can strip XMP; stripped-XMP-plus-intact-Info inconsistency is itself a soft flag.
  - _local:_ pikepdf (pip) or plain lxml on the metadata stream. Milliseconds. No system deps.
  - _sources:_ https://www.meridiandiscovery.com/articles/pdf-forensic-analysis-xmp-metadata/, https://developer.adobe.com/xmp/docs/XMPNamespaces/xmpMM/, https://www.4n6k.com/2014/02/forensics-quickie-pdf-metadata.html
- **Checksum-valid identifiers + cross-source agreement (IBAN, SIREN/SIRET, VAT) — fully offline** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ python-stdnum validates checksums locally for IBAN (mod-97), French SIREN/SIRET (Luhn), French VAT keys (FR key = f(SIREN)), and EU VAT formats — no API calls. Extract every occurrence of these identifiers from ALL sources inside the document (text layer, Factur-X XML, EPC QR payload, 2D-Doc signed fields) and require (a) checksum validity and (b) agreement across sources. The FR VAT/SIREN arithmetic link is a bonus consistency check: the VAT number embeds the SIREN, so both appearing on an invoice must be mutually consistent.
  - _signal:_ Legitimacy confirmation: all identifiers checksum-valid and identical across independent in-file sources = solid consistency bonus (fraudsters typing a fake or fat-fingered edit frequently break a checksum: ~97% of random IBAN edits fail mod-97). Tamper detection: the same identifier differing between two in-file sources (text vs XML vs QR) is a high/critical flag. FP risk: very low for checksum failures on cleanly extracted text; guard against OCR digit errors by only flagging checksum failure when the text layer (not OCR) was the source.
  - _local:_ pip install python-stdnum (pure Python, already a factur-x dependency). Microseconds per check, fully offline — unlike the INSEE API module, this sends nothing anywhere.
  - _sources:_ https://arthurdejong.org/python-stdnum/, https://github.com/akretion/factur-x

---

### RESEARCH — Font and text-layer forensics in PDFs for detecting text edits (subset-font anomalies, ToUnicode/CMap attacks, rendering-parameter jitter, invisible text, z-order overlays)

**Key insights:**
- PyMuPDF — already TrustyFile's core dependency — natively exposes almost every primitive these techniques need, verified empirically on the installed 1.26.5: get_texttrace() returns per-span render mode ('type': 3 = invisible text), opacity, z-order seqno, spacewidth, and per-char (unicode, glyph_id, origin, bbox); get_bboxlog() returns the drawing-op sequence ('fill-text'/'ignore-text'/'fill-image'/'fill-path') for z-order overlay checks; xref_get_key()/extract_font() give font-dict and font-program access. Only new pip deps needed across all 14 techniques: fonttools (pure Python) and optionally pikepdf (self-contained wheels) — no new apt packages.
- The cheapest high-value combo is: subset-tag cohort analysis (every save session mints a fresh random 6-letter prefix, so two tag cohorts on one base font = two edit sessions) + TouchUp_TextEdit scan (near-zero-FP Acrobat edit marker, used as decisive evidence in the Craig Wright case) + revision text-diff (materialize prior revisions pdfresurrect-style and diff the text — '1025 -> 2025' output is the most explainable evidence any module can produce). All three are pure PyMuPDF and run in milliseconds.
- OCR cross-validation (render page, tesseract, compare against extracted text layer) is the universal catch-all: it is the canonical defense from USENIX 2017 'PDF Mirage' against ToUnicode/CMap remapping, and it also catches invisible-text poisoning and overlays — anything that makes what a human sees differ from what a machine extracts. Since automated invoice processing reads the text layer, this what-you-see-vs-what-you-parse check should gate every amount/date field. Budget: 2–5s/page, so run it selectively (amount-bearing pages, or as stage 2 when static flags fire).
- False-positive control hinges on explicitly whitelisting four benign patterns that mimic fraud: (1) OCR sandwich layers — invisible text in Tesseract's GlyphLessFont under a full-page image is legitimate, invisible text OVER visible text is not; (2) merged/concatenated PDFs legitimately carry multiple subset tags; (3) incremental updates are legitimate for signing/forms/annotations — flag only text-content diffs between revisions; (4) justified text legitimately varies Tw per line — flag only mid-line/intra-token spacing changes.
- Sub-pixel positioning is a real, quantifiable forensic channel: PDF positional adjustments are expressed in 1/1000-of-font-size units (0.0042mm at 12pt), each producer workflow has a characteristic deterministic glyph-shift scheme, and editing a Word document splits text fragments and resets Word's shift accumulator — so baseline jitter, advance-width residuals, and shift-pattern discontinuities can localize the exact edited token (arXiv:2206.02285, Edact-Ray).
- For goal #2 (confirming legitimacy), the strongest positive signals are: a single subset-tag cohort, uniform font embedding state, valid ToUnicode on all fonts, /Widths consistent with embedded font programs (the PDF/A 6.3.6 property), zero or annotation-only revisions, and — most powerful — a producer content-stream fingerprint (coordinate precision, operator style, shift scheme, font naming) that matches the generator the metadata claims. Fingerprints integrate naturally into the planned templates/ per-issuer database as local JSON.
- Reference implementations to study (all local-first): questio (Python CLI, font session tracking + Acrobat edit signature + stream fingerprinting, 0-100 trust score like TrustyFile), PDFRecon (PyMuPDF-based, 40+ indicators with high/indication confidence tiers — a ready-made severity taxonomy), pdfresurrect (revision materialization), Edact-Ray (glyph-shift analysis + overlay-box detection at 4% FP on real corpora), veraPDF (glyph-width validation rules and its documented false-positive traps: CFF nominalWidthX defaults, shared font streams with differing CIDToGIDMap).

**Techniques:**
- **Font subset-tag cohort analysis (multiple subsets of the same base font)** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Enumerate every font in the document (PyMuPDF doc.get_page_fonts(i) per page, or scan xref objects with /Type /Font), strip the subset prefix matching ^[A-Z]{6}\+ from BaseFont/FontName, and group fonts by base name. Per the PDF spec, each subset gets a 6-uppercase-letter tag and different subsets in one file MUST have different tags; generators mint a fresh random tag set per save session. Flag: (a) the same base font appearing with 2+ distinct subset tags (classic sign a word was re-set by an editor in a second session), (b) the same base font both subset-embedded and fully/non-embedded, (c) count distinct tag 'cohorts' as an estimate of edit sessions (the approach used by the questio forensic CLI's 'font session tracking', which calls it its most reliable signal).
  - _signal:_ Catches text edited/re-saved by a second tool: the editor embeds its own subset of the visually-same font. Medium-high strength — one of the best cheap signals. False positives: merged/concatenated PDFs (mail-merge, appended T&C pages), portfolio documents, and some generators that re-subset per page; treat 2 cohorts as 'medium' severity, not critical, and combine with revision count.
  - _local:_ Pure PyMuPDF (already a dependency) plus one regex; no new deps, no system packages. Milliseconds per document, negligible RAM.
  - _sources:_ https://github.com/abcreativ/questio, https://www.verypdf.com/document/pdf-format-reference/pg_0419.htm, https://github.com/Rasmus-Riis/PDFRecon, https://www.pdflib.com/pcos-cookbook/resources/advanced-font-properties/
- **Acrobat TouchUp_TextEdit marker scan (smoking-gun edit marker)** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Adobe Acrobat's Edit Text tool inserts a /TouchUp_TextEdit marked-content point (MP/BDC operator) into the content stream at every edited region — five edits produce five markers. Decompress all content streams (PyMuPDF page.read_contents() or xref stream access) and regex-search for TouchUp_TextEdit; also detect Acrobat-specific CIDFont objects that only Acrobat's edit tool produces (questio's 'Acrobat Edit Signature'). Report the marker count and, by parsing surrounding text-positioning operators, the approximate page location of each edit.
  - _signal:_ Near-zero false-positive, high-confidence proof that text was edited in Acrobat after generation — used as decisive evidence in real litigation (the Craig Wright/Satoshi case) and classified 'high confidence' by PDFRecon. Catches only Acrobat edits (fraudsters using other editors escape it), so absence proves nothing.
  - _local:_ Pure PyMuPDF + regex over decompressed streams. No new deps, milliseconds per document.
  - _sources:_ https://mjg59.dreamwidth.org/73317.html, https://github.com/Rasmus-Riis/PDFRecon, https://github.com/abcreativ/questio, https://www.forensicfocus.com/forums/general/pdf-manipulated/
- **Mid-line / mid-token font, size, and color change detection** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ Extract character-level attributes (pdfplumber page.chars gives fontname/size per char; PyMuPDF get_text('rawdict') or get_texttrace() gives font, size, color, per-char bbox). Cluster chars into visual lines by baseline y, then tokens by whitespace gaps. Flag any change of fontname, font size (>0.2pt delta), fill color, or weight (Bold in name) occurring INSIDE a token — especially inside amount-like tokens (digits, currency). Commercial bank-statement tamper detectors specifically hunt 9pt-vs-9.5pt insertions; 0.5pt deltas are invisible to humans but trivially machine-detectable.
  - _signal:_ Catches the most common manual fraud: a number or word retyped with an editor that picked a near-match font/size. Strong signal when confined to within-token changes. False-positive risk is real for line-level changes (bold totals, styled currency symbols, superscripts), so restrict to intra-token changes and exempt sub/superscript patterns (size change + baseline shift together).
  - _local:_ pdfplumber and PyMuPDF are already project dependencies. Pure Python, ~tens of ms per page, negligible RAM.
  - _sources:_ https://precisa.in/blog/bank-statements-document-tampering-detection/, https://patents.google.com/patent/US11651093B1/en, https://pymupdf.readthedocs.io/en/latest/app1.html
- **Baseline jitter and advance-width residual analysis (Tm/Td micro-offsets)** (effort: large)
  - _how:_ For each visual line, compute the dominant baseline y from char origins (get_texttrace gives exact per-char origin points — verified locally: chars are (unicode, glyph_id, (x,y) origin, bbox)). Flag tokens whose baseline deviates from the line's baseline by a sub-point amount (0.05–0.5pt) — manually placed replacement text is almost never re-positioned to the exact original baseline. Second check: recompute each character's expected x-advance from font metrics (glyph_width × size / 1000) and compare with the actual next-char origin; machine-generated text has residuals that are zero or follow the producer's deterministic glyph-shift scheme, while pasted text objects show discontinuous residuals. The 'Story Beyond the Eye' paper (arXiv:2206.02285) established that positional adjustments are expressed in 1/1000-of-font-size text-space units (sub-pixel precision, 0.0042mm at 12pt), that each producer workflow has a characteristic shift scheme, and crucially that editing a text fragment in Word SPLITS it into two fragments and RESETS the shift accumulator — so fragment boundaries and shift-pattern discontinuities mid-line localize edits.
  - _signal:_ Catches manually repositioned/patched text with sub-pixel sensitivity; the fragment-split + accumulator-reset artifact directly localizes Word-originated edits. Medium-strong, but the highest false-positive risk in this list: justified text, kerning TJ adjustments, and tables all produce legitimate offsets. Ship baseline-jitter first (low FP: baselines within one line should be exactly equal), keep advance-residual analysis as corroboration only.
  - _local:_ PyMuPDF get_texttrace() for origins (verified working locally); pikepdf (pip 'pikepdf', self-contained wheels bundle qpdf) or pdfminer.six to parse raw TJ/Td/Tm operators if operator-level data is needed. CPU trivial; the paper's tooling ran on a consumer laptop.
  - _sources:_ https://arxiv.org/pdf/2206.02285, https://resistant.ai/blog/document-fraud, https://pikepdf.readthedocs.io/en/latest/topics/content_streams.html
- **Character/word spacing (Tc/Tw) and TJ-kern anomaly detection** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ Parse content streams (pikepdf.parse_content_stream yields (operands, operator) tuples) and track Tc (char spacing), Tw (word spacing), and inline TJ adjustment values per text object. Flags: Tc/Tw values that change mid-line while font stays constant; TJ kern values on one token that are statistical outliers vs the document's distribution (fraudsters stretch/squeeze replacement text to fill the original width — e.g. swapping a narrow '1' for a wide '4' and compensating with negative kerns); a token whose internal inter-char gaps deviate >2–3σ from same-font tokens elsewhere. Equivalent gap statistics can be computed without stream parsing from get_texttrace char origins.
  - _signal:_ Catches width-compensated character swaps — a sophisticated edit invisible to font-name checks because the SAME font is reused. Medium strength. False positives: justified paragraphs (legitimate per-line Tw changes — but rarely mid-line), letter-spaced headings, tabular kerning; calibrate on the document's own distribution rather than absolute thresholds.
  - _local:_ pikepdf (pip, no system deps, wheels include qpdf) or reuse get_texttrace origins with PyMuPDF. Milliseconds per page, low RAM.
  - _sources:_ https://arxiv.org/pdf/2206.02285, https://pikepdf.readthedocs.io/en/latest/topics/content_streams.html, https://precisa.in/blog/bank-statements-document-tampering-detection/
- **Invisible text detection with legit-OCR vs malicious-overlay classification** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ PyMuPDF page.get_texttrace() returns span 'type' = the text render mode — verified locally: render-mode-3 text comes back as type 3 and appears as 'ignore-text' entries in page.get_bboxlog(). Also detect opacity==0 and text whose bbox is covered by a later-drawn object (higher seqno). Then CLASSIFY: legitimate OCR layer = invisible text using Tesseract's 'GlyphLessFont' (the signature of ocrmypdf/Tesseract sandwich PDFs), lying UNDER a full-page scanned image, with producer strings like ocrmypdf/ABBYY; malicious = invisible text overlapping VISIBLE text spans, or invisible text whose content contradicts what OCR of the rendered page reads (extraction poisoning — automated invoice-processing systems read the invisible layer, humans see the image).
  - _signal:_ Detection itself is trivial and certain; the classification carries the value. Invisible text overlapping visible text with different content is a strong fraud/poisoning indicator. False positives are dominated by OCR layers — the GlyphLessFont + under-image + producer heuristics whitelist virtually all of them, leaving low residual FP.
  - _local:_ Pure PyMuPDF (verified working on installed 1.26.5). Milliseconds per page. The contradiction check reuses the OCR cross-validation module below.
  - _sources:_ https://ocrmypdf.readthedocs.io/en/latest/advanced.html, https://github.com/pymupdf/PyMuPDF/discussions/3537, https://github.com/Rasmus-Riis/PDFRecon
- **Z-order overlay detection: text-after-image, image-over-text, white rectangles** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ page.get_bboxlog() returns the page's drawing operations in execution order — ('fill-text'|'ignore-text'|'fill-image'|'fill-path'|'stroke-path', bbox) — verified locally. Three flags: (1) fill-text drawn AFTER a fill-image with intersecting bbox = text patched on top of a scanned/rasterized document (classic amount replacement on a scanned invoice); (2) fill-image or opaque fill-path drawn AFTER fill-text it covers = 'hide' overlay concealing original content (the PDF Shadow Attack 'Hide' variant; iText's detection enhances text extraction to flag images covering extracted text); (3) white/near-white filled rectangles over text — Edact-Ray's redaction detector refined this by checking whether pixels in each covered glyph's bbox are one uniform color, reporting only 4% FP on real court documents. Whitelist: a full-page image drawn FIRST (scanned page background) is the normal OCR-PDF pattern; small logos/stamps/signature images that overlap no text.
  - _signal:_ Catches both overlay-hiding (fraudster covers original value) and patch-on-scan (fraudster paints new value over raster). Strong signal when the overlap region contains amount/date text. False positives: letterhead graphics, stamps, watermarks, signature images, highlight annotations — bbox-overlap-with-text plus uniform-color tests keep FP low (~4% in Edact-Ray's evaluation).
  - _local:_ Pure PyMuPDF get_bboxlog + a rendered pixmap for the uniform-color check. ~100ms per page, low RAM.
  - _sources:_ https://itextpdf.com/blog/technical-notes/investigating-pdf-shadow-attacks-depth-pdf-security-using-itext-part-2, https://www.ndss-symposium.org/wp-content/uploads/ndss2021_1B-4_24117_paper.pdf, https://arxiv.org/pdf/2206.02285, https://www.group-ib.com/blog/pdf-tampering/
- **OCR cross-validation: rendered pixels vs extracted text layer (ToUnicode-remap defense)** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ Render each page to an image (fitz page.get_pixmap at ~300dpi), OCR it with local Tesseract (pytesseract — already in the stack), and align the OCR tokens against the PDF's extracted text layer (word-level alignment via bboxes; compare with fuzzy matching). Substantial mismatches — especially on digits, amounts, dates, IBANs — reveal ToUnicode/CMap remapping (font renders glyph 'A' while ToUnicode claims 'C'), content-masking fonts, invisible-text poisoning, or overlays. This is the canonical defense proposed in the USENIX Security 2017 'PDF Mirage: Content Masking Attack' paper, and it is the ONLY check that validates what a human actually sees against what machines extract.
  - _signal:_ Catch-all with the broadest coverage: any technique that makes rendered content differ from extracted content trips it. Very strong on digit/amount tokens (OCR is reliable on clean digits). False positives: ligatures, hyphenation, diacritics, stylized fonts, OCR errors — mitigate by comparing only high-OCR-confidence tokens, normalizing ligatures/whitespace, and weighting digit-token mismatches highest.
  - _local:_ pytesseract + tesseract-ocr (+tesseract-ocr-fra) apt packages — all already in TrustyFile's stack/packages.txt. Cost is the ceiling: 2–5s per page on one CPU core, ~200–500MB RAM. Run only on pages containing amounts, or as a second-stage check when other flags fire, to stay within the seconds-per-document budget.
  - _sources:_ https://www.usenix.org/system/files/conference/usenixsecurity17/sec17-markwood.pdf, https://www.cs.purdue.edu/homes/luo401/paper/Exploiting_PDF_Obfuscation_in_LLMs__arXiv__and_More.pdf
- **Static ToUnicode/CMap and encoding anomaly checks** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ For each font dict (PyMuPDF doc.xref_get_key(xref, 'ToUnicode') / 'Encoding' — verified locally): flag (a) fonts with no ToUnicode AND a non-standard/custom encoding (text extraction from them is unverifiable — a favorite of extraction-poisoning and sloppy editors), (b) /Differences arrays that remap standard printable codes to unrelated glyph names, (c) two font objects sharing a BaseFont but carrying different Encoding/Differences or different ToUnicode CMaps (a second, differently-mapped copy of 'the same' font is an editing artifact), (d) ToUnicode CMaps mapping used codes to Unicode outside plausible ranges for an invoice's language. MuPDF returning U+FFFD for characters during extraction is itself a per-char symptom of broken/missing mapping.
  - _signal:_ Individually weak-to-medium — many legitimate generators emit odd encodings — so treat each as a low-severity corroborating flag; the combination (custom encoding + no ToUnicode + font used only in one amount token) is strong. Its real value is routing: any of these flags should trigger the OCR cross-validation module. As a legitimacy signal, all-fonts-have-valid-ToUnicode is a positive trust marker.
  - _local:_ Pure PyMuPDF xref access; CMap stream parsing is ~50 lines of Python (bfchar/bfrange). Milliseconds per document.
  - _sources:_ https://github.com/typst/typst/issues/4582, https://www.usenix.org/system/files/conference/usenixsecurity17/sec17-markwood.pdf, https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S2666281726000181
- **Glyph-width consistency check: /Widths array vs embedded font program** (effort: large)
  - _how:_ For each embedded font actually used for rendering: extract the font program (doc.extract_font(xref) — API verified present), parse with fontTools (TTFont; hmtx table for TrueType, CharStrings widths for CFF), and compare per-glyph advance widths against the PDF font dictionary's /Widths (simple fonts) or /W (CID fonts) for the glyphs referenced in content streams. This is PDF/A rule 6.3.6 ('glyph width info in the font dictionary and in the embedded font program shall be consistent'), which veraPDF implements. A mismatch means someone swapped text or the font program while faking widths to preserve layout, or edited with a tool that reused the original font dict.
  - _signal:_ Medium-strong: clean single-generator documents essentially always pass, so failures on invoice-class documents are meaningful. Known false-positive traps documented in veraPDF issues: CFF nominalWidthX/defaultWidthX defaults, two font dicts sharing one font stream with different CIDToGIDMap, MissingWidth fallback — allow ±1/1000-unit tolerance and check ONLY glyphs actually rendered (veraPDF's own policy). Passing the check is a good positive-legitimacy signal (the doc would survive PDF/A font validation).
  - _local:_ pip install fonttools (pure Python, no system deps; NOT currently installed in the project env). ~10–50ms per font to parse; a document has typically <10 fonts. Low RAM.
  - _sources:_ https://github.com/veraPDF/veraPDF-library/issues/1009, http://martin.hoppenheit.info/blog/2018/pdfa-validation-and-inconsistent-glyph-width-information/, https://github.com/veraPDF/veraPDF-library/issues/941, https://github.com/veraPDF/veraPDF-library/issues/1145
- **Subset coverage check: characters used but absent from the embedded subset** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ get_texttrace() exposes both the unicode and the GLYPH ID of every rendered char (verified locally: chars are (unicode, glyph_id, origin, bbox)). For each subset-embedded font, collect the glyph IDs used on all pages and verify each exists (non-empty outline) in the embedded font program via fontTools (glyf/CFF table, cmap). Text inserted after generation that references character codes the original subset never included renders as .notdef/blank or garbage — the docling project documents extraction producing 'GLYPH<id>' placeholders exactly in this situation. Also flag glyph_id=0 (.notdef) occurrences inside rendered text.
  - _signal:_ Used-but-missing glyphs are a strong edit indicator (a correct generator never emits codes outside its own subset) with low false-positive risk; occasional broken-but-legit generators exist, so severity 'high' not 'critical'. The inverse (subset contains exactly the used glyphs, nothing missing) is a mild legitimacy signal.
  - _local:_ PyMuPDF + fontTools (pip 'fonttools'). Milliseconds to tens of ms per font. No system deps.
  - _sources:_ https://github.com/docling-project/docling/issues/2170, https://www.compdf.com/blog/what-is-so-hard-about-pdf-text-extraction, https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S2666281726000181
- **Incremental-update revision extraction and text-level diff** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ Count revisions (multiple %%EOF/startxref markers, or PyMuPDF's journal/xref facilities). If >1: reconstruct each earlier revision by truncating the file bytes at each prior startxref/%%EOF boundary (pdfresurrect's technique — the PDF format keeps full previous versions in the file), open each truncated version with fitz.open(stream=...), extract text per page, and diff token-by-token against the final version. Report exactly what changed — '1025' -> '2025' in the totals line is a smoking gun no other check produces. Complementary structural flags from PDFRecon: objects with generation >0, 'linearized + updated' combination, trailer /ID first-vs-second mismatch, XMP history gaps, multiple startxref entries. This also detects Shadow-Attack-style hide-and-replace edits which live entirely in incremental updates.
  - _signal:_ When the diff shows content changes to amounts/dates, this is the single most conclusive and most explainable signal available. False positives: incremental updates are LEGITIMATE for signing, form-filling, and annotations — so never flag the mere presence of revisions; flag only content-stream/text diffs, and downgrade annotation-only or signature-only updates to neutral (a valid signature revision is a positive trust signal).
  - _local:_ Pure PyMuPDF + byte-level scanning. Fast (one extra open+extract per revision; most docs have 1–2 revisions). Low RAM.
  - _sources:_ https://github.com/enferex/pdfresurrect, https://github.com/Rasmus-Riis/PDFRecon, https://www.ndss-symposium.org/wp-content/uploads/ndss2021_1B-4_24117_paper.pdf, https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.00827
- **Font embedding-state and descriptor consistency check** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ For every font: read FontDescriptor (FontFile/FontFile2/FontFile3 presence = embedded) via xref access. Flags: (a) document mixes embedded subset fonts with non-embedded system fonts (Arial/Calibri/TimesNewRoman) — the patented commercial heuristic is that text injected after creation often has a different embedded/non-embedded state than the original run ('fonts-embedded-types detector', US11651093); (b) FontDescriptor /Flags bits contradict the font name (Symbolic flag on a text font, Serif bit on 'Arial'); (c) a text box/line containing multiple fonts where sibling lines use one ('wrong font injected into a text box' — US12164657); (d) non-embedded non-standard-14 fonts in a supposedly professionally generated invoice.
  - _signal:_ Medium. Very cheap and catches lazy edits (editor defaults to a non-embedded system font). False positives: many legitimate producers use non-embedded standard-14 fonts throughout — flag the MIXTURE and the mid-box injection, not non-embedding per se. Uniform embedding state across the document is a positive trust signal.
  - _local:_ Pure PyMuPDF xref reads; milliseconds. No new deps.
  - _sources:_ https://patents.google.com/patent/US11651093B1/en, https://image-ppubs.uspto.gov/dirsearch-public/print/downloadPdf/12164657, https://www.pdflib.com/pcos-cookbook/resources/advanced-font-properties/
- **Producer content-stream fingerprinting (legitimacy confirmation)** (effort: large)
  - _how:_ Learn per-generator 'style' fingerprints from known-good corpora: coordinate decimal precision (Word emits different precision than wkhtmltopdf or ReportLab), preferred operators (Td vs Tm vs TJ), glyph-shift scheme class (MS Word's dependent accumulated-WYSIWYG-error scheme vs Google Docs' unadjusted exports — reverse-engineered in arXiv:2206.02285), font resource naming patterns (/F1 vs /TT0 vs /C2_0), object count ranges, subset-prefix style. At analysis time, compare the document's observed style against the fingerprint of the generator its Producer metadata claims. Match = trust bonus; mismatch (claims 'Microsoft Word' but content stream looks like an online editor) = strong flag. This is questio's 'content stream fingerprinting' with local JSON issuer profiles, and it dovetails with TrustyFile's planned template-matching module (add a per-issuer stream fingerprint to each template.json).
  - _signal:_ This is the premier POSITIVE trust signal for goal #2 (confirming legitimate files): a document whose stream style, shift scheme, and font naming all match its claimed producer is very likely untouched. As a fraud detector it catches producer-metadata spoofing. False positives: generator version drift changes fingerprints — store min/max ranges per version and decay confidence for unknown versions.
  - _local:_ pikepdf or PyMuPDF for operator extraction; fingerprints as local JSON (questio stores them in ~/.questio/fingerprints). Needs a corpus of known-good documents per issuer/generator — start with the 5 planned template companies. Fast at inference (<100ms); the effort is corpus curation.
  - _sources:_ https://github.com/abcreativ/questio, https://arxiv.org/pdf/2206.02285

---

### RESEARCH — Machine learning for document forgery detection on CPU-only infrastructure (datasets, pretrained tamper-localization models, ONNX feasibility, lightweight alternatives, OCR cross-checking)

**Key insights:**
- Honest feasibility verdict: published deep tamper-localization models (DTD 67M/272 GFLOPs, FFDN 89M/453 GFLOPs, TruFor 68.7M/232 GFLOPs, CAT-Net 114M/134 GFLOPs per 512x512 crop) would take minutes per A4 page and >1.5GB RAM on a small CPU box, most carry non-commercial licenses (DocTamper dataset, TruFor code), and none have published ONNX exports — they do not fit the hot path.
- Even ignoring compute, generalization kills them: DocForge-Bench (2026) shows DocTamper-trained models collapse from F1 0.914 in-domain to 0.045 on T-SROIE and ~0.002-0.013 on real receipt forgeries; NO method achieves F1>=0.3 on 6+ datasets. Born-digital French invoices are outside every training distribution, so expect noisy masks and false positives, the exact thing TrustyFile wants to minimize.
- The collapse is largely a CALIBRATION problem, not a feature problem: models keep Pixel-AUC 0.76-0.90 cross-domain, and recalibrating the threshold with only ~10 labeled in-domain images recovers 39-55% of the oracle F1 gap (documents have 0.27-4.17% tampered pixels vs 10-30% in natural-image benchmarks). Whatever scorers TrustyFile uses, keep continuous scores and calibrate thresholds on a small in-domain set.
- The highest-ROI 'ML-adjacent' technique for this project is not a neural net: OCR-vs-PDF-text-layer cross-checking (render page, OCR, diff against embedded text) is a few seconds per page with tesseract/RapidOCR, catches overlay/hidden-text tampering, and doubles as a strong positive legitimacy signal when everything matches.
- If a deep model is added later, the only realistic route under 1-2GB RAM / few-seconds-per-page is training your own SegFormer-B0-class segmenter (~3.7M params, ~8.4 GFLOPs@512) on SELF-SYNTHESIZED tampered documents (DocTamper's synthesis code + FSTS's realistic tampering-parameter modeling, which gains +14-21% F1 on real data), then INT8 ONNX/OpenVINO export: ~2-8s/page at 150dpi. Training needs a one-off free-tier GPU; inference stays fully local.
- Classical forensics + sklearn remains the right default: copy-move keypoint detection, ELA/JPEG-ghost/blocking-grid analysis (a-contrario NFA variants give controlled false-alarm rates), LBP/IQM features + SVM, and OCR character-geometry outlier detection each run in <1-3s/page in pure pip packages (opencv-python, scikit-learn, scikit-image, numpy) — but gate JPEG-history and copy-move checks by page type, since born-digital vector pages have no JPEG history and repeated glyphs are pixel-identical by construction (major false-positive trap).
- Public datasets (DocTamper 170k, Find it! 1,969 French receipts, Find it again! 988, T-SROIE 462, FSTS 294k, SIDTD/FantasyID for ID docs) are best used for EVALUATION and false-positive regression testing rather than as shipped training data — several are research/non-commercial licensed, and their legitimate subsets are the most valuable asset for measuring TrustyFile's false-positive rate per document type. All analysis stays local; datasets must not be re-uploaded to third-party services.

**Techniques:**
- **OCR-vs-PDF-text-layer cross-check (render page, OCR it, diff against embedded text)** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ Render each PDF page to an image with fitz.page.get_pixmap() (150-200 dpi), run OCR (pytesseract or rapidocr-onnxruntime), then align OCR word boxes against the embedded text-layer words from pdfplumber/PyMuPDF (same coordinate space after scaling). Compare per-word with fuzzy matching (rapidfuzz) and flag words where the visible pixels disagree with the text layer — this catches text drawn OVER original content, invisible/hidden text layers, and image patches pasted over text. A high match rate is a strong positive legitimacy signal for born-digital PDFs.
  - _signal:_ Catches overlay tampering (new text object drawn on top), hidden-text mismatches, and image-over-text patches; simultaneously CONFIRMS legitimacy when visible text == text layer everywhere. Signal is strong and interpretable. False-positive risk is moderate: OCR errors on small fonts, ligatures, accented French characters, and rotated text — mitigate with per-word OCR confidence thresholds, fuzzy matching (Levenshtein ratio > 0.8), and only flagging clusters of mismatches, not single words. A patent (US10489645) and industry write-ups describe exactly this text-layer-vs-OCR comparison as a core tamper check.
  - _local:_ Fully local. pip: pytesseract (+ apt tesseract-ocr tesseract-ocr-fra) or rapidocr-onnxruntime (pure pip, ONNX models ~15MB, det 100-210ms + rec 20-30ms per region on CPU); pdfplumber, PyMuPDF, rapidfuzz. Tesseract: ~2-5s/page; RapidOCR: ~1-3s/page. RAM well under 500MB. Comfortable on a 1-2GB box.
  - _sources:_ https://blog.tampercheck.ai/document-tampering-detection-vs-ocr, https://image-ppubs.uspto.gov/dirsearch-public/print/downloadPdf/10489645, https://github.com/rapidai/rapidocr, https://pypi.org/project/rapidocr-onnxruntime/
- **OCR character-geometry graph features + sklearn Random Forest (anomaly in glyph placement)** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ Build a graph from OCR/text-layer character and word bounding boxes: node features = box height/width, baseline y-offset, inter-character spacing, font-size ratio to line median; edge features = neighbor alignment deltas. Feed aggregated features (per word/line) to a Random Forest or gradient boosting classifier that flags words whose geometry deviates from the document's own statistics — inserted or replaced characters almost never sit perfectly on the original baseline/kerning grid. Hariharan et al. (arXiv 2009.05158) framed manipulation detection exactly this way on real business documents and 'dramatically outperformed' prior procedural methods.
  - _signal:_ Catches character insertion/substitution (amount changes like 1025→2025), text moved or re-typeset in an editor — the most common invoice fraud. Works on both born-digital and scanned docs. Medium-strong signal; degrades to unsupervised heuristics gracefully (flag words > k MAD from line statistics) if you lack training data. False-positive risk: proportional/tabular font mixes and right-aligned numeric columns naturally vary — calibrate on legitimate invoice corpora and flag only strong outliers.
  - _local:_ Fully local, trivial compute. pip: scikit-learn, numpy, pdfplumber/pytesseract for boxes. Model is <1MB, inference microseconds; feature extraction rides on OCR you already run. No system deps beyond tesseract. Needs labeled or self-synthesized training data (see synthesis technique) — or ship it as pure statistical outlier scoring first.
  - _sources:_ https://arxiv.org/abs/2009.05158
- **Copy-move forgery detection with OpenCV keypoints (SIFT/ORB + clustering + RANSAC)** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ Extract SIFT keypoints on the rendered page or embedded images, match descriptors against the same image (ratio test), cluster matched pairs with DBSCAN, and validate clusters with RANSAC affine estimation — spatially coherent groups of matches between two different regions indicate a region was duplicated (classic Amerini et al. TIFS 2011 pipeline; multiple reference Python/OpenCV implementations exist). Directly addresses the 'Paint copy-paste a digit elsewhere' test case in the project plan.
  - _signal:_ Catches duplicated amounts/digits/stamps/signatures copied within a document or between regions of an embedded photo/scan. Strong when it fires with a large coherent cluster. CRITICAL caveat: on born-digital PDFs every repeated glyph is a pixel-perfect duplicate by construction, so raw glyph-level matches are meaningless — restrict to scanned/photographed pages (detect via image-only pages), require clusters covering multi-character regions, and exclude matches whose offset equals text-line spacing. With those filters FP risk is low-moderate; without them it is very high.
  - _local:_ Fully local. pip: opencv-python (SIFT is in main OpenCV >= 4.4, patent expired), scikit-learn for DBSCAN, numpy. 1-3s per page at 150-200 dpi, <300MB RAM. No system deps.
  - _sources:_ https://github.com/lambertoballan/sift-forensic, https://github.com/andreacos/SIFT-based-copy-move-forgery-detection, https://github.com/cantugba/Copy_Move_Forgery_Detection
- **Double-JPEG / blocking-grid forensics on embedded images (ELA + JPEG ghost + grid misalignment)** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ For each embedded JPEG (extracted via PyMuPDF without re-encoding): (1) ELA — resave at quality ~95 and map per-region error; already-compressed regions change less than freshly pasted ones; (2) JPEG ghost — resave across a quality sweep and look for local minima that differ by region; (3) blocking-artifact grid detection — locate the 8x8 JPEG grid per region (cross-correlation of blockiness); a pasted patch usually has a shifted or absent grid. The a-contrario grid detector (tinankh/GOD, IPOL-style) gives a statistically controlled false-alarm rate (NFA), which matters for your minimize-false-positives goal.
  - _signal:_ Catches pasted/edited regions inside scanned or photographed documents (receipts, ID photos) — resaves and splices leave compression-history inconsistencies. Medium-strong on scans/photos; near-zero value on vector-only born-digital pages (no JPEG history) — gate it on page type. FP risk: heavily recompressed WhatsApp-style images flatten the signal and uniform white backgrounds carry no grid; a-contrario NFA thresholds keep false alarms controlled; treat ELA alone as weak/advisory (AWS's own ELA+CNN sample admits it misses subtle edits).
  - _local:_ Fully local, pure numpy/Pillow/OpenCV; optional jpegio for direct DCT coefficient access. Milliseconds to ~1s per image, negligible RAM. This is the natural core of the planned Module H (forensics.py).
  - _sources:_ https://github.com/tinankh/GOD, https://www.infosecinstitute.com/resources/digital-forensics/error-level-analysis-detect-image-manipulation/, https://github.com/aws-samples/document-tampering-detection, https://arxiv.org/pdf/2003.09393
- **Classical features + small sklearn classifier (image-quality measures, LBP, FFT texture -> SVM/RF)** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ Compute cheap handcrafted features per page or per region: image quality measures (Ryu & Lee used 17 IQM features + SVM for document forgery), Local Binary Patterns for texture discontinuities, FFT energy for periodic/moire patterns, local noise variance maps. Train an SVM/RandomForest on legitimate vs tampered samples (from public receipt datasets or your own synthesis). This is the lineage of methods used by Find it! contest (ICPR 2018) participants on real French receipts.
  - _signal:_ Catches background discontinuities, re-saved patches, texture breaks around edits. Medium signal — LBP+FFT+SVM reports 98.7% on CASIA v2, but CASIA is natural images; on documents expect much less. Main value: extremely cheap second opinion that can be trained/recalibrated on YOUR document distribution. FP risk moderate and controllable via threshold choice; the classifier is only as good as domain match of training data.
  - _local:_ Fully local. pip: scikit-learn, scikit-image (LBP), opencv-python, numpy. Feature extraction <1s/page, model <5MB, inference instant. Ideal for a 1-2GB box. Training data is the bottleneck, not compute.
  - _sources:_ https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/978-3-540-89796-5_50, https://hal.science/hal-02316399/, https://www.techrxiv.org/users/908787/articles/1282650/master/file/data/Fraud_Detection_Survey_TechRxiv/Fraud_Detection_Survey_TechRxiv.pdf
- **Off-the-shelf deep tamper-localization models (DTD, FFDN, ADCD-Net, TruFor, CAT-Net) — evaluated honestly: NOT recommended as a production module** (effort: large)
  - _how:_ These are pixel-level segmentation networks: DTD (ConvNeXt+SwinV2 dual RGB/DCT streams, 67M params, 272 GFLOPs@512), FFDN (89M, 453 GFLOPs), ADCD-Net (ICCV'25, DCT+RGB+OCR masks, weights on GitHub), TruFor (Noiseprint++ + SegFormer, 68.7M params, ~232 GFLOPs@512), CAT-Net (114M, 134 GFLOPs). You would slide 512x512 crops over a rendered page and merge masks. Weights are downloadable (DocTamper repo, FFDN/ADCD-Net GitHub, ForensicHub checkpoint links, TruFor docker).
  - _signal:_ In-domain they look spectacular (DTD F1 0.914 on DocTamper test) but DocForge-Bench (2026) shows catastrophic cross-domain collapse: DocTamper-trained models drop to F1 0.045 on T-SROIE and ~0.002-0.013 on real receipt forgeries; best cross-domain mean F1 is only ~0.34 (TruFor/CAT-Net), and no method reaches F1>=0.3 on 6+ datasets. On flat born-digital French invoices (out of every training distribution) expect noisy masks and high false-positive risk. Licensing is also blocking: DocTamper dataset and TruFor code are explicitly non-commercial.
  - _local:_ Technically runnable locally (PyTorch CPU wheels) but impractical on the target box: 232-453 GFLOPs per 512x512 crop means roughly 5-20s per crop on a small vCPU; an A4 page at 300dpi is ~24-35 crops, i.e. minutes per page; FP32 weights alone are 270-360MB and peak RAM exceeds 1.5-2GB. No published ONNX exports exist for the document-specific models; DCT-stream models additionally require true JPEG inputs (a rendered PDF page has no JPEG history, breaking their main feature). Verdict: keep out of the hot path; at most an optional offline 'deep scan' mode.
  - _sources:_ https://github.com/qcf-568/DocTamper, https://openaccess.thecvf.com/content/CVPR2023/html/Qu_Towards_Robust_Tampered_Text_Detection_in_Document_Image_New_Dataset_CVPR_2023_paper.html, https://arxiv.org/html/2603.01433v2, https://arxiv.org/pdf/2505.11003
- **Train-your-own lightweight tamper segmenter (SegFormer-B0 / small U-Net) on self-synthesized tampered documents, exported to INT8 ONNX** (effort: large)
  - _how:_ If you want deep tamper localization, the realistic path is: (1) generate training data by programmatically tampering YOUR document distribution — DocTamper's repo includes its data-synthesis code, and the FSTS paper (2025) shows that modeling realistic tampering parameter distributions (copy-move, splice, insert, replace + post-processing like recompression/blur) yields +14-21% F1 on real-world test sets vs DocTamper-style synthesis; (2) fine-tune a small encoder-decoder (SegFormer-B0 ~3.7M params ~8.4 GFLOPs@512, or MobileNetV3-U-Net) on 512 crops; (3) export to ONNX, statically quantize to INT8 (onnxruntime or OpenVINO, 2-3x speedup on Intel with AVX-VNNI), run sliding-window at 150dpi (~6-9 crops/A4).
  - _signal:_ A domain-matched small model beats a domain-mismatched SOTA model — DocForge-Bench shows the failure mode is distribution shift, not architecture. Catches pixel-level edits (pasted patches, inpainted text) that classical modules miss. Signal medium until you invest in good synthesis; FP risk is the key danger and MUST be controlled by threshold calibration (see calibration technique) and by reporting region+confidence rather than binary verdicts.
  - _local:_ Inference fully local and genuinely feasible: pip onnxruntime (or openvino), model file 4-15MB, ~0.3-1s per 512 crop on 2 vCPUs, so ~2-8s/page at 150dpi, peak RAM <700MB — fits the 1-2GB box. Training requires a one-off GPU (Colab/Kaggle free tier suffices for B0-scale). This is the only deep-learning route that meets your constraints.
  - _sources:_ https://arxiv.org/html/2511.12658v1, https://github.com/qcf-568/DocTamper, https://codezen.medium.com/blazing-fast-inference-with-quantized-onnx-models-518f23777741, https://arxiv.org/pdf/2502.09941
- **Per-domain threshold calibration with a tiny labeled set (the cheapest ML win from DocForge-Bench)** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ DocForge-Bench's central finding: cross-domain 'failure' of tamper detectors is mostly CALIBRATION failure, not feature failure — models keep Pixel-AUC 0.76-0.90 while F1 at the default 0.5 threshold collapses, because documents have only 0.27-4.17% tampered pixels vs 10-30% in natural-image benchmarks. Recalibrating the decision threshold using just N=10 labeled in-domain images recovered 39-55% of the oracle F1 gap. Practically: whatever scorer you use (deep model, ELA energy, classifier probability), fit its flag threshold on a small held-out set of YOUR legitimate + tampered samples instead of using paper defaults.
  - _signal:_ Not a detector itself but the highest-leverage way to reduce false positives across every ML/forensic module — directly serves TrustyFile's minimize-false-positives goal. Also implies you should keep raw continuous scores in ModuleResult and calibrate at the scoring.py layer.
  - _local:_ Trivial: sklearn threshold sweep / isotonic or Platt calibration on ~10-50 sample documents per document type. Zero runtime cost.
  - _sources:_ https://arxiv.org/html/2603.01433v2
- **One-class anomaly detection on legitimate-document features (legitimacy confirmation)** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ Train IsolationForest or One-Class SVM on feature vectors extracted from known-legitimate documents (per issuer/template or globally): font list + counts, producer/creator strings, page geometry, text-block layout positions, image count/DPI, color statistics. At analysis time, a document deep inside the learned 'normal' manifold earns a trust bonus; an outlier gets a soft flag. This is the ML formalization of your planned Module I template matching, and pairs with it: template match confirms identity, one-class model confirms statistical normality.
  - _signal:_ Primarily a POSITIVE legitimacy signal (goal 2): 'this EDF invoice looks like the 500 EDF invoices we know'. Weak as a fraud detector on its own (fraudsters copy templates well), so use asymmetrically: inliers gain trust points, outliers only get low-severity flags. FP risk low if used that way; high if outlier=fraud.
  - _local:_ Fully local, sklearn only, model <1MB, instant inference. The hard part is curating a legitimate corpus per template — you already plan a templates/ directory, so store feature vectors beside each template.json.
  - _sources:_ https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0957417421017164, https://medium.com/@dasarimeenakshi/invoice-anomaly-detection-using-ml-5dc35e47c497
- **Screenshot / recapture detection (classical frequency + resolution heuristics, optional tiny classifier)** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Detect documents that are screenshots or photos-of-screens rather than originals: check page image dimensions against common screen resolutions and 72/96 dpi, FFT of the page for periodic moire/subpixel peaks, absence of any text layer combined with perfectly axis-aligned uniform background, and EXIF software tags. Optionally train the classical-features sklearn classifier (technique above) with a 'recaptured' class.
  - _signal:_ A screenshot-of-an-invoice PDF is a classic laundering step that destroys metadata and font evidence — flagging it is a valuable medium-severity signal that also explains WHY other modules went quiet (low confidence). FP risk moderate: legitimate users do email screenshots; keep severity medium and wording neutral.
  - _local:_ Fully local: numpy FFT + Pillow + existing PyMuPDF extraction; <0.5s/page. Extends your existing Module F screenshot checks with a frequency-domain test.
  - _sources:_ https://www.techrxiv.org/users/908787/articles/1282650/master/file/data/Fraud_Detection_Survey_TechRxiv/Fraud_Detection_Survey_TechRxiv.pdf
- **Public datasets pipeline for training and (crucially) false-positive regression testing** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ Assemble an evaluation harness from public corpora: DocTamper (170k doc images; Kaggle mirror, password via academic email, NON-COMMERCIAL license), T-SROIE (462 tampered receipts), Find it! ICPR 2018 (1,969 real French receipts with forgeries — closest to your use case), Find it again! ICDAR 2023 (988 SROIE receipts, 163 realistically forged), FSTS (294k images incl. 16,750 real human tampering instances, GitHub), SIDTD + FantasyID (forged EU ID documents — matches your French ID document use case). Run every TrustyFile module over the legitimate halves to measure false-positive rates and over forged halves for recall, per document type.
  - _signal:_ Not a detector — it is how you prove signal strength and FP rates instead of guessing. The legitimate subsets are the most valuable part for your minimize-false-positives goal. Watch licenses: DocTamper and several others are non-commercial/research-only — fine for internal evaluation and calibration, risky for training a commercial product's shipped weights (prefer self-synthesized training data, evaluate on public sets).
  - _local:_ Datasets are downloads (respect the org rule: keep them private, do not re-upload); harness is pytest + pandas. Storage a few GB; use samples/ conventions already in the project. No cloud services involved.
  - _sources:_ https://github.com/qcf-568/DocTamper, https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/dinmkeljiame/doctamper, https://hal.science/hal-02316399/, https://l3i-share.univ-lr.fr/2023Finditagain/index.html
- **ForensicHub / IMDLBenCo as research codebase (evaluation only, not deployment)** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ IMDLBenCo (pip install imdlbenco, NeurIPS'24) and its extension ForensicHub provide unified implementations, checkpoints (Google Drive links) and metrics for 8+ IMDL models plus the 4 document-specific models (DTD, FFDN, CAFTB-Net, TIFDM). Use it offline to benchmark candidate models on your own sample documents before committing to any, rather than integrating a model blind.
  - _signal:_ De-risks the ML decision: their own tables show document-specific models are heavy (67-89M params, 272-453 GFLOPs) and generalize poorly, while giving you a harness to verify claims. No direct end-user signal.
  - _local:_ pip: imdlbenco (PyTorch; CPU works for inference but slow — this is a workbench, not the production box). Run on a dev laptop, not the 1-2GB cloud host.
  - _sources:_ https://pypi.org/project/imdlbenco/, https://github.com/scu-zjz/IMDLBenCo, https://github.com/scu-zjz/forensichub, https://arxiv.org/html/2406.10580v1

---

### RESEARCH — Open-source tools & academic literature on PDF/document fraud detection — borrowable, fully-local techniques for TrustyFile

**Key insights:**
- The cheapest, highest-yield upgrades for TrustyFile are file-structure forensics: revision-chain counting is ~100 lines of Python, and rolling back to previous versions to diff WHAT changed (amounts, IBANs) turns a vague 'file was modified' flag into a critical, explainable verdict — this is exactly what Inscribe sells as 'Document X-Ray' and it is fully replicable locally with PyMuPDF.
- Absence of tamper evidence is not evidence of authenticity: Ghostscript re-printing, print-to-PDF, or any full rewrite flattens revision history, resets metadata and produces one clean xref. Structure checks must return 'inconclusive' for such files (the htpbe.tech writeup is explicit about this), which is why legitimacy-confirmation signals matter as much as tamper detection.
- The best positive-trust signals are all locally verifiable: pyHanko validates digital signatures (incl. EU trust lists) in-process and replaces the EU DSS API in the roadmap; Factur-X embedded XML cross-checked against visible text is near-conclusive both ways and is tailor-made for French invoices under the 2026 e-invoicing mandate; python-stdnum validates SIRET/VAT/IBAN checksums with zero network.
- Metadata can lie, but style cannot easily: cross-layer consistency (Info dict vs XMP vs trailer /ID vs xmpMM:History) and producer coding-style fingerprinting (arXiv 2103.02702, 74–100% producer identification from file syntax alone) catch fraudsters who cleaned the obvious metadata — deliberate spoofing detected this way deserves higher severity than any single passive flag.
- Benchmarks exist to calibrate against: the Find it! ICPR 2018 contest (1,969 French receipts) and the ICDAR 2023 forged-receipt dataset show no single method is reliable (subtle price edits ~65% detection), that combined text+image ensembles win, and that image-forensics methods (ELA especially) have high false-positive rates when applied ungated — matching TrustyFile's weighted multi-module design and its minimize-false-positives goal.
- Commercial vendors' published methodology (Resistant AI's '500+ checks', Inscribe's detectors) decomposes into exactly five replicable families: metadata consistency, font/span anomalies, pixel-level image forensics, revision-history extraction, and cross-document template-reuse detection; their strongest differentiator — serial-fraud detection via near-duplicate template matching (98.3% claimed) — only needs perceptual hashing plus a local corpus.
- False-positive discipline that the sources agree on: legitimate signing/annotation workflows create incremental updates (score additive-only revisions low); legitimate users run invoices through online converters (flag, don't condemn); ELA is only meaningful on DCTDecode-origin images; copy-move detection on text drowns in repeated-glyph matches unless patches larger than a glyph including background are required (Abramova & Böhme); repeated fonts across zones of an invoice are normal — compare within lines/columns only.
- Tooling verdicts from the survey: adopt pikepdf (bundles qpdf, wheels, no apt), vendor Didier Stevens's public-domain pdfid.py, borrow algorithms from sherloq (Python/OpenCV reference implementations of ELA/ghost/noise/copy-move), treat veraPDF as an optional Java-based add-on; peepdf is abandoned/buggy and OPAF is dead (Google Code archive) — skip both. pdfresurrect is C but its /Prev-chain logic is trivially reimplemented in Python.
- Benford's-law digit analysis, though standard in the invoice-fraud accounting literature, is statistically meaningless on a single document — only consider it if TrustyFile ever analyzes batches of invoices from one vendor.
- Rough per-document budget on a small cloud box if all techniques run: structure+metadata+fonts+checksums <0.5 s; signature validation ~0.5 s; rollback diff and image forensics 1–3 s per page/image; copy-move is the outlier (0.5–2 s/page) — gate heavy pixel modules on triggers from the cheap structural modules to stay within a few seconds.

**Techniques:**
- **Incremental-update / revision-chain forensics (xref + %%EOF + /Prev walk, ghost objects, trailing bytes)** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Scan raw bytes for every %%EOF and startxref, then walk the /Prev chain backward from the last xref section: each node is one save session, so update_chain_length = xref_count - 1 revisions after creation. Additionally flag: objects with generation numbers > 0 or xref entries marked 'f' (freed = deleted content), objects present in the file body but absent from the final xref (ghost/orphaned objects whose old content is still recoverable), and any bytes after the final %%EOF (pdfid's -e option counts these). pdfresurrect (C, in Debian repos) and Inscribe's product both build on exactly this; it is ~100 lines of pure Python over the raw bytes, or use pikepdf to enumerate objects.
  - _signal:_ Catches any post-creation edit that was saved incrementally (Acrobat, many editors default to this). Multiple revisions on an 'original' invoice is a medium-strength flag; ghost objects containing old amounts are near-conclusive. FP risk is real: legitimate signing (DocuSign), annotation and form-fill workflows also append incremental updates, and Linearized PDFs legitimately contain 2 xref sections — score additive-only updates low. Crucially, absence of revisions proves nothing (Ghostscript/print-to-PDF flattening erases history), so a clean chain must yield 'inconclusive', not 'legitimate'.
  - _local:_ Pure Python over bytes (regex + manual xref parse) or pikepdf (pip pikepdf, bundles qpdf, wheels for all platforms, no apt deps). <50 ms and <50 MB RAM per document.
  - _sources:_ https://htpbe.tech/blog/pdf-xref-table-forensics, https://github.com/enferex/pdfresurrect, https://forensicdiscovery.expert/blog/document-history-how-pdf-saves-reveal-the-past/, https://arxiv.org/abs/2003.10546
- **Version rollback + semantic diff (local 'Document X-Ray')** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ For each detected revision, truncate the file at that revision's %%EOF and open the truncated bytes with fitz.open(stream=...) — this reconstructs each historical version of the document. Extract text (and optionally render pixmaps) from each version and diff consecutive versions: report exactly which words/amounts/dates changed, and pixel-diff renders to localize visual changes. This is the technique Inscribe markets as 'Document X-Ray' and the NDSS 2021 Shadow-Attacks paper's PDF-Detector uses (compare document to its pre-signature version by stripping incremental updates).
  - _signal:_ Upgrades 'this file was edited' into 'the total changed from 1 025 € to 2 025 €' — a smoking gun, justifying a critical flag and a killer UI feature. Very low FP when the diff shows substitution of amounts/dates/IBANs; additive-only diffs (new signature field, annotation) are benign and should be auto-downgraded, which is itself the FP mitigation.
  - _local:_ PyMuPDF only (pip pymupdf, already in stack). Text diff via stdlib difflib; render-diff via fitz pixmaps + numpy. ~0.5–2 s per document for a handful of revisions; RAM proportional to page renders (~100–300 MB peak at 150 dpi).
  - _sources:_ https://www.ndss-symposium.org/wp-content/uploads/ndss2021_1B-4_24117_paper.pdf, https://www.inscribe.ai/fraud-detection/document-fraud, https://forensicdiscovery.expert/blog/document-history-how-pdf-saves-reveal-the-past/
- **Trailer /ID + XMP cross-consistency and xmpMM:History parsing** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Parse three metadata layers and cross-check them: (1) trailer /ID array — ID[0] is permanent, ID[1] changes on every save, so ID[0] != ID[1] means the file was re-saved after creation; (2) XMP packet (fitz Document.get_xml_metadata(), parse with stdlib ElementTree) — xmpMM:DocumentID vs xmpMM:InstanceID mismatch means re-saves, and xmpMM:History is a literal edit trail listing softwareAgent + timestamp + action for each save (e.g. 'Adobe Photoshop ... saved'); (3) compare XMP xmp:CreateDate/ModifyDate/CreatorTool against the Info-dict CreationDate/ModDate/Creator — editors that spoof one layer usually forget the other.
  - _signal:_ xmpMM:History naming Photoshop/Illustrator/Acrobat on an invoice, or Info-vs-XMP date/tool disagreement, is a high-severity flag (disagreement implies deliberate or tool-induced metadata rewriting). FP: moderate — many pipelines legitimately re-save (archival, mail gateways), and lots of cheap generators emit no XMP at all, so missing XMP must be neutral. Matching, coherent three-layer metadata is also a mild positive legitimacy signal.
  - _local:_ PyMuPDF + stdlib xml. Zero extra deps, <10 ms per document.
  - _sources:_ https://www.meridiandiscovery.com/articles/pdf-forensic-analysis-xmp-metadata/, https://www.giac.org/paper/gmon/663/detection-malicious-documents-utilizing-xmp-identifiers/143772
- **Producer 'coding-style' fingerprinting to unmask spoofed metadata** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ Every PDF generator has a recognizable syntactic style independent of the Producer string: xref table vs xref stream, object numbering order, dictionary key ordering, whitespace/delimiter habits, stream-length conventions, whether Info precedes the body, comment lines, etc. The arXiv paper 'Robust PDF Files Forensics Using Coding Style' built 192 such rules for 11 producers and identified the true generator with 74% overall (100% for some producers) on 500k PDFs. Implement a scoped version: hand-write style rules for the ~10 producers that matter for invoices (Word, LibreOffice, Chrome/wkhtmltopdf print, LaTeX, iText, ReportLab, Acrobat, InDesign, iLovePDF/smallpdf outputs), then compare the inferred producer against the claimed /Producer.
  - _signal:_ Claimed-vs-actual producer mismatch means the metadata was deliberately edited — one of the few checks that catches a fraudster who cleaned the metadata, and deliberate deception warrants a critical flag. FP: medium — post-processors (compressors, mail gateways, printers) legitimately rewrite files while keeping the original Producer string, so only flag confident rule hits and keep an allowlist of known post-processing chains.
  - _local:_ Pure Python over raw bytes + pikepdf for object walks. No system deps, <100 ms. Main cost is building/maintaining the rule set, not runtime.
  - _sources:_ https://arxiv.org/abs/2103.02702
- **pdfid-style keyword triage + pikepdf/qpdf structural integrity check** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Borrow Didier Stevens's pdfid approach (public-domain, single-file Python, vendorable): count occurrences of /JS, /JavaScript, /AA, /OpenAction, /Launch, /EmbeddedFile, /XFA, /URI, /SubmitForm and hex-obfuscated names (#6A#61...) directly in the bytes — obfuscated keywords are themselves a red flag. Complement with pikepdf.open() + Pdf.check() (qpdf's --check engine): it reports xref damage, recovered/repaired tables, stream-length mismatches and bad object offsets. pdfalyzer (pip pdfalyzer) additionally walks the object tree and runs YARA rules over decoded streams if you want deeper triage; peepdf is abandoned/buggy — do not adopt it.
  - _signal:_ JavaScript or launch actions inside an invoice is highly anomalous (fraud kits and phishing lures, plus glyph-remap tricks that show humans one text and parsers another). A file whose xref had to be 'repaired' was produced or modified by a broken tool — weak-to-medium tamper signal. FP: medium for structural errors (plenty of legitimate generators emit sloppy PDFs — flag, lower confidence, don't condemn); low for JS/Launch in the invoice context.
  - _local:_ pip pikepdf (bundles qpdf, no apt needed); pdfid.py vendored (stdlib only); optional pip pdfalyzer + yara-python. All <200 ms, tiny RAM.
  - _sources:_ https://blog.didierstevens.com/programs/pdf-tools/, https://github.com/michelcrypt4d4mus/pdfalyzer, https://bitsgalore.org/2021/09/06/pdf-processing-and-analysis-with-open-source-tools.html
- **Local digital-signature validation with pyHanko + post-signature change detection (shadow-attack aware)** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ pyHanko (pip 'pyHanko') validates PDF signatures entirely in-process: cryptographic integrity, certificate chain to configurable trust roots, ByteRange coverage, and DocMDP modification policy — it explicitly reports whether incremental updates after the signature stay within the allowed change set. With the [etsi] extra it can use the EU Trusted Lists as roots (the list download sends nothing about the document and is cached; fully offline operation works with pre-provisioned roots). This replaces TrustyFile's current EU DSS API dependency. For shadow attacks, do what the NDSS PDF-Detector does: strip all incremental updates after the signed revision, render both versions, and diff — a valid signature plus visible-content change after signing is the attack.
  - _signal:_ A valid qualified signature covering the whole file is the strongest legitimacy-confirmation signal available (+trust bonus); an invalid signature, a signature covering only part of the file, or disallowed post-signature changes is a high/critical flag. FP: low if you distinguish 'no signature' (neutral — most invoices are unsigned) from 'broken signature' (bad); untrusted-but-intact chains (internal CAs) should be a soft warning only.
  - _local:_ pip pyHanko + pyhanko-certvalidator (pure Python + cryptography wheels). No apt deps. ~100–500 ms per signature; EUTL cold-cache fetch is minutes but one-off and cacheable — or ship pinned roots for full offline.
  - _sources:_ https://docs.pyhanko.eu/en/latest/lib-guide/validation.html, https://www.ndss-symposium.org/wp-content/uploads/ndss2021_1B-4_24117_paper.pdf, https://pypi.org/project/pyHanko/
- **Factur-X / ZUGFeRD embedded-XML vs visible-text cross-check** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ French/EU hybrid e-invoices are PDF/A-3 files with a machine-readable CII XML (factur-x.xml) embedded as an attachment with AFRelationship=Alternative, declared in XMP. Extract the XML (pip 'factur-x', or fitz embedded-file API), validate it against the EN 16931 XSD, then cross-check its fields — total TTC/HT, VAT amounts and rates, IBAN, SIREN/SIRET, invoice number, dates, seller name — against the text extracted from the visible layer. A fraudster who edits the visible amount almost never updates the embedded XML.
  - _signal:_ XML-vs-visible mismatch on an amount or IBAN is near-conclusive tampering (critical). Presence + XSD-valid + fully consistent Factur-X is a strong legitimacy bonus, increasingly common as France's B2B e-invoicing mandate rolls out from 2026 — this is the single best positive-trust check for the project's French-invoice audience. FP: low; main pitfall is OCR/extraction noise when matching numbers, so compare normalized amounts, not raw strings.
  - _local:_ pip factur-x (deps: lxml, pypdf) or pure PyMuPDF embedded-file extraction + lxml XSD validation. Fully offline, <200 ms.
  - _sources:_ https://pypi.org/project/factur-x/, https://www.pdflib.com/pdf-knowledge-base/zugferd-and-factur-x/
- **Font-subset pairing and span-level font anomaly detection** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Beyond counting fonts, exploit subset naming: embedded subset fonts are named ABCDEF+BaseName; two different subset prefixes of the same base font (ABCDEF+Arial and GHIJKL+Arial) mean text was added in a separate tool/session — original generators produce one subset per font. Via fitz page.get_text('dict'), inspect per-span font name, size and flags: flag mid-line/mid-word font switches, fractional size deviations within a visual block (genuine rows at 9.0 pt, the inserted row at 9.5 pt), a non-embedded system font amid otherwise-embedded fonts, and editor-default fonts (Helvetica base-14, Roboto) appearing only in one span. Commercial vendors (Resistant AI, Inscribe, Precisa) all list this among their highest-yield checks.
  - _signal:_ Dual subsets of the same base font is a strong, targeted edit indicator (high severity) with low FP; mid-line size/font changes are medium signals — invoices legitimately mix fonts across zones (logo vs body), so restrict comparisons to within-line/within-table-column. This catches exactly the 'edited an amount in Acrobat/online editor' scenario TrustyFile targets.
  - _local:_ PyMuPDF only (already in stack); page.get_fonts() + get_text('dict'). <100 ms, no new deps.
  - _sources:_ https://precisa.in/blog/bank-statements-document-tampering-detection/, https://resistant.ai/products/documents, https://www.inscribe.ai/fraud-detection/document-fraud
- **Character-geometry checks: baseline, alignment and spacing of amounts** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ pdfplumber exposes exact per-character bounding boxes. Within each text line, verify all characters share the same baseline y (tolerance ~0.3 pt) and that inter-character advance is consistent with the font's metrics; within amount/date columns, verify right-edge alignment across rows. Digits retyped over an erased region in an editor land at slightly wrong baselines/offsets, and OCR-graph-feature research (arXiv 2009.05158) plus the altered-text literature confirm geometric misalignment is a dominant tell for inserted text in documents.
  - _signal:_ A digit sitting 0.5 pt off its line's baseline inside a total is a high-severity, well-localized flag. FP: medium — justified text, superscripts (currency symbols, ordinals) and table renderers with per-cell positioning create legitimate offsets; only apply within homogeneous runs (digits of one number, cells of one column) and whitelist superscript flags from the font data.
  - _local:_ pip pdfplumber (already in stack) + numpy. Pure CPU, ~100–300 ms/page.
  - _sources:_ https://arxiv.org/abs/2009.05158, https://hal.science/hal-02316399/, https://lokeshkvn.github.io/files/ICPRAI_handwriting_compressed.pdf
- **Copy-move (clone) detection tuned for document images** (effort: large)
  - _how:_ Render pages (or extract embedded scans), detect duplicated regions via ORB/SIFT keypoint matching plus dense block matching (DCT/PCA blocks with lexicographic sort), clustering matched pairs by consistent translation vector. Critical document-specific adaptation from Abramova & Böhme: identical glyphs legitimately repeat everywhere in text, so naive CMFD drowns in false positives — require matched regions larger than a single glyph (multi-character patches), require identical noise/background around the patch, and ignore matches aligned with the text grid unless the surrounding background also matches pixel-perfectly (Paint-style paste copies the background too, which real repeated words don't).
  - _signal:_ Catches the classic 'copy a digit/price from elsewhere in the doc and paste it' fraud (TrustyFile test idea #9/#10). Strong when a >glyph-sized region including background matches exactly; FP high if untuned (repeated words, logos, dotted lines), moderate after the document-specific constraints. Best used to localize evidence, not as a standalone verdict.
  - _local:_ opencv-python + numpy (SIFT is patent-free and in mainline OpenCV now). ~0.5–2 s per page at 150–200 dpi, ~200–400 MB RAM. No apt deps beyond opencv wheels.
  - _sources:_ https://informationsecurity.uibk.ac.at/pdfs/Abramova2016_CMFD_ScannedDocuments_EI.pdf, https://github.com/GuidoBartoli/sherloq
- **ELA + JPEG-ghost + local-noise inconsistency suite for embedded images and scanned pages** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ For JPEG-origin content: Error Level Analysis (re-save at quality ~90 with Pillow, amplify per-pixel absolute difference — edited regions re-compress differently) and JPEG ghosts (re-save at a sweep of qualities 50–100; a pasted region previously compressed at quality q shows a difference minimum at q while the rest doesn't — Krawetz's Black Hat whitepaper gives the exact recipe). For any raster: local noise maps (residual after median/wavelet denoise, computed per 32×32 block) reveal pasted rectangles with uniform or mismatched noise versus the naturally noisy scan background — this matches TrustyFile's planned paste-detection and the methods that scored best on the Find it!/ICDAR receipt-forgery benchmarks. Sherloq (Python/OpenCV, GPL) contains reference implementations of all three to borrow from.
  - _signal:_ Localizes screenshot-and-paste and Photoshop edits on scanned invoices. ELA alone is notoriously noisy — high FP on high-contrast edges, text, and anything not JPEG (PNG, flattened, re-encoded by the PDF producer), so gate it: only run when the embedded stream is DCTDecode, report as supporting evidence, and require noise-map agreement before raising severity. Noise inconsistency is medium signal / medium FP; ghosts are strong when a clear quality minimum appears.
  - _local:_ Pillow + numpy + opencv-python; optionally PyWavelets (pip pywavelets) for wavelet noise. ~0.3–1 s per image; fine on a small box. Extract original JPEG bytes losslessly from the PDF via fitz xref stream (filter DCTDecode) — never re-render before analysis.
