  - _sources:_ https://blackhat.com/presentations/bh-dc-08/Krawetz/Whitepaper/bh-dc-08-krawetz-WP.pdf, https://github.com/GuidoBartoli/sherloq, https://l3i.univ-larochelle.fr/app/uploads/sites/12/2024/05/ICDAR_2023_Find_it_again.pdf, https://29a.ch/2017/02/05/jpeg-forensics-in-forensically
- **JPEG quantization-table ballistics on embedded images** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Extract raw DCTDecode streams from the PDF (fitz, lossless) and read their quantization tables (PIL Image.quantization). Compare against known tables: libjpeg-standard tables scaled per quality, camera-vendor tables, and Photoshop's distinctive 'Save for Web' tables (Farid's 'Digital Image Ballistics from JPEG Quantization' and the DFRWS 2008 software-table database catalog these). An image inside a 'bank-generated' PDF whose quant table matches Photoshop was processed by an editor; two embedded images with wildly different tables suggest compositing from different sources.
  - _signal:_ Photoshop/GIMP-signature quant tables in an official document's logo or scan is a medium-to-high flag with low FP (tables are deterministic per software). Also a mild legitimacy signal when all images share one generator-consistent table. Limitations: only applies to JPEG-embedded images; PDF producers that re-encode images destroy the original table (then the table fingerprints the producer instead — still useful for cross-checking /Producer).
  - _local:_ Pillow + PyMuPDF, plus a small bundled table database (build from the DFRWS paper + your own samples). <50 ms per image, trivial RAM.
  - _sources:_ https://farid.berkeley.edu/downloads/publications/tr06a.pdf, https://dfrws.org/sites/default/files/session-files/2008_USA_paper-using_jpeg_quantization_tables_to_identify_imagery_processed_by_software.pdf
- **Standards-conformance validation as a legitimacy signal (veraPDF / Arlington model, pikepdf fallback)** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Run veraPDF (open-source, Open Preservation Foundation + PDF Association) locally against the file: it validates claimed PDF/A or PDF/UA conformance rule-by-rule and, via the Arlington PDF Model, checks every object against the PDF grammar far more thoroughly than qpdf. Use it in two ways: (1) a document whose XMP claims PDF/A-3 (e.g. a Factur-X invoice) but fails validation was post-processed after generation; (2) a passing PDF/A file from a professional producer earns a trust bonus, because casual editors rarely preserve conformance.
  - _signal:_ Claimed-but-failed conformance is a medium tamper/post-processing signal; verified conformance + coherent metadata is one of the few honest positive signals for unsigned invoices. FP: low for the claim-vs-reality check (the claim is explicit in XMP); do not penalize files that never claimed conformance.
  - _local:_ veraPDF is Java: apt default-jre-headless + the veraPDF CLI zip; ~1–3 s and 300–500 MB JVM per run — acceptable but the heaviest dependency listed, so make it optional. Lightweight fallback: pikepdf/qpdf --check plus your own XMP-claim parser (small).
  - _sources:_ https://docs.verapdf.org/cli/validation/, https://bitsgalore.org/2021/09/06/pdf-processing-and-analysis-with-open-source-tools.html, https://openpreservation.org/blogs/validation-ok-they-said-fixing-the-rendering-of-a-so-called-valid-pdf/
- **Perceptual-hash serial-fraud and known-good template matching** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ Render page 1 at fixed dpi, compute perceptual hashes (pHash/dHash via pip imagehash) plus a coarse layout signature (grid of text-block bounding boxes from fitz). Maintain two local databases: (a) previously analyzed documents — near-duplicate hits (small Hamming distance) with different amounts/names indicate a reused fraud template ('template farms'; Resistant AI reports 98.3% of serial fraud caught this way without prior template knowledge); (b) known-good vendor templates (TrustyFile Module I) — verify logo position via template matching/SSIM (opencv matchTemplate, skimage.metrics.structural_similarity), font set, and field positions against the reference.
  - _signal:_ Same-template-different-payee across submissions is a very strong serial-fraud signal (the top detection mechanism claimed by commercial vendors); matching a known-good template earns a trust bonus, and claiming to be EDF/Amazon while mismatching the reference layout is high severity. FP: template families legitimately vary by year/region — version your references and use tolerances on positions; near-duplicate detection needs a corpus, so value grows with usage.
  - _local:_ pip imagehash, Pillow, opencv-python, scikit-image; hashes stored in SQLite — all local (mind the org rule: the document corpus stays private). ~200–500 ms per document.
  - _sources:_ https://resistant.ai/products/documents, https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/financial-services/resistant-ai-document-forensics-built-on-google-cloud-document-ai, https://github.com/Stirling-Tools/Stirling-PDF
- **Offline identifier-checksum and invoice-arithmetic validation (python-stdnum)** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Validate every extracted identifier with pure-math checksums, no network: SIREN/SIRET (Luhn), French VAT key (FR key = (12 + 3*(SIREN mod 97)) mod 97), IBAN mod-97, EAN, and dozens more via pip python-stdnum (stdnum.fr.siret, stdnum.eu.vat, stdnum.iban). Then check invoice arithmetic: line items sum to subtotal, VAT amount equals rate × base for legal French rates (20/10/5.5/2.1 %), TTC = HT + TVA, and dates are ordered (issue ≤ due; service period plausible). Fabricated invoices routinely contain checksum-invalid SIRETs or VAT math that doesn't close.
  - _signal:_ A checksum-invalid SIRET/VAT/IBAN on an invoice is high severity with essentially zero FP (it can never be valid); all-checksums-valid plus arithmetic that closes is a solid legitimacy confirmation that needs no external API — complements (and mostly replaces) the INSEE lookup. FP on arithmetic: rounding conventions and multi-rate invoices need per-line rate grouping and ±0.01/line tolerance.
  - _local:_ pip python-stdnum (pure Python, tiny); arithmetic is stdlib + your existing extraction. <10 ms. The weak link is extraction quality — validate only high-confidence extracted strings.
  - _sources:_ https://pypi.org/project/python-stdnum/, https://www.acfe.com/-/media/images/acfe/products/publication/self-study-cpe/workbook/using-benfords-law/using_benfords_law_sample.pdf

---

### RESEARCH — Local image forensics for detecting edited regions in document images (scans/screenshots of invoices and IDs)

**Key insights:**
- Almost all classical image-forensics methods assume photographic content; invoices/IDs are flat text, which breaks them in two specific ways: ELA lights up every glyph edge (text IS high-contrast edges), and copy-move detectors match legitimately repeated letterforms. Every module needs document-aware gating: variance/flatness masks, glyph-scale minimum-region filters, and background-only analysis for noise methods.
- Gate all pixel forensics on document type first: born-digital (vector) PDF pages must be excluded — they legitimately have zero noise, pure-255 white, and no JPEG grid, so every noise/white-point/grid method false-positives on them. Run image forensics only on embedded raster images and full-page scans/photos; a cheap classifier (page covered by one large image + global noise level + no extractable text layer) decides.
- Extract embedded JPEGs from the PDF losslessly (PyMuPDF raw image streams), never by re-rendering pages with get_pixmap(): re-rendering destroys the JPEG compression history that ELA, ghost, ADQ/DQ, ZERO and quantization-table analysis all depend on.
- For the minimize-false-positives requirement, prefer methods with a-contrario statistical false-alarm control (NFA): ZERO for JPEG-grid misalignment and Noisesniffer for noise anomalies. These are the only classical detectors designed to be fully automatic with bounded false alarms; threshold-based heatmap methods (ELA, BAG, NOI1) should only corroborate, never flag alone.
- The compression-history methods form a complementary matrix: ADQ/DQ works when the final file is a re-saved JPEG; JPEG Ghost when the pasted part was a LOWER-quality JPEG; ZERO works even after PNG conversion (pixel-domain grid traces); quantization-table fingerprinting works on any surviving JPEG stream. Run all cheap ones and require spatial agreement between at least two maps before raising severity.
- Quick wins for TrustyFile's Module H, in order: (1) white-point/zero-variance pasted-box detector (pure OpenCV, catches the Paint-edit test cases, near-zero FP on scans), (2) quantization-table quality estimation + producer fingerprinting (Pillow-only, doubles as a legitimacy signal), (3) pyIFD (Apache-2.0, pip from GitHub) for ADQ1/ADQ2/GHOST/BLK/NOI1 in one dependency, (4) ZERO via subprocess (AGPL isolation) or PhotoHolmes.
- Legitimacy confirmation is achievable, not just tamper detection: a single consistent JPEG grid over the whole page (ZERO, no anomalies), standard scanner/camera quantization tables with high NSE fit, a globally consistent intensity-dependent noise model (Noisesniffer clean), and no double-quantization signature together support 'compressed once, untouched since acquisition' — worth a positive trust bonus in scoring.
- PRNU is a dead end for single anonymous documents (needs dozens of reference images from the known device; destroyed by scan/recompress pipelines) — exclude it and document why.
- Signal-strength reality check from benchmarks: everything degrades sharply after recompression/resizing (e.g., WhatsApp-forwarded invoice photos); expect silent misses, not false alarms, from the JPEG-domain methods in that case — so absence of forensic findings must lower module confidence, not raise the trust score.
- Deep models (TruFor, CAT-Net via PhotoHolmes; DocTamper for character-level document edits) are the accuracy ceiling and run locally on CPU in seconds, but weights carry research-only/non-commercial licenses — a licensing review is required before shipping them in a product.

**Techniques:**
- **Calibrated Error Level Analysis (ELA)** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Resave the JPEG at a known quality, subtract from the original, and amplify the per-pixel absolute difference: regions with a different compression history compress differently and stand out. 'Done right' means calibrating the resave quality to the image's actual quality instead of a fixed 90: read the quantization tables (Pillow `img.quantization` or jpeglib), estimate the original quality by least-squares matching against IJG standard tables scaled with Kornblum's formula (S=5000/Q for Q<50, 200-2Q for Q>=50), then resave at that estimated quality (or estimated-5) and compute block-level (8x8) mean error rather than per-pixel. Report a Nash-Sutcliffe fit coefficient so you know when the table is non-standard and the estimate is unreliable.
  - _signal:_ Catches regions pasted in from a source with different JPEG history (edited amount boxes, swapped photos on IDs) when the final file is a JPEG. Signal strength: weak-to-moderate and notoriously subjective; IEEE evaluation found it works for splicing/retouching but fails after recompression, rotation, or filtering which homogenize artifacts. False-positive risk on documents is HIGH: ELA inherently lights up high-contrast edges, and invoices are wall-to-wall text edges; solid white background reads near-black while every glyph reads bright. Use only as a visual/aggregate feature (per-block error variance in background regions), never as a standalone flag. Meaningless on PNG screenshots or born-digital PDF pages.
  - _local:_ Trivially local: Pillow + NumPy only (already in stack). pyIFD ships an ELA implementation; sherloq has reference code. No system deps. Milliseconds-to-tens-of-ms per page, negligible RAM.
  - _sources:_ https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Error_level_analysis, https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/document/7412439/, https://bitsgalore.org/2024/10/30/jpeg-quality-estimation-using-simple-least-squares-matching-of-quantization-tables.html, https://www.infosecinstitute.com/resources/digital-forensics/error-level-analysis-detect-image-manipulation/
- **JPEG quantization-table quality estimation and producer fingerprinting (legitimacy signal)** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Extract the luma/chroma quantization tables from each embedded JPEG (Pillow `.quantization`, or jpeglib/jpegio for full access). Least-squares match against standard IJG tables to estimate quality Q and a fit score (NSE); non-standard tables can be matched against a small local database of known producers (Photoshop 'Save for Web', common scanner firmwares, iPhone camera, WhatsApp ~Q75, screenshot tools). Kornblum's classic paper shows software can be identified from its tables.
  - _signal:_ Dual use. Legitimacy: tables consistent with the claimed origin (e.g., a scanner's stock tables, single compression, high NSE) support 'untouched scan'. Fraud: a document claiming to be a raw scan but carrying Photoshop signature tables, or absurdly low quality (heavy re-save laundering), is suspicious. Strong, cheap, near-zero false-positive metadata-adjacent signal — but tables are absent if the fraudster exports to PNG or re-renders the PDF, and a matching table proves nothing by itself (easy to spoof).
  - _local:_ Pure local: Pillow only for tables; jpeglib (pip install jpeglib, bundles libjpeg, no apt deps) or jpegio for DCT-level access. Reference script: KBNLresearch/jpeg-quality-demo (jpegquality-lsm.py). Microseconds per image. Important: extract embedded JPEGs from the PDF losslessly with PyMuPDF (raw stream) — re-rendering the page destroys the compression history this and all JPEG-domain methods depend on.
  - _sources:_ https://bitsgalore.org/2024/10/30/jpeg-quality-estimation-using-simple-least-squares-matching-of-quantization-tables.html, https://github.com/KBNLresearch/jpeg-quality-demo, https://arxiv.org/pdf/1802.00992, https://jpeglib.readthedocs.io/en/latest/
- **JPEG Ghost detection (Farid)** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Resave the image at every quality in a sweep (e.g., Q=30..100 step 5) and compute per-block (16x16) normalized squared difference against the original. A region originally compressed at lower quality Qi than the rest exhibits a local minimum ('ghost') in the difference at Q=Qi while the rest keeps decreasing toward the final quality. Localize blocks whose difference-vs-Q curve has a distinct early minimum.
  - _signal:_ Catches spliced content that was previously saved at a LOWER JPEG quality than the host (pasted logos, lifted signature crops, numbers copied from another invoice photo). Moderate signal when the quality gap exists; blind to same-or-higher-quality inserts and destroyed by resizing. False positives on flat/saturated regions (their difference curves are degenerate) — mask out near-constant blocks. On documents, restrict to photographed/scanned pages.
  - _local:_ Pure Pillow/NumPy loop (~15 resaves — roughly 0.3-1 s per page image on a small VPS). pyIFD implements it (GHOST); sherloq has 'JPEG Ghost Maps'. No system deps, RAM trivial.
  - _sources:_ https://farid.berkeley.edu/downloads/publications/tifs09.pdf, https://github.com/AICoE/pyIFD, https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6839440/
- **Aligned double-JPEG / double-quantization detection (ADQ1, ADQ2/DQ, NADQ)** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ When an edited JPEG is re-saved, untouched 8x8 blocks are quantized twice (their DCT-coefficient histograms show periodic peaks/valleys — the double-quantization effect) while tampered blocks were re-rasterized and are only singly compressed. ADQ methods model per-block posterior probability of double compression from the DCT histograms (read coefficients directly with jpegio/jpeglib — do NOT decode to pixels first) and output a tamper-probability map where 'singly compressed' islands mark edits.
  - _signal:_ One of the strongest classical localizers when its precondition holds: final file is JPEG, saved exactly once after editing, no resize. Bianchi-Piva ADQ2/DQ scored among top classical methods in benchmarks (PhotoHolmes includes DQ for this reason). Catches Photoshop/GIMP edits to camera photos of documents and photo-substitution on IDs. Fails silently (no signal, not false alarms) on PNG output, resized images, or multiply-recompressed files; moderate FP on very flat content where histograms are starved — for invoices, gate on block having enough nonzero AC coefficients.
  - _local:_ pyIFD implements ADQ1/ADQ2/NADQ (pip install git+https://github.com/AICoE/pyIFD, Apache-2.0, pure Python); PhotoHolmes implements DQ (pip, PyTorch-based framework but DQ itself is classical). Needs jpegio/jpeglib for coefficient access. ~0.5-2 s per image, <500 MB RAM.
  - _sources:_ https://github.com/AICoE/pyIFD, https://arxiv.org/html/2412.14969v1, https://arxiv.org/pdf/2003.09393, https://medium.com/@trentonford/pyifd-python-based-image-forgery-detection-toolkit-6dc1214b0e51
- **ZERO: JPEG grid origin / grid-misalignment detection with a-contrario validation** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ JPEG quantization forces many DCT coefficients to zero on the 8x8 grid. ZERO counts, for each pixel neighborhood and each of the 64 possible grid origins, the number of zero DCT coefficients; the true grid origin maximizes zeros. Regions whose local grid origin differs from the global one (misaligned paste), or that show no grid at all (re-rendered/inpainted content), are flagged. Every detection carries a Number of False Alarms (NFA) from a-contrario theory, so thresholding NFA<1 makes the method fully automatic with mathematically controlled false alarms.
  - _signal:_ Excellent fit for TrustyFile's minimize-false-positives goal: detects copy-paste, splicing, and locally re-rendered regions in anything that was once JPEG (works even after conversion to PNG, since it reads pixel-domain traces). Also a legitimacy signal: a single consistent grid across the whole page with no anomalies supports 'unmodified since compression'. Blind when the whole image is recompressed at high quality after editing or resized (grid destroyed everywhere — reported as 'no grid', which is itself informative for a claimed scan).
  - _local:_ Reference implementation in C (github.com/tinankh/ZERO, AGPL-3.0 — call as subprocess to avoid license contamination, or use the Rust crate forgery-detection-zero) with a Python driver script; also implemented in PhotoHolmes (pip). Fast: well under 1 s per page, tiny RAM. IPOL paper includes certified source code.
  - _sources:_ https://www.ipol.im/pub/art/2021/390/, https://github.com/tinankh/ZERO, https://lib.rs/crates/forgery-detection-zero, https://arxiv.org/html/2412.14969v1
- **Blocking Artifact Grid (BAG/BLK) and CAGI grid-inconsistency analysis** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Extract the 8-pixel-periodic blocking grid left by JPEG using a second-order difference filter (BAG, Li et al.) and look for local grid discontinuities, or use CAGI which scores each block's alignment confidence against the global grid and adds content-aware filtering so that grid breaks explained by image content (strong edges) are discounted. Both output heatmaps of blocks violating the dominant grid.
  - _signal:_ Same underlying evidence as ZERO (grid misalignment from pasting/resampling) but older formulations without NFA guarantees — noisier, more manual-inspection-oriented. CAGI's content-aware step reduces but does not eliminate false positives on textured/edge-dense content, which documents are full of. Prefer ZERO; keep BLK/CAGI only as cheap corroboration since pyIFD already ships them.
  - _local:_ pyIFD implements BLK and CAGI in pure Python (Apache-2.0). ~1-3 s per image, low RAM. No system deps.
  - _sources:_ https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S1047320318301068, https://www.researchgate.net/publication/222146847_Passive_detection_of_doctored_JPEG_image_via_block_artifact_grid_extraction, https://github.com/AICoE/pyIFD, http://www.ipol.im/pub/art/2020/283/
- **Copy-move / clone detection — keypoint-based (SIFT/ORB) with document-specific filtering** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ Detect keypoints and descriptors (cv2.SIFT_create() — patent expired, in standard opencv-python; ORB as a faster fallback), match the image against itself with a generalized 2nd-nearest-neighbor test excluding trivially-near matches, then cluster matched pairs by consistent translation/affine transform (RANSAC or shift-vector histogram). A cluster of many matches sharing one transform reveals a duplicated region. For documents you MUST add filters: minimum displacement (> glyph size), minimum cluster area (> single character), and require dense contiguous support.
  - _signal:_ Directly catches the most common invoice fraud: a digit, amount, line item, stamp, or signature cloned from elsewhere in the same document, and duplicated ID photos. Keypoint methods survive rotation/scale of the pasted region. False-positive risk on text documents is EXTREME if unfiltered — every repeated letterform ('e' matching another 'e') is a legitimate self-similarity; Christlein et al.'s benchmark documents this repetitive-pattern failure mode. With glyph-scale filtering and word-or-larger cluster thresholds, precision becomes usable; treat any surviving cluster as high-severity.
  - _local:_ opencv-python only (already in stack). Reference recipes abound; the pimage package (pip install pimage) covers the block/PCA-based side. 0.5-3 s per page at ~150 dpi; keep working resolution <= ~2500 px to bound RAM (<1 GB).
  - _sources:_ https://arxiv.org/abs/1208.3665, https://arxiv.org/pdf/1612.03989, https://www5.cs.fau.de/research/software/copy-move-forgery-detection/, https://github.com/rnazali/pimage
- **Copy-move detection — block-based (overlapping-block DCT/PCA features)** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ Slide an overlapping window (e.g., 16x16), compute a robust low-dimensional feature per block (quantized DCT coefficients per Fridrich, or PCA projection per Popescu), lexicographically sort features, and flag pairs of near-identical blocks whose spatial offset vector recurs many times (shift-vector counting). Complements keypoints: works on smooth, low-texture areas where SIFT finds nothing — e.g., cloned patches of blank background used to erase a line item.
  - _signal:_ Catches exact or near-exact duplication including 'erasure by background cloning', which keypoint methods miss on featureless paper. Robust to mild JPEG/noise (DCT features). Fragile to rotation/scaling. FP risk on documents: large uniform white areas are all mutually identical — must exclude near-constant blocks (variance threshold) before matching, and require large same-shift clusters. CPU is the main cost (sorting millions of blocks) — downscale or restrict to regions of interest.
  - _local:_ pimage (pip install pimage, implements Fridrich robust-match and Popescu fast-PCA, pure Python) or a NumPy/OpenCV recipe. 2-10 s per page depending on resolution; a few hundred MB RAM. No system deps.
  - _sources:_ https://github.com/rnazali/pimage, https://arxiv.org/pdf/1308.5661, https://arxiv.org/abs/1208.3665
- **Local noise-variance inconsistency (wavelet-MAD, NOI family)** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Estimate local noise standard deviation on tiles (e.g., 64x64) using the median absolute deviation of the diagonal high-frequency wavelet subband: sigma = median(|HH1|)/0.6745 (Mahdian & Saic, NOI1). Alternative estimators: kurtosis-based (NOI2, Lyu), PCA-based. Segment tiles into consistent-noise groups; an island with significantly different sigma than its surroundings indicates content from a different source or local smoothing/inpainting.
  - _signal:_ Catches pasted regions with mismatched sensor/scan noise and airbrushed/blurred patches (noise too LOW is the classic edit signature). On scans this is genuinely useful in background regions: real scanner background carries uniform noise, a pasted white rectangle has none. FP risk: moderate-to-high naively — noise estimation confuses texture with noise, so text-dense tiles read as high-noise; JPEG compression flattens noise in flat areas causing spurious lows. Mitigate by analyzing background-classified tiles only and comparing against the page's own background statistics.
  - _local:_ pyIFD implements NOI1/NOI2/NOI4/NOI5 (pure Python, pip from GitHub); a from-scratch NOI1 is ~40 lines with PyWavelets (pip install PyWavelets) + NumPy. <1 s per page, trivial RAM.
  - _sources:_ https://www.ijcsit.com/docs/Volume%205/vol5issue06/ijcsit20140506155.pdf, https://cse.buffalo.edu/~siweilyu/papers/iccp12.pdf, https://github.com/AICoE/pyIFD
- **Noisesniffer — a-contrario noise-anomaly detection** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ Models how the acquisition pipeline shapes intensity-dependent noise across the whole image (noise curve as a function of intensity), then finds spatial regions whose noise statistics deviate from that global stochastic model. Candidate anomalies are grown region-wise and each detection is validated with a Number of False Alarms (NFA) threshold, making it fully automatic. Refined version published with certified code at IPOL 2024 (Gardella et al.).
  - _signal:_ Same fraud class as NOI methods (spliced or locally smoothed regions) but with statistical false-alarm control instead of an arbitrary threshold — the best-behaved noise method for a low-FP product. Detects 'noise too low for this intensity level here' which matches pasted flat boxes over scans well. Limits: weak on already heavily compressed images (noise mostly destroyed everywhere) and on born-digital content that legitimately has zero noise — gate by document type.
  - _local:_ Certified source code downloadable from the IPOL article (Python/C); also implemented in PhotoHolmes (pip install from github.com/photoholmes/photoholmes). Runs in seconds on CPU, <1 GB RAM.
  - _sources:_ https://www.ipol.im/pub/art/2024/462/, https://hal.science/hal-03243928, https://arxiv.org/html/2412.14969v1
- **White-point / background-flatness inconsistency (pasted-box detector)** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Real scanned or photographed paper background is off-white (e.g., 245-253) with per-pixel noise; a rectangle drawn or pasted by an editor is exactly RGB (255,255,255) (or one uniform value) with zero local variance. Implement: compute a local variance map (cv2.boxFilter on image and image^2), threshold near-zero-variance pixels that are also saturated-white, extract connected components (cv2.connectedComponentsWithStats), and flag axis-aligned rectangular components embedded inside otherwise-noisy background. Also compare the histogram peak of each candidate region against the page's global background peak (255 vs 249 mismatch).
  - _signal:_ Cheap and surprisingly effective against amateur invoice fraud (Paint/Photoshop white-box-over-amount edits) — the exact scenario in TrustyFile's test plan. Practitioner heuristic used by commercial doc-fraud vendors rather than an academic method. Near-zero FP on genuine scans/photos; but 100% FP on born-digital PDFs, where pure flat white is universal — MUST be gated on 'this page is a scan/photo' (detect via global noise level / OCR-vs-text-layer / image covering full page). Sophisticated forgers who clone real background instead of painting white evade it (the block copy-move detector covers that case).
  - _local:_ Pure OpenCV/NumPy, ~50 lines, milliseconds per page. No packages beyond the existing stack.
  - _sources:_ https://hawkeyeforensic.com/scanned-vs-original-the-invisible-clues-that-reveals-tampering/, https://www.koncile.ai/en/ressources/document-fraud-detection-software-3-methods, https://www.klippa.com/en/blog/information/document-fraud-detection-software/
- **Resampling / interpolation trace detection (Popescu-Farid p-map)** (effort: large)
  - _how:_ Interpolation (resize/rotate of a pasted region) makes pixels linear combinations of neighbors with a spatially periodic pattern. Fit a linear predictor per pixel with an EM algorithm to obtain a probability map of 'pixel is correlated with neighbors'; take the FFT of the p-map — resampled regions show characteristic spectral peaks whose positions encode the scaling factor. Faster variant (Kirchner): fixed linear predictor residue instead of EM.
  - _signal:_ In principle catches rescaled/rotated pastes even in PNG output. In practice one of the NOISIEST classical methods on documents: JPEG compression introduces its own 8-px periodicity that masquerades as resampling; halftone/dither patterns in printed-then-scanned documents alias badly; and whole-page rescaling (extremely common and legitimate in PDF workflows) lights up everything. Recommend implementing only as a low-weight corroborating signal or skipping for MVP.
  - _local:_ No maintained pip package; sherloq contains a Python 'Image Resampling' implementation (GPL-3, portable as reference). NumPy/SciPy only. EM version is slow (~5-20 s/page naive); Kirchner variant is ~1 s. 
  - _sources:_ https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/1411328.1411333, https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1155/2017/7130491, https://github.com/GuidoBartoli/sherloq, https://cs229.stanford.edu/proj2011/HoMaMeyer-ResamplingDetectionForDigitalImageForensics.pdf
- **PRNU sensor-fingerprint analysis — assessed as NOT feasible for this use case** (effort: large)
  - _how:_ Photo-Response Non-Uniformity is a per-device multiplicative sensor noise pattern; extract a noise residual (wavelet denoise, subtract) and correlate against a camera fingerprint built from dozens of known images from the same device; region-wise absence of the fingerprint localizes tampering.
  - _signal:_ Gold standard in law-enforcement camera attribution, but inapplicable to TrustyFile: you receive one image from an unknown device, never a set of reference images, so no fingerprint can be built; PRNU is also largely destroyed by scanner pipelines, recompression, screenshots, and PDF re-encoding. Equal-error rates vary wildly by sensor even in lab settings (0.2-23% in biometric studies). Verdict: document why it's excluded; revisit only if a future feature lets a company enroll its own scanner with many reference scans (then flatbed scanners do have usable PRNU-like fixed-pattern noise).
  - _local:_ Would be local if used: polimi-ispl/prnu-python (GitHub, NumPy/SciPy/PyWavelets). But the reference-image requirement makes it a dead end for single-document analysis, not the CPU cost.
  - _sources:_ https://github.com/polimi-ispl/prnu-python, https://arxiv.org/pdf/1907.04025, https://ietresearch.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1049/iet-bmt.2016.0117
- **Local deep-learning forgery localizers (optional heavy module: DQ/CAT-Net/TruFor via PhotoHolmes; DocTamper models)** (effort: large)
  - _how:_ PhotoHolmes (pip, PyTorch) wraps 10 detectors behind one API (`photoholmes run <method> <image>`), including classical (DQ, ZERO, Splicebuster, Noisesniffer) and pretrained CNN/transformer localizers (CAT-Net: RGB+DCT streams tuned to compression artifacts; TruFor: noise-fingerprint transformer with a reliability map; PSCC-Net; FOCAL). Weights download once, then inference is fully offline on CPU. For document-specific text tampering, the DocTamper line of work (CVPR 2023, 170k tampered document images incl. invoices/receipts) provides models built around DCT + visual features.
  - _signal:_ Benchmarks in the PhotoHolmes paper: FOCAL/TruFor lead on splicing (F1 0.74-0.95 on Columbia/DSO), ZERO leads on JPEG-quality manipulations, and everything degrades sharply after social-media-style recompression — so treat scores as one weighted module, not truth. Deep models are the only class that stays competitive on character-level document edits (DocTamper). FP risk: moderate and hard to explain to users (black box); TruFor/CAT-Net weights are research-license-only — check before commercial deployment; DocTamper dataset/models are non-commercial.
  - _local:_ pip install from github.com/photoholmes/photoholmes; PyTorch CPU wheels (~200 MB), model weights 100-500 MB, inference ~2-10 s/page CPU, needs ~2-4 GB RAM — feasible but heaviest option for a small cloud box. No document leaves the machine. License review required for each weight set.
  - _sources:_ https://arxiv.org/html/2412.14969v1, https://github.com/qcf-568/DocTamper, https://arxiv.org/pdf/2507.16397, https://arxiv.org/pdf/2505.11003

---

### RESEARCH — PDF internal structure forensics: incremental updates, revision diffing, xref anomalies, content-stream traces, redaction failures, signature coverage, and structural legitimacy signals

**Key insights:**
- Incremental-update COUNT is a weak, high-false-positive signal (signing, form filling, Bates stamping all append revisions — the DoJ Epstein files' incremental updates were benign Bates numbering, and automated scanners that flagged them drew false conclusions). The high-value move is revision ROLLBACK + DIFF: truncate at each %%EOF (or use pyHanko's historical resolvers), extract text/render each version, and show exactly what changed — this converts a weak structural hint into near-courtroom evidence or into a confident false-positive dismissal.
- The strongest single tamper signals in this domain, in rough order: (1) visible-text vs embedded Factur-X XML mismatch, (2) revision diff showing a changed amount/date, (3) /TouchUp_TextEdit or /IceniObject editor breadcrumbs in content streams, (4) text hidden under an opaque rectangle confirmed by pixel rendering, (5) signature ByteRange not covering the full file with an invasive post-signing diff. All are implementable with PyMuPDF + pikepdf + pyHanko + x-ray + factur-x, pure pip, sub-second each.
- Legitimacy confirmation is as tractable as tamper detection: intact digital signature with ENTIRE_FILE coverage (pyHanko, offline with cached trust roots), schema-valid Factur-X matching the text layer, single revision with trailer ID[0]==ID[1], valid unbroken linearization, PDF/A conformance, and producer structural-dialect matching the metadata claim. These positive signals directly serve the 'minimize false positives / confirm legitimate files' goal and should add trust points, not just absence-of-flags.
- Absence of structural evidence is NOT cleanliness: Save-As, Ghostscript/qpdf rewrites, print-to-PDF, and scan-to-PDF flatten all history and strip every trace discussed here. Score such files 'inconclusive at the structure layer' and lean on the image-forensics and content modules instead; conversely a structure-clean file that IS a full rewrite of a bank-style linearized original is itself mildly suspicious (laundering).
- False-positive control comes from classification and convergence, not thresholds: classify WHAT changed between revisions (content stream/image = critical; annotations/signatures/DSS = benign), whitelist OCR invisible-text layers (Tr 3 over full-page image) before flagging hidden text, gate JS/XFA flags by document type (French CERFA/tax forms legitimately contain them), and require multi-domain corroboration before high severity (Holofin's model: 2 corroborating domains ×1.25, 3+ ×1.5 — 'one lie is an anomaly, four lies are a pattern').
- Recommended stack for structure.py, all local: pikepdf (xref tables, trailer, qpdf check, object graph), PyMuPDF (revisions via version_count, content streams, drawings/rawdict, rendering), pyhanko (signatures, coverage, revision-aware reading, diff rules), x-ray (bad redactions), factur-x (e-invoice XML), optional vendored pdfid.py/pdf-parser.py (byte-level census) and veraPDF as an optional Java-based deep tier. Everything except veraPDF is wheel-only pip with no apt deps and fits a few-seconds-per-document budget on a small cloud box.
- Parser diversity matters: the Epstein case study's core lesson is that single-parser automated verdicts misfire — qpdf, PyMuPDF and a raw byte-scanner disagree on malformed files, so run the cheap byte-level scan (EOF/startxref/Linearized//ID) independently of the object-model parse and reconcile, lowering module confidence when they disagree rather than emitting flags.

**Techniques:**
- **Incremental update / revision counting (%%EOF + startxref + /Prev chain)** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Byte-scan the raw file for xref sections terminated by %%EOF (regex like rb'[^t]xref.*?%%EOF' plus counting b'startxref' and b'%%EOF' occurrences), then cross-check by walking the trailer's /Prev pointers (and /XRefStm for hybrid files) which form a linked list of every save operation. Each incremental save appends new objects + a new xref section + trailer instead of rewriting the file, so revision_count = number of xref sections. PyMuPDF exposes doc.version_count directly; pikepdf's Pdf.get_xref_table() gives structured entries. Didier Stevens' pdfid.py -e and pdftool.py 'iu' command implement the same counting.
  - _signal:_ 1 revision = written once by the generator (weak positive signal). 2+ revisions = the file was re-saved after creation by some editor. Catches Acrobat/Foxit 'Save' after editing amounts/dates. FALSE-POSITIVE RISK IS HIGH if used alone: digital signing, form filling, Bates stamping, and annotation workflows all legitimately append revisions (the DoJ Epstein files had incremental updates that were just Bates numbering). Treat count alone as low/medium severity; the discriminator is WHAT changed (see revision diffing).
  - _local:_ Pure Python on raw bytes + PyMuPDF (pip: PyMuPDF, already in stack) or pikepdf (pip: pikepdf, bundles qpdf in wheels, no apt needed). Milliseconds per file, negligible RAM.
  - _sources:_ https://blog.didierstevens.com/programs/pdf-tools/, https://blog.didierstevens.com/2021/01/31/new-tool-pdftool-py/, https://htpbe.tech/blog/pdf-xref-table-forensics, https://isc.sans.edu/diary/25904
- **Prior-version extraction (rollback) + text/render diff — show WHAT was edited** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ Because incremental saves are append-only, truncating the file at each successive %%EOF boundary yields each historical version: for revision i, take bytes[0:end_offset_i] (cumulative from file start, not between offsets) and open it as its own PDF (PyMuPDF opens slightly-damaged prefixes in repair mode). Then extract text per page from each version and difflib-diff them, and/or render pages to pixmaps and pixel-diff, to display exactly which words/amounts/dates changed between saves. pyHanko offers a cleaner parser-level route: PdfFileReader(r).get_historical_resolver(rev) reads any prior revision without byte truncation. C tool pdfresurrect and Didier Stevens' pdftool.py 'iu' do the extraction standalone.
  - _signal:_ Strongest evidence in this whole domain: 'revision 1 said 1 025,00 EUR, revision 2 says 2 025,00 EUR' is near-courtroom-grade and makes a great UI. Converts the weak 'has incremental updates' signal into critical or into a false-positive dismissal (diff shows only a signature/annotation was added → downgrade to benign, even a positive signal). FP risk very low when the diff is classified: content-stream/text changes = critical; only /Annots, /AcroForm values, or DSS/signature objects = benign. Caveats: %%EOF byte-scan can false-match inside streams (validate each prefix actually parses); Save-As/flattened files have no history (inconclusive, not clean).
  - _local:_ PyMuPDF (already in stack) + stdlib difflib; pyHanko (pip: pyhanko) for historical resolvers; optional pdfresurrect (apt: pdfresurrect on Debian/Kali). Cost: one extra parse+text-extraction per revision — typically <1s total for invoices; rendering pixmaps for visual diff adds ~0.1-0.3s/page. Fine for a small box.
  - _sources:_ https://github.com/pymupdf/PyMuPDF/discussions/3096, https://khkonsulting.com/2017/10/pdf-time-machine/, https://github.com/enferex/pdfresurrect, https://blog.didierstevens.com/2021/01/31/new-tool-pdftool-py/
- **Object-level diff between revisions (classify which objects were redefined)** (effort: large)
  - _how:_ Parse each revision's xref section and list the object numbers it (re)defines. For every object redefined in a later revision, resolve its type/role in the document graph: page /Contents stream, image XObject, /FontDescriptor, /Info dict, /Metadata XMP, /Annots, /AcroForm field, DSS/signature objects. Implement by walking xref sections yourself (pikepdf.Pdf.get_xref_table + parsing trailers per revision) or via pyHanko's revision-aware reader; pyHanko's pyhanko.sign.diff_analysis module already contains a rule-based engine that judges which object-updating operations between revisions are legitimate (its reject-by-default StandardDiffPolicy is reusable outside signature contexts).
  - _signal:_ Precisely identifies the nature of the edit without needing full text diffing: a later revision that redefines a page's /Contents or swaps an image XObject is content tampering (high/critical); one that only adds signature/DSS/annotation objects is a legitimacy-compatible update (neutral/positive). Also detects shadow-attack patterns: duplicate object IDs redefined post-signing, overlay objects retyped in a new xref. FP risk low-moderate: some editors rewrite the page object even for trivial changes; combine with text diff for severity.
  - _local:_ pikepdf (pip: pikepdf) and/or pyhanko (pip: pyhanko), both pure pip installs with wheels; CPU cost is one parse per revision, well under a second for typical invoices.
  - _sources:_ https://docs.pyhanko.eu/en/latest/lib-guide/validation.html, https://pikepdf.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api/main.html, https://www.ndss-symposium.org/wp-content/uploads/ndss2021_1B-4_24117_paper.pdf
- **Orphan/ghost object detection (deleted-but-present content)** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ Build the reachability graph from the trailer's /Root (catalog) and /Info: recursively follow all indirect references, including objects packed inside /ObjStm compressed object streams (pdf-parser.py -O extracts these; pikepdf resolves them transparently). Any object present in the file but unreachable from the current trailer is an orphan — typically content deleted or replaced in a later revision whose bytes still exist (old text streams, old images, old Info dicts). Extract and decode orphaned streams; if an orphaned content stream or string contains text resembling invoice fields (amounts, dates, IBANs), surface it and diff against visible text.
  - _signal:_ Catches 'deleted' original values still recoverable in the file — e.g., the pre-edit amount lurking in a superseded stream — and hidden metadata (the Epstein analysis found orphaned document-info dictionaries inside compressed object streams). Strong when the orphan contains readable divergent text. FP risk moderate: linearization hint streams, unused resources from sloppy generators, and superseded objects from benign updates are all orphans too — score low unless the orphan decodes to meaningful text that differs from the visible layer.
  - _local:_ pikepdf or PyMuPDF (xref walk: doc.xref_length() + doc.xref_object(i)) — pure pip. pdf-parser.py (single-file BSD-ish script, vendorable) as reference implementation. Sub-second for invoices; RAM proportional to file size.
  - _sources:_ https://blog.didierstevens.com/2019/03/07/analyzing-a-phishing-pdf-with-objstm/, https://ascii.co.uk/news/article/news-20260206-becbc299/pdf-forensics-deep-dive-doj-epstein-files-reveal-sanitizatio, https://pdfa.org/a-case-study-in-pdf-forensics-the-epstein-pdfs/, https://www.pdf-insecurity.org/signature/shadow-attacks.html
- **Xref anomaly analysis (free list, generation numbers, offset integrity, hybrid tables)** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ Validate the cross-reference machinery itself: (a) object 0 must be free with generation 65535 and head a consistent free-list linked list; (b) generation numbers >0 on in-use objects mean an object number was freed and reused — essentially never produced by one-shot generators, so it implies multi-session editing; (c) verify each xref offset actually lands on 'N 0 obj' for the right N (offset/object mismatches indicate manual tampering or signature-wrapping-style xref games); (d) flag mixed xref styles across revisions (classic table then xref stream, or Word-style hybrid /XRefStm) inconsistent with the claimed producer; (e) startxref pointing nowhere or a suspiciously large /Prev. qpdf --check / pikepdf Pdf.check() reports most of these as warnings; parse its output rather than reimplementing.
  - _signal:_ Catches hand-edited files, tools that splice objects, and the malformed-xref family of signature attacks (Signature Wrapping inserts a manipulated xref + ByteRange games). Generation-number reuse and offset/object mismatches are rare in legitimate files (decent signal); however qpdf warnings in general are COMMON on legitimate PDFs from sloppy generators — bucket 'spec-sloppiness' (low) separately from 'structurally impossible' (high) anomalies to control false positives.
  - _local:_ pikepdf (pip; bundles qpdf, exposes check() and get_xref_table()) — no apt needed. Milliseconds. Optionally qpdf CLI (apt: qpdf) for --show-xref during development.
  - _sources:_ https://htpbe.tech/blog/pdf-xref-table-forensics, https://www.pdf-insecurity.org/signature/swa.html, https://pypdf2.readthedocs.io/en/3.0.0/dev/pdf-format.html, https://man.archlinux.org/man/extra/qpdf/qpdf.1.en
- **Editor breadcrumbs in content streams: /TouchUp_TextEdit and friends** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Decompress every page content stream (PyMuPDF doc.xref_stream(xref) or pikepdf) and regex for editor-inserted marked-content operators: Adobe Acrobat's 'Edit Text & Images' inserts '/TouchUp_TextEdit MP' at each edited region (one marker per edit — five edited amounts leave five markers); Iceni Infix wraps edits in '/IceniObject << ... >> DP'. Also scan for editor-specific /PieceInfo entries in page/document dictionaries. These are plain tokens sitting next to the drawing operators; most viewers ignore them but they are trivially greppable.
  - _signal:_ Near-smoking-gun that the text layer was edited in place with a PDF editor — exactly the 'change 1025 to 2025' fraud pattern. False-positive rate is extremely low (the marker only exists if the tool was used); the only nuance is legitimate corrections by the issuer, which is still worth a high-severity flag on an invoice that should come straight from a billing system. Absence proves nothing (many editors leave no marker, and a full rewrite strips them).
  - _local:_ PyMuPDF or pikepdf (already available); pure regex over decompressed streams; microseconds per page.
  - _sources:_ https://holofin.ai/blog/document-fraud-detection-what-a-pdf-cant-hide/, https://community.adobe.com/questions-9/touchup-objects-in-todays-versions-of-acrobat-1252432, https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/978-3-642-22309-9_12
- **Content-stream overlay anomaly detection (opaque rects and late-drawn text over content)** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ PDF paints in stream order, so anything covering earlier content must be drawn after it. Three checks: (1) opaque rectangle over text — use PyMuPDF page.get_drawings() for filled rects and page.get_text('rawdict') for character bboxes, flag text whose bbox is covered by a later-drawn non-transparent rect; confirm by rendering the clip (pix = page.get_pixmap(clip=bbox); pix.is_unicolor / pix.color_topusage()) to prove the text is visually hidden; (2) text-over-text — two text spans from different BT/ET blocks whose bboxes overlap heavily (an overlay value pasted on top of the original); (3) appended-overlay structure — /Contents is an array whose last stream contains only a tiny BT...ET block with an absolute Tm jumping into an already-painted region, or a q...Do of a small XObject at the end of the page: classic 'value patched on top' signature, also the Hide variant of shadow attacks.
  - _signal:_ Catches covered/replaced values and fake redactions; text-under-opaque-rect confirmed by pixel rendering is high severity with low FP. Text-over-text and appended-stream heuristics have moderate FP (legitimate stamps, 'PAID' watermarks, letterheads, Bates numbers are also late-drawn overlays) — whitelist page-margin positions and diagonal watermark geometry, and raise severity only when the overlay sits on an amount/date/IBAN region.
  - _local:_ PyMuPDF only (already in stack). get_drawings + rawdict + selective pixmap rendering ≈ 0.1-0.5s/page. Well within budget.
  - _sources:_ https://medium.com/@pymupdf/unmasking-fake-redactions-with-pymupdf-82de0b34d21d, https://github.com/pymupdf/PyMuPDF-Utilities/blob/master/jupyter-notebooks/detect-hidden.ipynb, https://www.pdf-insecurity.org/signature/shadow-attacks.html, https://www.group-ib.com/blog/pdf-tampering/
- **Redaction-failure and invisible-text detection (x-ray + render-mode checks)** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Use Free Law Project's x-ray library (pip: x-ray, PyMuPDF-based): it finds rectangles, maps text under them, renders each rect and checks color uniformity to distinguish bad redactions (text merely covered) from real ones. Complement with invisible-text checks: text rendering mode 3 (Tr 3, invisible), fill color equal to background (white-on-white), and glyphs positioned outside the CropBox — all extractable from PyMuPDF rawdict + content stream inspection.
  - _signal:_ Dual purpose: fraud (original values 'hidden' rather than removed — recover and diff them against visible values) and a legitimacy/privacy warning. High signal when hidden text differs from visible text in the same region. KEY false-positive trap: scanned documents with an OCR text layer legitimately use invisible text (Tr 3) across the whole page — detect the OCR pattern (full-page invisible text over an image) and exempt it, otherwise every OCRed attestation gets flagged.
  - _local:_ pip: x-ray (pulls PyMuPDF). No system deps. ~0.2-1s per document. Small RAM.
  - _sources:_ https://github.com/freelawproject/x-ray, https://www.argeliuslabs.com/deep-research-on-pdf-redaction-failures-and-security-risks-exploits-and-best-practices/, https://github.com/pymupdf/PyMuPDF/discussions/2263
- **Signature /ByteRange coverage + post-signing diff analysis (pyHanko)** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ For each embedded signature (r = PdfFileReader(f); r.embedded_signatures), pyHanko checks that /ByteRange covers the whole file except the /Contents hole and reports status.coverage as SignatureCoverageLevel (ENTIRE_FILE vs ENTIRE_REVISION vs worse). If coverage is ENTIRE_REVISION, later incremental updates exist; pyHanko's diff_analysis module (DEFAULT_DIFF_POLICY, reject-by-default rules, MDPPerm levels) adjudicates whether those post-signing updates are permissible (form fill, DSS, second signature) or invasive, exposed via status.modification_level and docmdp_ok. Cryptographic validation runs fully offline given trust roots (skip revocation, or pre-cache CRLs); even WITHOUT any trust anchor you can still verify document-integrity (digest matches, coverage complete) purely locally.
  - _signal:_ Tamper detection: bytes appended after the signed range + diff policy flagging content changes = signed-then-modified, catches naive fraud on signed invoices/attestations and (partially) shadow attacks — pair with duplicate-object-ID and retyped-object checks per the NDSS 2021 countermeasures. Legitimacy confirmation: an intact signature with ENTIRE_FILE coverage is the single strongest positive trust signal available (+large trust bonus), common on French government attestations and payroll docs. FP risk: pyHanko's own docs warn diff adjudication is 'inherently somewhat risky' — treat 'suspicious post-signing update' as high, not critical, and show the object diff.
  - _local:_ pip: pyhanko (pure Python + cryptography/asn1crypto). No apt deps. Signature validation ~100-500ms. Trust roots (e.g., EU Trusted List certs, French ANTS/AC roots) can be bundled/cached locally so nothing leaves the box.
  - _sources:_ https://docs.pyhanko.eu/en/latest/lib-guide/validation.html, https://www.ndss-symposium.org/wp-content/uploads/ndss2021_1B-4_24117_paper.pdf, https://www.pdf-insecurity.org/signature/swa.html, https://pdfa.org/recently-identified-pdf-digital-signature-vulnerabilities/
- **Active-content census: /JS, /OpenAction, /AA, /Launch, /EmbeddedFile, /XFA** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Count risk-bearing name occurrences across all objects including inside /ObjStm: /JavaScript and /JS (scripts), /OpenAction and /AA (auto-run actions), /Launch (execute files), /EmbeddedFile and /Names→/EmbeddedFiles (attachments), /RichMedia, /XFA. pdfid.py does the byte-level census (obfuscation-resistant via hex-name normalization); peepdf-3 (pip: peepdf-3) and pdfalyzer (pip: pdfalyzer, YARA-based) give object-level context. Implement natively by iterating pikepdf objects and checking dictionary keys — 30 lines.
  - _signal:_ In an invoice or attestation, JavaScript, Launch actions, and unexpected embedded files are near-always illegitimate (malware or staging) — high severity, tiny FP rate for /Launch and /RichMedia. CRITICAL caveat for your document types: French tax/government forms (impots.gouv.fr) legitimately use XFA and AcroForm with field-validation JavaScript — gate the JS/XFA flags on document type or you will flag real CERFA forms. Embedded files also matter for fraud: attackers sometimes leave the original unedited document attached.
  - _local:_ pikepdf or vendored pdfid.py (single-file). pdfalyzer needs yara (pip: yara-python, wheels available). All sub-second, no apt deps.
  - _sources:_ https://zeltser.com/peepdf-malicious-pdf-analysis, https://blog.didierstevens.com/programs/pdf-tools/, https://github.com/michelcrypt4d4mus/pdfalyzer, https://hacktricks.wiki/en/generic-methodologies-and-resources/basic-forensic-methodology/specific-software-file-type-tricks/pdf-file-analysis.html
- **Broken-linearization check (/Linearized dict vs actual file state)** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Linearized ('Fast Web View') PDFs start with a /Linearized dictionary whose /L key must equal the exact file length, with a specific first-page-first object layout. An incremental save appends data, so the header still says /Linearized but /L no longer matches the true byte length and the xref chain no longer matches the linearized layout. Check: scan the first ~1KB for '/Linearized', read /L, compare to os.path.getsize(); combined with revision count >1 this proves the file left its generator and was edited by an appending tool.
  - _signal:_ Crisp, cheap corroborator: /L mismatch = 'file was modified after being produced by a linearizing generator' (many bank/telecom billing systems emit linearized PDFs). Catches edits even when metadata was scrubbed. FP risk low-moderate: a few tools emit broken linearization from the start, and re-linearizing on Save-As resets the evidence — use as medium-severity corroboration, and treat a VALID linearized single-revision file as a small positive trust signal.
  - _local:_ Pure Python byte scan, microseconds. qpdf --check also reports linearization errors via pikepdf.
  - _sources:_ https://www.nutrient.io/blog/linearized-pdf/, https://kbdeveloper.qoppa.com/how-to-check-that-a-pdf-document-is-linearized-fast-web-view/, https://apryse.com/blog/pdf-format/what-is-pdf-linearization
- **Producer structural fingerprinting (metadata claims vs structural dialect)** (effort: large)
  - _how:_ Every generator writes a recognizable structural dialect independent of the (spoofable) /Producer string: LibreOffice puts /DocChecksum in the trailer; Word uses hybrid xref (/Prev + /XRefStm); pdfTeX/pdfLaTeX has distinctive object ordering and PTEX comments; Chrome's Skia omits /ID and uses LF line endings; iText, ReportLab, wkhtmltopdf each have telltale object layouts, coordinate precision, and operator styles in content streams. Build a fingerprint table (trailer keys, xref style, line endings, object numbering pattern, content-stream operator style, font-subsetting style) and compare the observed dialect against the claimed Producer/Creator. The 'questio' project (pip: questio, MIT) implements this as learnable per-issuer content-stream fingerprints plus font-subset-tag session tracking.
  - _signal:_ Tamper detection: metadata says 'SAP'/'Microsoft Word' but structure is Ghostscript/qpdf-rewrite or online-converter dialect → the file was laundered or the metadata forged (medium-high). Legitimacy confirmation: dialect matches claimed producer AND matches previously seen genuine invoices from the same issuer → meaningful positive trust (this is structural template matching, complementing your Module I). FP risk moderate until the fingerprint corpus is decent; questio's docs note single-sample issuer fingerprints are too narrow and cause false alerts — require 3+ samples per issuer.
  - _local:_ Pure Python over pikepdf/PyMuPDF parses; questio is pip-installable (needs exiftool via apt: libimage-exiftool-perl / brew exiftool). Fast; the cost is corpus curation, not CPU.
  - _sources:_ https://holofin.ai/blog/document-fraud-detection-what-a-pdf-cant-hide/, https://github.com/abcreativ/questio
- **Trailer /ID pair consistency + font-subset session tracking** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ The trailer /ID is an array of two byte strings: the first is the permanent ID set at creation, the second changes on every update — on a freshly generated file both are EQUAL; after any incremental update they DIFFER while the first stays constant. Check equality and cross-revision stability of ID[0]. Complementary session tracker: each save by a subsetting editor assigns fresh random 6-letter subset prefixes (ABCDEF+Arial); multiple different subset prefixes for the SAME base font on one page, or a font used on exactly one page of a multipage doc, indicate a separate editing session touched that page (questio's 'font session tracking').
  - _signal:_ ID[0]==ID[1] with a single revision corroborates 'never modified since creation' (positive signal); differing IDs corroborate modification even when other traces were cleaned. Two subset prefixes for one base font is a strong text-edit indicator with low FP on machine-generated invoices (generators subset once per document). FP risk: some producers omit /ID entirely (Chrome/Skia) or regenerate both on full rewrite — score only when /ID is present; merged/concatenated PDFs legitimately mix font subsets, so gate on document type.
  - _local:_ pikepdf (trailer access) + PyMuPDF page.get_fonts() — all pip, microseconds.
  - _sources:_ https://holofin.ai/blog/document-fraud-detection-what-a-pdf-cant-hide/, https://github.com/abcreativ/questio, https://htpbe.tech/blog/pdf-xref-table-forensics
- **Well-formedness and PDF/A conformance as positive trust (qpdf check, veraPDF)** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Run pikepdf's Pdf.check() (qpdf --check equivalent) to get a syntactic-validity verdict plus warning list; a clean single-revision, spec-conformant file from a known-good producer earns a trust bonus. Optionally run veraPDF (Java CLI, open source, by the PDF Association/OPF) to validate PDF/A conformance: files claiming PDF/A in XMP that fail validation were altered or re-produced by a non-conforming tool; files genuinely conforming to PDF/A-1b/2b/3b were emitted by a serious archival pipeline.
  - _signal:_ Primarily a legitimacy-confirmation signal: professional billing systems emit consistent, valid, often PDF/A or linearized files. A claimed-PDF/A-but-invalid file is a medium tamper signal. FP control: do NOT penalize generic qpdf warnings (very common on legit files); only reward cleanliness and flag claim/reality mismatches. veraPDF is heavy (Java, apt: default-jre-headless ~200MB, ~1-2s+ per file, few hundred MB RAM) — make it an optional deep-scan tier; pikepdf check is the always-on tier.
  - _local:_ pikepdf (pip) always-on; veraPDF optional (Java runtime via apt, runs fully offline). Both local-only.
  - _sources:_ https://verapdf.org/home/, https://openpreservation.org/tools/verapdf/, https://pikepdf.readthedocs.io/
- **Factur-X / embedded e-invoice XML cross-validation (French/German invoices)** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ French/German e-invoices increasingly embed a machine-readable XML (factur-x.xml, EN 16931 / CII) as an /EmbeddedFile inside a PDF/A-3 container (Factur-X/ZUGFeRD standard — mandatory rollout for French B2B e-invoicing). Use the factur-x library (pip: factur-x, Akretion, BSD) or pikepdf to extract the attachment, validate it against the official XSD/schematron (bundled, offline), then cross-check XML fields (total amount, date, invoice number, seller SIRET/VAT, IBAN) against the visible text layer extracted with pdfplumber/PyMuPDF.
  - _signal:_ Devastatingly effective for your exact use case: a fraudster who edits the visible amount almost never updates the embedded XML → visible-vs-XML mismatch is a critical, near-zero-FP tamper flag. Conversely, presence of schema-valid Factur-X whose values match the text layer (ideally plus PDF/A-3 conformance and a signature) is a top-tier legitimacy confirmation. FP risk: rounding/locale formatting when matching amounts (compare normalized decimals, not strings); absence of Factur-X is neutral.
  - _local:_ pip: factur-x (+ lxml). No system deps, fully offline, milliseconds. Ideal for the small-box budget.
  - _sources:_ https://github.com/akretion/factur-x, https://pypi.org/project/factur-x/

---

### RESEARCH — PDF metadata forensics for tamper detection: producer fingerprinting, DocInfo/XMP cross-validation, trailer /ID analysis, date-format fingerprints, PDF/A markers

**Key insights:**
- Metadata is a claim, not proof: every DocInfo/XMP field is trivially editable, so the highest-value checks are INTERNAL CONSISTENCY cross-checks (DocInfo vs XMP vs raw structure vs date-string style vs trailer IDs) rather than absolute values — a sophisticated forger must keep ~6 independent layers coherent, and almost none do.
- Most strong indicators are ONE-WAY: /ID pair mismatch, incremental updates, '; modified using iText', TouchUp_TextEdit prove processing/editing happened, but their ABSENCE proves nothing — full-rewrite tools (Ghostscript, most converters) legally erase all history. Score asymmetrically: presence = flag, absence = neutral.
- The biggest blind spot of metadata forensics is from-scratch fabrication: a fake invoice typed in Word/Canva has pristine, consistent metadata. The counters are Creator-class checks (Canva/PowerPoint/Photoshop creator on an 'official' doc), coding-style vs claimed-issuer verification, and TrustyFile's template/content modules — metadata alone cannot carry this case.
- Two mandatory false-positive whitelists before flagging revisions: linearized ('Fast Web View') PDFs legitimately contain two %%EOF/startxref pairs when unedited, and a single signing pass (DocuSign, eIDAS signatures) legitimately adds one incremental revision.
- The Adhatarao–Lauradoux measurements show online converters usually don't say who they are: Smallpdf shows up as 'Microsoft Word for Office 365' or '3-Heights(TM)', PDF24 as 'LibreOffice'/'Ghostscript', Sejda as 'Apache FOP'/'SAMBox' — so a producer database must map engine strings to services, and 'LibreOffice' on a corporate invoice deserves mild suspicion on its own.
- For the French market, Factur-X is the emerging killer feature: the embedded CII XML is a tamper-evident second copy of every amount/date/SIRET — text-layer vs XML divergence is near-zero-FP proof of tampering, and validity is the strongest positive trust signal available; France's 2026 e-invoicing mandate makes coverage grow rapidly.
- Everything researched here runs fully locally in pure Python (pymupdf + pikepdf + factur-x + stdlib regex/xml) in well under a second per document; the only heavyweight optional dependency is veraPDF (Java) for full PDF/A validation, and pdfresurrect (apt) as a cross-check for revision extraction.
- Revision archaeology (truncate at each %%EOF, reopen with fitz, diff text/metadata across versions) upgrades the verdict from 'file was modified' to 'here is the original amount before the edit' — the single most persuasive output a fraud-detection UI can show, and it is ~50 lines of pure Python.
- Scoring should fire on PATTERN COMBINATIONS, not single fields (e.g. years-old CreationDate + institutional Creator + iLovePDF Producer + 3 revisions = high risk; the same converter producer alone = low), which aligns with TrustyFile's existing minimize-false-positives philosophy.

**Techniques:**
- **Producer/Creator fingerprint database (generator vs editor vs online converter)** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Read /Producer and /Creator from DocInfo (PyMuPDF doc.metadata) and classify against a curated string database in three tiers: (1) GENERATORS that produce first-generation documents: 'Microsoft Word' / 'Microsoft® Word for Microsoft 365', 'LibreOffice X.Y', 'Adobe PDF Library' (+Creator 'Adobe InDesign'), 'Acrobat Distiller', 'pdfTeX-1.40.x', 'Skia/PDF m##' (Chrome print-to-PDF), 'macOS Version ... Quartz PDFContext', SAP/Oracle BI/billing-system strings; (2) EDITORS/LIBRARIES that imply post-generation processing: 'Adobe Acrobat Pro/DC', 'iText'/'iTextSharp', 'Apache PDFBox' (used by Stirling-PDF), 'SAMBox' (Sejda's engine), 'pikepdf', 'itext-paulo', 'Aspose.PDF', 'Photoshop' or 'GIMP' or 'Canva' as Creator on an official document; (3) ONLINE CONVERTERS (empirically measured in the Adhatarao–Lauradoux study): 'www.ilovepdf.com', Sejda='Apache FOP Version 2.3' (conversion) or 'SAMBox 1.1.x' (other ops), Smallpdf='3-Heights(TM)' (compression) or 'Microsoft Word for Office 365' (conversion — they run real Word), PDF24='LibreOffice 6.x'/'GPL Ghostscript 9.x', 'PDF Candy', 'lightpdf.com', 'Online2PDF.com', 'Soda PDF Online', 'zonepdf.com', 'Neevia PDFcompress', 'FreePDF.net PDFill', 'pdfaid using ABCpdf'. Score generator=neutral/positive, editor=medium flag on an invoice, converter=medium flag; institutional-billing producer = small trust bonus.
  - _signal:_ Catches lazy tampering (most amateur fraud passes through iLovePDF/Sejda/Acrobat and the Producer betrays it) and confirms legitimacy when the producer matches known billing pipelines. Medium strength: strings are trivially spoofable and some legitimate senders genuinely use converters (CLAUDE.md already notes this). FP risk moderate — flag, don't heavily penalize; note online services often mask themselves as LibreOffice/Ghostscript/Word so a 'LibreOffice' producer on a corporate invoice is itself mildly informative.
  - _local:_ Pure Python: pymupdf (pip) doc.metadata, plus a JSON string database you maintain. Zero system deps, <1 ms, negligible RAM.
  - _sources:_ https://arxiv.org/abs/2103.02702 (Adhatarao & Lauradoux, 'Robust PDF Files Forensics Using Coding Style' — Table 11 lists measured producer strings of ~25 online services), https://htpbe.tech/blog/pdf-metadata-fields-complete-reference, https://www.checkfile.ai/en-CA/blog/detect-pdf-metadata-tampering, https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3437880.3460405 (Exploitation and Sanitization of Hidden Data in PDF Files)
- **Producer append-chain parsing ('; modified using iText…')** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ iText/iTextSharp (AGPL versions and derivatives, which power many online editors including parts of iLovePDF) never replace the Producer when modifying a file — they APPEND: e.g. 'Adobe Acrobat 9.4.1; modified using iTextSharp 5.0.5 (c) 1T3XT BVBA'. Split /Producer on ';' and regex for 'modified using', 'edited', 'converted by', version chains. Each appended segment is one documented processing pass; the first segment reveals the original generator, later segments reveal the editing tools in order.
  - _signal:_ Very strong, near-zero-FP tamper/processing evidence: the string literally logs the modification chain, and iText's API deliberately makes it hard to remove (only paid licensees can). Catches invoices generated by a real system then round-tripped through an iText-based editor. Editing is not always fraud (a company may stamp/merge legitimately), so treat as high flag on invoices, critical when combined with amount-bearing pages changing.
  - _local:_ Pure Python string parsing on doc.metadata['producer']. No deps beyond pymupdf. Microseconds.
  - _sources:_ https://api.itextpdf.com/iText/java/9.0.0/com/itextpdf/commons/actions/producer/ProducerBuilder.html, https://www.mail-archive.com/itext-questions@lists.sourceforge.net/msg55425.html
- **Creator/Producer coherence-pair validation** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Maintain a table of known-coherent (Creator, Producer) pairs: ('Microsoft Word', 'Microsoft Word...'), ('Writer', 'LibreOffice 7.x'), ('Adobe InDesign CC', 'Adobe PDF Library 15.x'), ('Chromium'/'Mozilla', 'Skia/PDF m##'), ('LaTeX'/'TeX', 'pdfTeX-1.40.x'), ('PScript5.dll', 'Acrobat Distiller'). Flag incoherent pairs: institutional Creator (SAP, 'Oracle BI Publisher', 'PeopleSoft') with a consumer/editor Producer (iLovePDF, PDF24, Acrobat), or design-tool Creators (Canva, PowerPoint, Photoshop, GIMP) on documents claiming to be invoices/attestations — those tools are how fakes get drawn from scratch. Optionally add per-issuer consistency: if you later match a template (Module I), verify the Producer equals the one that issuer normally uses.
  - _signal:_ Medium strength. Catches (a) re-export/editing after original generation, (b) from-scratch fabrication in design tools — the case that defeats pure tamper-detection because the file is 'clean'. FP risk: moderate (legitimate archiving/scanning workflows change the pair); keep at medium severity, escalate only in combination.
  - _local:_ Pure Python lookup table over doc.metadata. Negligible cost.
  - _sources:_ https://htpbe.tech/blog/pdf-metadata-fields-complete-reference, https://www.checkfile.ai/en-CA/blog/detect-pdf-metadata-tampering, https://www.meridiandiscovery.com/articles/pdf-forensic-analysis-xmp-metadata/
- **DocInfo vs XMP cross-validation** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ Extract DocInfo (doc.metadata) and the raw XMP packet (doc.get_xml_metadata() in PyMuPDF, or pikepdf.open_metadata()). Compare the mirrored fields after normalization: /CreationDate ↔ xmp:CreateDate, /ModDate ↔ xmp:ModifyDate, /Creator ↔ xmp:CreatorTool, /Producer ↔ pdf:Producer (normalize PDF 'D:YYYYMMDDHHmmSS+HH'mm'' vs XMP ISO-8601 to UTC before comparing). Flags: (1) date mismatch beyond a few seconds → one metadata layer was edited without the other (metadata-spoofing tools and many naive editors only rewrite DocInfo); (2) DocInfo ModDate LATER than xmp:ModifyMetadataDate → file modified by a non-XMP-aware writer after original export; (3) different Producer/CreatorTool between layers → re-processing; (4) empty DocInfo but populated XMP (or vice versa) → selective scrubbing.
  - _signal:_ Strong tamper signal with low FP when dates are timezone-normalized and a small tolerance is used — legitimate single-pass exports from Word/Adobe/LibreOffice write both layers consistently. Agreement between layers is also a mild legitimacy confirmation. Known FP: tools that legitimately update only DocInfo (some CLI utilities), so severity=high only for date contradictions, medium for producer/creator divergence.
  - _local:_ Pure Python: pymupdf for both layers; parse XMP with stdlib xml.etree or regex (avoid python-xmp-toolkit, which needs the exempi system lib). pikepdf (pip, wheels bundle libqpdf) is a robust alternative. <10 ms per doc.
  - _sources:_ https://www.meridiandiscovery.com/articles/pdf-forensic-analysis-xmp-metadata/, https://pikepdf.readthedocs.io/en/latest/topics/metadata.html, https://www.checkfile.ai/en-CA/blog/detect-pdf-metadata-tampering
- **xmpMM lineage analysis (DocumentID / InstanceID / OriginalDocumentID / DerivedFrom / History)** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ Parse the xmpMM namespace from the XMP packet: xmpMM:DocumentID is a GUID meant to persist across a document's life; xmpMM:InstanceID is regenerated on every save; xmpMM:OriginalDocumentID survives 'save as'; xmpMM:DerivedFrom names the source document (stRef:documentID/instanceID) when the file was produced by converting another; xmpMM:History is an ordered array of edit events, each with stEvt:action ('saved','converted'), stEvt:softwareAgent (e.g. 'Adobe Acrobat Pro DC 21.x'), stEvt:when (timestamp), stEvt:changed ('/metadata' vs '/content'). Rules: InstanceID != DocumentID → at least one resave after creation; History entries whose softwareAgent differs from the claimed Producer, or whose stEvt:when timestamps postdate /ModDate or contradict CreationDate ordering → edited; DerivedFrom present on a 'native' invoice → it was converted from something else.
  - _signal:_ When present (mostly Adobe-toolchain files), this is a literal edit log — strong, detailed evidence including WHICH tool edited and WHEN, and PDFRecon treats History gaps/ID mismatches as indicators. Absence is normal (most non-Adobe tools don't write xmpMM), so never penalize absence. FP low for contradiction rules; note all of it is forgeable by a sophisticated attacker, so it's supporting evidence, not proof.
  - _local:_ Pure Python XML parsing of the XMP stream extracted via pymupdf. No extra deps. <10 ms.
  - _sources:_ https://www.meridiandiscovery.com/articles/pdf-forensic-analysis-xmp-metadata/, https://github.com/Rasmus-Riis/PDFRecon, https://community.adobe.com/t5/indesign-discussions/accessing-the-xmpmm-history-property-in-indesign-cs4/td-p/4845685
- **Missing/stripped-XMP interpretation for professional documents** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Check whether an XMP metadata stream exists at all (doc.get_xml_metadata() empty?) and whether DocInfo is empty. Interpretation matrix: professional origin claimed (bank, utility, government — known from template/content modules) + no XMP and/or empty DocInfo → suspicious, because Word, Adobe tools, InDesign and enterprise billing systems always emit XMP; whereas developer-generated PDFs (ReportLab, FPDF/fpdf2, PDFKit, Puppeteer/Skia) legitimately omit or minimize metadata. Also flag the 'scrub signature': DocInfo present but all values empty strings, or XMP packet present but containing only a bare rdf:RDF skeleton — signs of deliberate metadata removal.
  - _signal:_ Weak-to-medium, context-dependent — use only as a score modifier gated on document type/claimed issuer, never standalone (this is exactly where FPs live: plenty of legitimate SaaS invoices are ReportLab/wkhtmltopdf with no XMP). Deliberate stripping on a corporate document is the interesting case: it usually means the original metadata was incriminating.
  - _local:_ Pure Python via pymupdf. Negligible cost.
  - _sources:_ https://htpbe.tech/blog/pdf-metadata-fields-complete-reference, https://www.meridiandiscovery.com/articles/pdf-forensic-analysis-xmp-metadata/
- **Trailer /ID pair analysis (first vs second identifier)** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ The trailer /ID is an array of two 16-byte strings. Per ISO 32000, the FIRST is set once at creation (traditionally an MD5 of time+path+size) and must stay permanent; the SECOND is regenerated on every update. Read via PyMuPDF: doc.xref_get_key(-1, 'ID') (xref -1 = trailer). Rules: (a) ID[0] == ID[1] → consistent with a never-updated original export (mild trust signal); (b) ID[0] != ID[1] → the file WAS updated at least once by a spec-respecting writer (medium flag; combine with revision count and producer); (c) /ID entirely missing → fingerprint of minimalist generators (cairo and Skia/Chrome omit it per the coding-style paper) — informative for producer verification, mildly suspicious if metadata claims Word/Adobe which always write /ID; (d) if multiple revisions exist, compare the /ID in each trailer of the /Prev chain — a changed FIRST ID across revisions violates the spec and indicates crude tampering/rebuilding.
  - _signal:_ One-way signal: mismatch proves an update happened (could be benign signing/stamping — DocuSign etc. legitimately produce one update), while equality proves nothing because full-rewrite tools (Ghostscript, many converters) mint a fresh equal pair, erasing history. Low FP for the 'mismatch + suspicious producer + >2 revisions' combination; never flag mismatch alone above medium.
  - _local:_ Pure Python: pymupdf xref_get_key(-1,'ID') plus a raw-bytes scan of earlier trailers for the /Prev chain. <5 ms.
  - _sources:_ https://opensource.adobe.com/dc-acrobat-sdk-docs/pdfstandards/PDF32000_2008.pdf (ISO 32000-1 §14.4 File Identifiers), https://pymupdf.readthedocs.io/en/latest/recipes-low-level-interfaces.html, https://arxiv.org/abs/2103.02702
- **Incremental-update counting with linearization/signature whitelist** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Count document revisions three redundant ways and reconcile: (1) PyMuPDF Document.version_count (= incremental saves + 1); (2) raw-bytes count of %%EOF + startxref occurrences; (3) walk the trailer /Prev chain. Then whitelist benign causes before flagging: a /Linearized dictionary near the file start means 'Fast Web View' files legitimately contain two %%EOF/startxref even when unedited (classic false positive — check whether the second startxref merely points into the linearization structure); one extra revision containing a /Sig object (digital signature or DocuSign stamp) is a normal signing pass. Scoring: 1 revision = clean; 2 with signature = normal; 3+ or 2 without signature on an invoice = suspicious, escalating when the Producer is an editor/converter.
  - _signal:_ Strong and cheap: incremental updates are the PDF format's built-in change journal and resist metadata scrubbing (clearing them requires a full rewrite, which itself changes producer/ID fingerprints). The htpbe reference calls xref count 'the forensic lynchpin'. FP risk handled by the linearization and signature whitelists; remaining FP: legitimate workflows that annotate/stamp (delivery notes) — cap severity at high, not critical.
  - _local:_ Pure Python: pymupdf version_count + re.findall(rb'%%EOF'|rb'startxref') on the raw bytes. <10 ms even for large files.
  - _sources:_ https://pymupdf.readthedocs.io/en/latest/document.html (version_count), https://www.forensicfocus.com/forums/general/viewing-pdf-fragements/ (linearized two-EOF false positive), https://htpbe.tech/blog/pdf-metadata-fields-complete-reference, https://www.group-ib.com/blog/pdf-tampering/
- **Revision archaeology: extract prior versions and diff their metadata/text** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ When revisions > 1, reconstruct each earlier version by truncating the file bytes at each successive %%EOF offset and reopening the truncated buffer with fitz.open(stream=...) (MuPDF repairs gracefully). Then diff across versions: DocInfo values (did CreationDate/Producer/amount-bearing text change?), page text extracted per version, /Info object number (a NEW /Info object appended in the last revision while the old Info object still exists as a ghost in the body = metadata was rewritten during the edit — recover the ORIGINAL values from the old object via doc.xref_object() on the orphan). This turns 'file was modified' into 'here is exactly what the fraudster changed', e.g. 1025€ → 2025€.
  - _signal:_ The strongest evidence in this whole domain when it fires: you literally recover the pre-tampering content and can show the diff to the user. Near-zero FP for content diffs that alter amounts/dates/names; benign diffs (added signature page, stamp) are recognizable. Limitation: only works when the editor used incremental save (Adobe Acrobat does by default; full-rewrite tools destroy history).
  - _local:_ Pure Python with pymupdf + byte scanning; the C tool pdfresurrect (apt install pdfresurrect) does the same as a cross-check. CPU: tens of ms per revision; RAM: one extra copy of the file per version — fine on a small box.
