  - _sources:_ https://github.com/enferex/pdfresurrect, https://github.com/pymupdf/PyMuPDF/discussions/3096 (retrieving all versions of an incrementally saved PDF), https://arxiv.org/pdf/2003.10546 (Forensic Analysis of Residual Information in Adobe PDF Files), https://www.group-ib.com/blog/pdf-tampering/
- **Date-string micro-format and timezone fingerprinting** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ Fingerprint the raw /CreationDate and /ModDate STRINGS, not just their parsed values: (1) format variant — trailing apostrophe D:20230117161128+05'30' (pre-PDF-2.0 style, written by Acrobat/BFO/most Adobe-era tools) vs no trailing apostrophe (ISO 32000-2 style), 'Z' vs explicit offset vs NO timezone at all, missing 'D:' prefix, truncated precision, or outright malformed dates (JHOVE's PDF-HUL-133 class) — each generator writes ONE consistent style, so a date style inconsistent with the claimed Producer (e.g. Producer says pdfTeX but the date lacks pdfTeX's characteristic format) means metadata was hand-edited or spoofed; (2) internal consistency — CreationDate offset differs from ModDate offset → the modification happened on a different machine/timezone; DocInfo offset differs from the XMP date's offset → layers edited separately; (3) jurisdiction plausibility — a French invoice (EDF, Orange…) should carry +01'00' in winter and +02'00' in summer MATCHING the date's DST period; a UK/US/UTC offset on a claimed-French document, or +01'00' on an August date, is a cheap high-value anomaly.
  - _signal:_ Medium-strong as a consistency check with low FP (you only flag contradictions, not absolute values). The DST-vs-date check is a neat non-obvious catch for fraudsters who backdate documents. FPs: multinationals generating invoices centrally in another timezone, UTC-configured servers (many SaaS invoices are legitimately +00'00Z') — so gate the jurisdiction rule on 'claimed local French issuer' templates and keep severity medium.
  - _local:_ Pure Python regex on the raw DocInfo strings (access unparsed values via pikepdf docinfo or PyMuPDF xref_get_key on the /Info object to avoid library normalization). Negligible cost.
  - _sources:_ https://bfo.com/blog/2023/01/17/odds_and_ends_dates_timezones_and_apostrophes/, https://openpreservation.org/blogs/a-date-with-pdf-hul-133-improperly-formed-date/, https://www.checkfile.ai/en-CA/blog/detect-pdf-metadata-tampering (jurisdiction timezone anomalies), https://www.meridiandiscovery.com/articles/pdf-forensic-analysis-xmp-metadata/ (timezone-offset discrepancy between create and modify)
- **pdfaid PDF/A claim validation (claim vs reality)** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ Parse pdfaid:part and pdfaid:conformance from XMP (e.g. part=3, conformance='B' → claims PDF/A-3b, the profile required for Factur-X e-invoices). Then verify the claim is plausible with cheap local checks: all fonts embedded (PyMuPDF page.get_fonts() ext/embedded flags), no encryption, no JavaScript (doc.xref count scan for /JS //JavaScript), XMP present and well-formed, no non-conforming annotations. A file that CLAIMS PDF/A but violates basics was either re-processed by a non-conforming tool after generation (the edit broke conformance but kept the stale XMP claim) or had metadata pasted in. Optional full validation: veraPDF CLI, the PDF Association's official open-source validator, run as a subprocess. PDFRecon classifies 'PDF/A violations (when claimed)' as a high-confidence red flag.
  - _signal:_ Two-sided: claimed-and-valid PDF/A on an invoice is a meaningful legitimacy bonus (professional archival pipeline); claimed-but-violated is a strong stale-claim tamper indicator with low FP (conforming generators don't emit broken PDF/A; only post-editing does). No claim = neutral. FP risk mainly from your lightweight checker being stricter than the spec — keep the pure-Python checks to unambiguous rules (font embedding, encryption, JS).
  - _local:_ Claim parsing + basic checks: pure Python (pymupdf). Full validation: veraPDF is local, free, but Java — apt default-jre-headless, ~200-500 MB JVM RAM, 1-3 s/doc; make it an optional deep-scan path on a small box. Everything runs offline.
  - _sources:_ https://verapdf.org, https://www.pdflib.com/pdf-knowledge-base/zugferd-and-factur-x/, https://github.com/Rasmus-Riis/PDFRecon, https://gpdf.com/blog/pdfa-3-explained-and-how-to-verify/
- **Factur-X / ZUGFeRD embedded-XML cross-verification (French invoices)** (effort: medium)
  - _how:_ French/German hybrid e-invoices embed a machine-readable CII XML file (factur-x.xml) inside a PDF/A-3, declared in XMP under the fx: namespace (DocumentType, DocumentFileName, ConformanceLevel). Pipeline: detect the embedded file (PyMuPDF doc.embfile_names()), extract it, validate against the bundled XSD locally (pip package factur-x, xml_check_xsd()), then cross-check the XML's amounts, dates, seller name, SIRET/VAT and IBAN against the values extracted from the PDF's visible text layer. A fraudster who edits the visible text (Photoshop/iLovePDF/Acrobat) almost never updates the embedded XML → any amount/date/IBAN divergence between XML and text layer is near-certain tampering.
  - _signal:_ For documents that carry it: the single strongest signal available — valid + consistent Factur-X is a large trust bonus (France's B2B e-invoicing mandate from Sept 2026 makes coverage grow fast), and XML-vs-text divergence is a critical flag with essentially zero FP (allow rounding/format normalization on amounts). Absence is neutral today. Also check XMP fx: declaration present but attachment missing/invalid = re-processing destroyed the invoice payload.
  - _local:_ pip: factur-x (pulls lxml, PyPDF) + pymupdf for extraction; fully offline (XSDs ship with the package). ~100-300 ms. Optional deeper EN 16931 business-rule validation exists (Mustang, Java) but XSD + cross-check covers the fraud case.
  - _sources:_ https://pypi.org/project/factur-x/, https://www.pdflib.com/pdf-knowledge-base/zugferd-and-factur-x/, https://validatefin.com/en/blog/facturx-zugferd-explained, https://ax.docentric.com/zugferd-e-invoices-compliance-explained/
- **Coding-style structural producer verification (metadata-independent, anti-spoofing)** (effort: large)
  - _how:_ Fingerprint the generator from the file's raw STRUCTURE and check it agrees with the claimed /Producer — defeats metadata spoofing because structure survives metadata edits. Concrete rules from the Adhatarao–Lauradoux study (192 regex/YARA rules, 11 producers, 74% overall / >90% for LibreOffice, pdfTeX, Word, Distiller, Quartz): (1) header line-2 binary magic: 0xB5B5B5B5=Word, 0xE2E3CFD3=Acrobat Distiller, 0xD0D4C5D8=pdfTeX/LuaTeX(TeXLive), 0xC7EC8FA2=Ghostscript, 0xC3A4C3BCC3B6C39F ('äüöß' UTF-8)=LibreOffice, 0xD3EBE9E1=Skia/Chrome, 0xB5EDAEFB=cairo; (2) trailer key sets: /DocChecksum appears ONLY in LibreOffice trailers; Word uniquely writes TWO trailer objects with /XRefStm; Distiller uses xref streams with /DecodeParms /Columns /Predictor; cairo and Skia omit /ID entirely; (3) object formatting style (key order, whitespace/newline conventions inside dictionaries, e.g. Word objects match /4 0 obj\r\n<</Filter/FlateDecode/Length \d+>>\r\nstream\r\n/); (4) xref table presence/absence. Rule: metadata claims Word but no 0xB5B5B5B5 header, no /XRefStm, wrong object style → Producer string is fake or the file was rebuilt by another tool.
  - _signal:_ Strong anti-spoofing signal: a producer/structure CONTRADICTION is highly suspicious with low FP if you only fire on well-attested producers (Word, LibreOffice, pdfTeX, Distiller, Chrome). Match = legitimacy confirmation of the claimed toolchain. FP risk: producer version drift changes styles over time and post-processing legitimately rewrites structure — so score 'mismatch' as medium-high, 'match' as bonus, and 'unknown structure' as neutral; start with ~10 header/trailer rules before investing in object-level rules.
  - _local:_ Pure Python re module over raw bytes (read first/last few KB of file); optionally pip yara-python for the full ruleset. Milliseconds, no system deps.
  - _sources:_ https://arxiv.org/abs/2103.02702 (full paper: header magic Table 2, trailer keys Table 3, per-tool accuracy Tables 6-10), https://www.semanticscholar.org/paper/Robust-PDF-Files-Forensics-Using-Coding-Style-Adhatarao-Lauradoux/81d989deb68fb6eb287a20115a6803ace744cea3
- **Acrobat edit-residue byte-scan (TouchUp_TextEdit and friends)** (effort: small)
  - _how:_ Scan the raw file bytes for residue that Adobe Acrobat's text-editing tools leave behind: '/TouchUp_TextEdit' marked-content/PieceInfo entries (written when text is edited with Acrobat's TouchUp/Edit-Text tool), plus related PieceInfo private data ('/PieceInfo', '/ADBE_CompoundType'), and combine with the incremental-update check (Acrobat edits save incrementally by default, so TouchUp residue usually co-occurs with revisions>1). PDFRecon — an open-source Python/PyMuPDF forensic tool built for exactly this use case — classifies TouchUp_TextEdit as a high-confidence 'altered' red flag; its 40+ indicator list (multiple startxref, dangling objects, white overlay rectangles, invisible text, XMP history gaps, ID mismatches) is a ready-made checklist to mine for TrustyFile modules.
  - _signal:_ High confidence that TEXT was edited in Acrobat after generation — on an invoice this is precisely the 'change 1025€ to 2025€' scenario. FP very low for the marker itself (it only appears after Acrobat text edits); residual risk is benign edits (fixing a typo before sending), so pair with WHERE the edit sits (amount/date regions → critical).
  - _local:_ Trivial pure-Python bytes.find on the raw file; PDFRecon itself is Python+PyMuPDF (source on GitHub) and can be mined for rules. Microseconds.
  - _sources:_ https://github.com/Rasmus-Riis/PDFRecon, https://community.adobe.com/questions-9/touchup-objects-in-todays-versions-of-acrobat-1252432, https://www.bitrecover.com/blog/how-to-check-if-pdf-was-altered/

---
